West Virginia Cannabis & Hemp Research

Last audited: 24 October 2026. This is the combined audited final version covering Parts 0–12: Part 1 is the combined audit (Part 1A, the Run 1 audit; Part 1B, the Run 2 audit carried from the delivered Run 2 audited file; and one combined still-unverified list); Part 2 is the full corrected rewrite (Part 2A, Run 1 Parts 0–6 with every audit correction applied in place; Part 2B, Run 2 Parts 7–12 carried from the delivered Run 2 audited file), closing with the Combined Research Notes. No section from either run has been dropped.

Method. This audit was done by a separate fact-checking agent that did not write either report. Confirmations rest on primary sources only: the WV Code and bill pages on wvlegislature.gov / code.wvlegislature.gov, Code of State Rules via the WV Secretary of State (apps.sos.wv.gov), the Office of Medical Cannabis (omc.wv.gov) and its parent department, the WV Department of Agriculture, the WV State Tax Department, the Treasurer/Auditor, the Secretary of State, USDA AMS, and official county/city/town sites and codes. News, law-firm blogs, trade press, AI summaries, trackers (LegiScan/VoteSmart/BillTrack) and republications (Justia/Cornell) do not confirm a claim; Cornell/Justia were used only to locate a pinpoint, labelled republication. Where only a secondary source exists, the claim is marked Unverified. Verdicts: Confirmed (primary text opened), Corrected (wording/figure must change), Removed (do not carry forward), Unverified (secondary only or not located). Internal contradictions were resolved against the most authoritative official source, and cross-checker conflicts are stated explicitly in Part 1A, not silently resolved. For any permit holder listed as an individual rather than a business, no personal phone/email is reproduced — name + official-source link only (no such case arose; only agency inboxes/phones appear).

Federal baseline applied as fixed context (not re-researched): state-licensed medical marijuana and FDA-approved products moved to Schedule III effective 28 April 2026 (91 FR 22714); all adult-use marijuana remains Schedule I; the broader 2024 rescheduling proposal is NOT final. IRS 280E is generally lifted for businesses no longer in Schedule I/II; adult-use remains fully subject to 280E. Hemp: PL 119-37 §781 takes effect 12 November 2026, but PL 119-103 §2019 limits it until 11 December 2026 to only the provisions excluding cannabinoids not naturally produced or synthesized; the full total-THC definition and the 0.4 mg total-THC per-container cap apply after 11 December 2026. USDA enforcement of the DEA-registered-lab hemp testing requirement is delayed to 31 December 2026. The federal medical-marijuana appropriations rider runs through 11 December 2026. No federal cannabis banking law has been enacted. No contradiction to that baseline was found in either run. Both runs correctly apply Schedule III to WV state-licensed medical permit holders and Schedule I to non-medical activity, and neither reads the federal 280E lift as a WV state lift. The WV state-tax caveat is preserved and strengthened below: WV has no 280E decoupling statute located, and the current fixed-date conformity windows (see the conformity row) do not import the April 2026 federal change automatically.

Contents: Part 1A Run 1 audit · Part 1B Run 2 audit (carried) · Combined still-unverified list · Part 2A Rewritten Parts 0–6 · Part 2B Rewritten Parts 7–12 (carried) · Combined Research Notes

Part 1 — Combined Audit

Scope. Part 1A audits Run 1 (Parts 0–6) from the three checker reports (Parts 0–1 legal; Parts 3–4 local rules/licences; Parts 2/5/6 taxes/licence data/market). Part 1B carries the Run 2 audit as delivered. The combined still-unverified list is the union of both audits, deduplicated.

The carrying bill number is [VERIFY] — do NOT state HB 4023 as pending and do NOT state it as enacted. SB 546 scheduling phrasing (Part 0, 1.2, 1.5.2)CorrectedSB 546 enrolled text (Run 2 audit); code.wvlegislature.gov/60A-2-204/ (Parts 0–1 check)Replace ‘unless expressly exempted’ with the lawful-channel exception: SB 546 made all delta-THCs Schedule I except products lawfully manufactured, distributed, or possessed under the Industrial Hemp Development Act (§19-12E) and the Medical Cannabis Act (Chapter 16A). Passed 10 March 2023, in effect 8 June 2023. Calling it a flat ‘delta-8 ban’ is misleading; §19-12E-12 expressly lists delta-8 and delta-10 as regulated hemp-derived cannabinoids.
§60A-2-204 proviso ‘chapter 16H’ — NEW FLAGUnverified — do not quotecode.wvlegislature.gov/60A-2-204/ (Parts 0–1 check, full text lines 0–467)The displayed proviso cites ‘chapter 16H’, which does not exist (its index URL falls back to the Chapter 1 index, the same fallback as the non-existent §16A-6-14). No second/medical preservation appears in the remainder of the section as displayed. The substantive medical preservation stands on the SB 546 enrolled text; the ‘chapter 16H’ citation must be checked against the enrolled act / a Code errata check before republication — do not quote it as law.
§16A-4-2 practitioner restrictions (1.2, 1.6, G-45)Correctedcode.wvlegislature.gov/16A-4-2/ (Parts 0–1 check)§16A-4-2 is LIVE, not repealed, including (a)(1) no remuneration to certify (other than examination fee), (a)(2) no direct/economic interest in a medical cannabis organisation, (a)(3) ‘A practitioner may not advertise the practitioner’s services as a practitioner who can certify a patient to receive medical cannabis’, (b) removal from registry, (c) discipline as unprofessional conduct. Run 1’s ‘shown as [Repealed] in bill excerpts [VERIFY]’ and ‘former §16A-4-2(a)(3)’ framings are wrong and are replaced everywhere.
Kratom fees, §19-12F-8 (1.2)Correctedcode.wvlegislature.gov/19-12F-8/ (Parts 0–1 check; Run 2 audit agrees)Kratom fees are $1,500 non-refundable application + $300 annual — not ‘$2,500 / $1,000’. Run 1’s warning (kratom fees ≠ hemp fees) is right in substance; the figures are wrong. Kratom fees are kratom-only and must never be substituted for hemp fees.
§16A-6-14 employee registration (Part 4, G-24)Removed as current lawcode.wvlegislature.gov/16A-6/ index (Parts 0–1 check); Parts 3–4 check agreesArticle 6 ends at §16A-6-13; §16A-6-14 does not exist. The $25 / 18+ employee-registration figures trace to SB 231 (2021) introduced text — a dead bill — and must not be presented as current law. Any employee-registry rule must be verified against 64 CSR 109 [VERIFY]. Run 1’s §4 passage already attributed the figures to 2021 bill text; the rewrite now states the non-existence explicitly and keeps the figures bill-text-only.
‘Bureau’ definition (Part 0)Correctedcode.wvlegislature.gov/16A-2-1/ (Parts 0–1 check)Current §16A-2-1(a)(3) defines ‘bureau’ as ‘the Bureau for Public Health within the Department of Health’ — Run 1’s caution that Chapter 16A text ‘still defines bureau by reference to DHHR in excerpts retrieved’ is outdated. Contrast: §16A-9-1(i)(2) as opened still says ‘Secretary of Health and Human Resources’ — a genuine drafting lag elsewhere in the Code.
Residency (1.4.5, 4.6, G-25)Corrected — combined findingcode.wvlegislature.gov/16A-2-1/ and /16A-6-3/ (Parts 0–1 and Parts 3–4 checks); HB 208 (2019 1X) (Parts 3–4 check)Patient residency is statutory via the §16A-2-1(a)(22)(C) definition (‘Is a resident of this state’). Organisation residency is removed from the statute: current §16A-6-3(a) has no residency clause ((a)(3) is ‘ready, willing, and able’); HB 208 shows the clause struck. OMC’s Tennessee Wine non-enforcement statement must be re-pinned to the patient-residency definition rather than to §16A-6-3, and its current operational status remains [VERIFY] against OMC guidance. Secondary summaries still listing ‘majority WV ownership required’ (e.g. CannBus Q3 2026) are outdated.

1.5.1 §19-12E-4(a) agricultural crop + plant/grow/harvest/possess/process/sell/buy on meeting §19-12E-5; (b) licence required to cultivate/handle/processConfirmedhttps://code.wvlegislature.gov/19-12E-4/

(b): "A person shall not cultivate, handle, or process industrial hemp in this state unless the person holds an industrial hemp license issued by this state."
1.5.1 §19-12E-9 defence (complete defence to possession/cultivation prosecution if growing industrial hemp under the article; NOT a defence to criminal sale/distribution not meeting the hemp definition)Confirmedhttps://code.wvlegislature.gov/19-12E-9/
(a): "It is a complete defense to a prosecution for the possession or cultivation of marijuana pursuant to… §60A-4-401 et seq.… that defendant was growing industrial hemp pursuant to the provisions of this article." (b): "This section is not a defense to a charge of criminal sale or distribution of marijuana… which does not meet the definition of industrial hemp."

(b), (c) (fingerprints; applicant pays actual costs; results not public records under §29B-1-1), (d) 31 December validity + renewal-pending continuation, (e) prior written entry consent, (f) discretionary USDA recognition ("may choose"). Part 1B note

The Run 2 audit section follows, carried unchanged except for its heading and the lead-in note above.

Part 1B — Run 2 audit (carried from the delivered Run 2 audited file)

Part 1B is the Run 2 audit exactly as delivered (Parts 7–12 plus Combined Research Notes). It is carried unchanged here for the audit trail; the Run 1 audit is Part 1A above, and the combined still-unverified list follows Part 1B. Note that two Run 2 positions are superseded by the Run 1 audit and are flagged in Part 1A-1: the conformity/HB 4023 position (windows end 1 January 2026; carrying bill number [VERIFY]) and the operating-count position (now resolved to the 70/9/9 Stat Sheet figure).

Scope. This audit covers the attached document only: West Virginia Cannabis & Hemp Research, Run 2, Parts 7–12, plus its Combined Research Notes. Run 1 (Parts 0–6) is a separate document and was not re-audited here; where a Run 2 correction also affects a Run 1 gap disposition, the Combined Notes below record it, but no Run 1 fact is silently rewritten. Primary sources only were used for Confirmed and Corrected verdicts: the West Virginia Code and bill pages on wvlegislature.gov, the Code of State Rules filings at apps.sos.wv.gov, omc.wv.gov, agriculture.wv.gov, tax.wv.gov, the USDA AMS hemp-plan list, and the BIA list at 91 FR 4102 (govinfo). News, law-firm blogs, trade press, trackers (LegiScan, VoteSmart, BillTrack50) and republications (Justia, Cornell LII) are treated as secondary: they can explain why a claim was not adopted, but they do not confirm it. A claim sourced only to a republication is therefore marked Unverified as a primary-source matter, even where the republication text looks consistent.

Federal baseline — no contradiction found. The report states the baseline correctly: state-licensed medical marijuana and FDA-approved products are Schedule III under 91 FR 22714 effective 28 April 2026, all other marijuana including all adult-use remains Schedule I, IRS 280E is generally lifted for Schedule III medical activity while adult-use remains fully subject to it, PL 119-37 §781 takes effect 12 November 2026 but PL 119-103 §2019 limits it until 11 December 2026 to the non-naturally-produced/synthesised-cannabinoid exclusions, after which the full total-THC definition and 0.4 mg per-container cap apply, USDA DEA-lab enforcement is delayed to 31 December 2026, the federal medical-marijuana appropriations rider runs through 11 December 2026, and no federal cannabis banking law has been enacted. The report correctly applies Schedule III to West Virginia's state-licensed medical permit holders, treats any non-medical possession as Schedule I, and — critically — does not present the federal 280E change as a West Virginia state-tax change. West Virginia's state 280E treatment remains unresolved (see the HB 4023 row below).

How to read the verdicts. Confirmed = the primary source says it. Corrected = the primary source says something different, and the rewritten report uses the primary-source version. Removed = the claim is wrong as law and no correct version of it was found in a primary source. Unverified = no primary source was opened that establishes it in this audit; the rewritten report keeps the claim only where the original correctly labelled it secondary or flagged, and otherwise keeps the flag. Where the report already handled a claim correctly (labelled secondary, flagged, or refused to adopt it), the verdict is Confirmed for the handling, even if the underlying fact itself is Unverified — the table says which.

9.1.6ABCA role: §19-12E-12(l)(2) enforcement agents may enforce administrative retailer provisions; “The ABCA Commissioner must provide requisite training, §19-12E-12(l)(3)”Corrected§19-12E-12(c)(2), (j), (l)(2)–(3)Corrected: (l)(2) is confirmed verbatim. 9.1.6 / R2-C-5 / G-9.5Secondary 2025 article: as of July 2025 WV requires ABCA permits for any hemp/kratom manufacturing/distribution/sale, citing “§19-12E-12, WV SB345 text”; SB 345 not verified on wvlegislature.govCorrected — claim removed as law; SB 345 identified§19-12E-12(d)(1); SB 345 (2025) introduced text (WV Legislature, 2025 Regular Session)Removed as law. SB 345 is a 2025 bill (Sen. Woodrum, introduced 13 February 2025, referred to Senate Judiciary) whose purpose is to authorise the ABCA Commissioner to promulgate a legislative rule relating to retail enforcement of select plant-based derivatives, including hemp and kratom (175 CSR 11, filed in the State Register 20 August 2024) — not to transfer hemp permitting to ABCA. Trackers record it as dying sine die 12 April 2025; that sine-die date is secondary, but the bill's purpose and committee referral are from its introduced text. It therefore does not support ABCA permitting, and §19-12E-12(d)(1) (Agriculture Commissioner issues the permits) controls. Whether 175 CSR 11 itself is in force remains [VERIFY].

10.2.3Closed-loop supply chain §16A-6-13(a)(7)–(8); hemp remote sellers §19-12E-12(b), (h), (i)(3); §19-12E-12(l)(1) contraband/seizure/forfeiture/destructionConfirmed§16A-6-13(a)(7)–(8); §19-12E-12(b), (h), (i)(3), (l)(1)All confirmed from Code pages opened in this audit. §16A-6-13(a)(7)–(8) verbatim in substance: a dispensary may only obtain medical cannabis from a permitted grower/processor, and a grower/processor may only provide to a permitted dispensary. §19-12E-12(b) subjects out-of-state remote distributors/retailers shipping into WV to WV permitting, labelling, business registration (§11-12-1 et seq.) and taxation; (h) requires an approved age-verification mechanism for non-in-person sales; (i)(3) collects use tax from sellers, marketplace facilitators and referrers. Part 0 — Headlines

  • Medical only, operating. West Virginia is medical-only. There is no adult-use programme, licence type, or decriminalisation of non-patient possession in current law. Lawful use is limited to certified patients and caregivers with bureau-issued cards. Source: W. Va. Code §16A-3-2(a), code.wvlegislature.gov/16A-3 — Accessed 2026-10-24.
  • Parent agency confirmed after the DHHR reorganisation: the Office of Medical Cannabis (OMC) is now in the West Virginia Department of Health, Bureau for Public Health, not DHHR. The former DHHR was dissolved effective 1 January 2024 by HB 2006 (2023). Sources: Department of Health and OMC advisory letterhead 24 Mar 2025, omc.wv.gov/media/67 — Accessed 2026-10-24. [VERIFY] Chapter 16A text still defines “bureau” by reference to DHHR in excerpts retrieved; do not cite the old parentage as current.
  • Statutory caps: ≤10 growers (each up to 2 locations per permit), ≤10 processors, ≤100 dispensaries; ≤10 dispensary permits, 1 grower permit, 1 processor permit per person; vertical integration expressly permitted. Source: §16A-6-13(a), code.wvlegislature.gov/16A-6-13 — Accessed 2026-10-24.
  • Operating counts — CONFLICT [VERIFY]: OMC 2025 Biennial Report Tables 13–15, counted from the extracted text, show 9 growers, 9 processors and 76 dispensary rows; current operational count is 70 dispensaries / 9 growers / 9 processors (OMC Stat Sheet, data 31 August 2026, updated monthly). Biennial Report Tables 13–15 rows (9/9/76) are table rows for the period ending 31 October 2025, not an operating count. Source for the table counts: OMC Biennial Report 2025 (PDF) — Accessed 2026-10-24; secondary: cannbus.org — Accessed 2026-10-24.
  • Applications closed for growers/processors/dispensaries since 18 Feb 2020, 3:00 p.m. EST; laboratory applications only continue to be accepted. Source: OMC Industry page — Accessed 2026-10-24. No upcoming grower/processor/dispensary window was located [UNAVAILABLE].
  • Hemp-derived cannabinoids are regulated, 21+, permitted and taxed — not an open unregulated market. §19-12E-12 requires a Commissioner of Agriculture permit, department-approved labels, and an 11% privilege tax on retail sales price. Source: code.wvlegislature.gov/19-12E-12 (PDF) — Accessed 2026-10-24.
  • Delta-8 / delta-10 are Schedule I outside lawful channels. Enrolled CS for SB 546 (2023), passed 10 Mar 2023, in effect 8 June 2023, made all delta tetrahydrocannabinols Schedule I under the lawful-channel exception, preserving products lawfully made/distributed/possessed under the Industrial Hemp Development Act and Medical Cannabis Act. Calling this a flat “delta-8 ban” is misleading: §19-12E-12 expressly lists delta-8 and delta-10 as regulated hemp-derived cannabinoids. Source: SB 546 enrolled text — Accessed 2026-10-24.
  • No West Virginia official response to PL 119-37 §781 / PL 119-103 §2019 or to the 12 Nov / 11 Dec 2026 federal hemp dates was located [UNAVAILABLE] — no WVDA, OMC, Tax Division, Attorney General or 2026 bill adopting a total-THC definition or 0.4 mg/container cap was found. See Part 1.5.7.
  • First dispensary sales November 2021 (secondary only, not verified against an OMC press release in this run [VERIFY]). Programme created by SB 386, Medical Cannabis Act, signed 19 April 2017 (secondary summary; enrolled-act date not opened [VERIFY]).
  • 2026 bills that did NOT become law: HB 5260 (edibles) passed the House 4 Mar 2026, to Senate Health and Human Resources 5 Mar 2026, no Senate passage; HB 5188 (possession/smoking by approved persons) pending in House Health and Human Resources at 4 Feb 2026; HB 4371 (adult-use by county referendum) introduced 15 Jan 2026, Pending in House Judiciary at 15 Jan 2026, no passage; SB 892 (Senate edibles companion) introduced 9 Feb 2026, no passage shown. Sources in Part 1.9 — all Accessed 2026-10-24.
  • Key numbers: medical privilege tax 10% of dispensary gross receipts (§16A-9-1(b)); hemp-derived privilege tax 11% (§19-12E-12(i)); patient/caregiver card fee $50, waivable for hardship (§16A-5-1(c)(5)); grower/processor $5,000 application / $50,000 permit / $5,000 renewal; dispensary $2,500 / $10,000 per location / $2,500 renewal (§16A-6-6); dispensary buffer 1,000 ft from school/daycare property line (§16A-8-2(a)(3)); patient driving serum threshold >3 ng active THC per mL blood in serum (§16A-5-10(1)). Fund balance ~$34m by Oct 2025 / ~$38m by Apr 2026 is secondary only, NOT official — no official Treasurer/OMC balance retrieved [UNAVAILABLE].

Part 1 — Laws

1.1 Legal status: medical only

“Notwithstanding any provision of law to the contrary, the use or possession of medical cannabis as set forth in this act is lawful within this state, subject to the following conditions: (1) Medical cannabis may only be dispensed to: (A) a patient who receives a certification from a practitioner and is in possession of a valid identification card issued by the bureau; and (B) a caregiver who is in possession of a valid identification card issued by the bureau.” — §16A-3-2(a), code.wvlegislature.gov/16A-3 — Accessed 2026-10-24.
“Except as provided in section two of this article, section four of article seven, article thirteen or article fourteen of this chapter, the use of medical cannabis is unlawful and shall, in addition to any other penalty provided by law, be deemed a violation of the Uniform Controlled Substances Act under chapter sixty-a of this code.” — §16A-3-3(a), same source.
“The growth, processing, manufacture, acquisition, transportation, sale, dispensing, distribution, possession and consumption of medical cannabis permitted under this act shall not be deemed to be a violation of the provisions of the Uniform Controlled Substance Act under chapter sixty-a of this code. If a provision of Uniform Controlled Substance Act under chapter sixty-a relating to cannabis conflicts with a provision of this act, this act shall take precedence.” — §16A-15-1, code.wvlegislature.gov/16A-15 — Accessed 2026-10-24.

For non-patients, legal: (i) licensed industrial-hemp activity under Chapter 19, Article 12E; (ii) permitted hemp-derived cannabinoid products sold to adults 21+ under §19-12E-12; (iii) CBD / non-THC hemp products within the hemp definition. Not legal: possession, purchase, gifting, sale, cultivation or use of marijuana outside Chapter 16A or Chapter 19, Article 12E. No personal-use amount, social-sharing defence, or adult-use purchase right exists. Home grow is prohibited for everyone except permitted growers — and for patients specifically, see 1.3.2.

Schedule III — how it applies to West Virginia: WV's programme is state-licensed (OMC permits under Chapter 16A), so its growers, processors and dispensaries fall within the baseline's “state-licensed MEDICAL” category. Federally from 28 April 2026 their medical activity is Schedule III and §280E is generally lifted for that activity. That does not create any WV adult-use right, authorise interstate shipment, change WV's 10% privilege tax, or change state product-form, smoking or home-grow bans. Any non-medical possession in WV remains Schedule I federally and a state Chapter 60A offence. State-tax caveat: see Part 2.5 — WV has no §280E decoupling statute located, and fixed-date conformity may not automatically import the April 2026 federal change.

“‘Serious medical condition’ means any of the following, as has been diagnosed as part of a patient's continuing care: (A) Cancer. (B) Positive status for human immunodeficiency virus or acquired immune deficiency syndrome. (C) Amyotrophic lateral sclerosis. (D) Parkinson's disease. (E) Multiple sclerosis. (F) Damage to the nervous tissue of the spinal cord with objective neurological indication of intractable spasticity. (G) Epilepsy. (H) Neuropathies. (I) Huntington's disease. (J) Crohn's disease. (K) Post-traumatic stress disorder. (L) Intractable seizures. (M) Sickle cell anemia. (N) Severe chronic or intractable pain of neuropathic origin or severe chronic or intractable pain. (O) Terminally ill.” — §16A-2-1(30), via code.wvlegislature.gov/16A — Accessed 2026-10-24. A 2020 bill (Eng. CS for SB 752) proposed adding ulcerative colitis, opioid use disorder and an approval route — that is bill text, NOT current Code. Do not add those conditions.
“The following entities shall be authorized to receive a permit to operate as a medical cannabis organization to grow, process or dispense medical cannabis: (1) Growers. (2) Processors. (3) Dispensaries.” — §16A-6-1, code.wvlegislature.gov/16A-6-1 — Accessed 2026-10-24.
“Before a permit may be issued, the bureau shall obtain the following: (1) A written approval from the board of health for the county in which the permit is to be located and operate business. (2) A written statement from the county commission for the county in which the permit is to be located and conduct business that the county has not voted, pursuant to 16A-7-6 of this code, to disapprove a medical cannabis organization to be located or operate within the county.” — §16A-6-13(b), code.wvlegislature.gov/16A-6-13 — Accessed 2026-10-24.
“A grower or processor may only grow, store, harvest or process medical cannabis in an indoor, enclosed, secure facility which: (1) Includes electronic locking systems, electronic surveillance and other features required by the bureau; and (2) Is located within this state.” — §16A-7-2(b), code.wvlegislature.gov/16A-7 — Accessed 2026-10-24.
“A county may pass an ordinance by vote of the residents of the county to prohibit the operation or location of a medical cannabis organization within that particular county. A prohibition under this section shall remain in effect unless and until changed by a subsequent vote.” — §16A-7-6, same source.

1.3 Possession, cultivation, consumption, product forms

1.3.1 Possession penalties for non-patients

Simple possession is a misdemeanour, 90 days to 6 months in jail and/or up to $1,000, for every offence (statute does not escalate simple possession to a felony on repeat). A first offence for less than 15 g of marijuana must be disposed of under §60A-4-407 (conditional discharge), not by conventional sentencing. There is no weight threshold that makes possession lawful; “less than 15 g” affects disposition of a first offence only. Verbatim §60A-4-401(c) is from consistent secondary mirrors; official Code page not opened in this run — [VERIFY] against code.wvlegislature.gov/60A-4-401 before republication. Manufacture, delivery and possession-with-intent are separate felony offences under §60A-4-401(a) — exact ranges [VERIFY], not quoted from secondary summaries. Sources: §60A-4-401, §60A-4-407 — Accessed 2026-10-24.

1.3.2 Cultivation; home grow prohibited — including for patients

“It shall be unlawful to: (1) Smoke medical cannabis. (2) Except as provided under subsection (c), incorporate medical cannabis into edible form or sell in edible form. (3) Grow medical cannabis unless the grower/processor has received a permit from the bureau under this act. (4) Grow or dispense medical cannabis unless authorized as a health care medical cannabis organization under article thirteen of this chapter. (5) Dispense medical cannabis unless the dispensary has received a permit from the bureau under this act.” — §16A-3-3(b), code.wvlegislature.gov/16A-3 — Accessed 2026-10-24.
“Nothing in this act shall be construed to preclude the incorporation of medical cannabis into edible form by a patient or a caregiver in order to aid ingestion of the medical cannabis by the patient.” — §16A-3-3(c), same source.

Home grow is prohibited for everyone in West Virginia, including registered patients and caregivers. Only a bureau-permitted grower/processor (or an Article 13 health-care organisation) may grow. A 2026 proposal to allow patient/caregiver cultivation (HB 5259, official 2026 subject index) did not pass — text/final status [VERIFY]: wvlegislature.gov subject index — Accessed 2026-10-24.

1.3.3 Public consumption; smoking vs vaporising

  • Smoking is unlawful, even for patients — §16A-3-3(b)(1). HB 5188, which would have authorised possession and smoking by approved persons, did not pass.
  • Vaporising/nebulising is the lawful inhalation route. §16A-3-2(2)(D) allows “A form medically appropriate for administration by vaporization or nebulization, excluding dry leaf or plant form until dry leaf or plant forms become acceptable under rules adopted by the bureau”, and §16A-3-2(3) provides medical cannabis may not be dispensed in dry leaf/plant form unless otherwise provided in rules under §16A-11-2. The rule route has been used: 64 CSR 110 §10.1.7 lists “Dry leaf or plant form” (Cornell mirror [VERIFY] against SOS). Net position: dry-leaf/flower for vaporisation is dispensed in practice; dry-leaf for smoking is not lawful use, because smoking itself is banned.
  • Public consumption: no general public-consumption offence or permission for patients was located in §§16A-3-2/3-3, 16A-5-10 or 16A-8. What is stated: unused medicine “shall be kept in the original package in which it was dispensed” (§16A-3-2(a)(6)); a patient/caregiver “shall possess an identification card whenever the patient or caregiver is in possession of medical cannabis” (§16A-3-2(a)(7)). Any “private only” claim is [VERIFY] — check 64 CSR and OMC patient guidance.
  • Driving / operating machinery while under the influence: §16A-5-10(1) — a patient may not operate or be in physical control of listed things (chemicals requiring a permit; high-voltage electricity/public utility; vehicle, aircraft, train, boat or heavy machinery) while under the influence with blood content of more than 3 ng active THC per mL blood in serum. §16A-5-10(2)–(4): no employment duties at heights or in confined spaces (including mining) while under the influence; employer may bar a patient from life-threatening or public-health/safety-risk duties while under the influence without that counting as an adverse employment decision. This is a serum-THC threshold tied to operating listed things while under the influence — not a general “any detectable THC = DUI” rule; interaction with the general DUI statute not examined [VERIFY]. Source: code.wvlegislature.gov/16A-5-10 — Accessed 2026-10-24.

1.3.4 Allowed medical product forms and limits

Statutory forms (§16A-3-2(2)): (A) Pill; (B) Oil; (C) Topical forms, including gels, creams or ointments; (D) A form medically appropriate for administration by vaporization or nebulization (dry-leaf proviso above); (E) Tincture; (F) Liquid; (G) Dermal patch. Edibles sold by a dispensary are NOT allowed — §16A-3-3(b)(2), except patient/caregiver home incorporation to aid ingestion (§16A-3-3(c)). Concentrates as a standalone statutory form are not listed; oils/liquids/vaporisation forms are the route by which concentrates reach patients in practice. Rule-level forms (64 CSR 110 §10.1, mirror [VERIFY]): Pill; Oil; Topical (gel, creams, ointments); vaporisation/nebulisation form; Liquid; Dermal patch; Dry leaf or plant form. The rule list omits “tincture” as a separate item (tinctures fall under oil/liquid in practice) — a wording difference, not a substance conflict. No statutory THC mg-per-serving or per-package cap for medical products was located [UNAVAILABLE]; the proposed HB 5260 edible cap (10 mg/serving in secondary summaries) is a proposed cap in a dead bill, not law. Any medical potency cap, if it exists, would be in 64 CSR or bureau product approvals [VERIFY]. Source for rule: Cornell LII, 64 CSR 110 §10 — Accessed 2026-10-24.

1.3.5 Medical-cannabis offences (Chapter 16A, Article 12)

1.4 Medical programme

1.4.1 Qualifying conditions

The 15 conditions in §16A-2-1(30), verbatim in 1.2: cancer; HIV/AIDS positive status; ALS; Parkinson's; multiple sclerosis; spinal-cord nervous-tissue damage with objective neurological indication of intractable spasticity; epilepsy; neuropathies; Huntington's; Crohn's; PTSD; intractable seizures; sickle cell anemia; severe chronic or intractable pain of neuropathic origin or severe chronic or intractable pain; terminally ill (prognosis ~1 year or less). No additional-condition approval route appears in the current text fetched. Anxiety, depression, insomnia, ulcerative colitis and opioid use disorder are not listed.

1.4.2 Physician certification and registration

“A certification to use medical cannabis may be issued by a practitioner to a patient if all of the following requirements are met: (1) The practitioner has been approved by the bureau for inclusion in the registry and has a valid, unexpired, unrevoked, unsuspended license to practice medicine in this state at the time of the issuance of the certification. (2) The practitioner has determined that the patient has a serious medical condition and has included the condition in the patient's health care record. (3) The patient is under the practitioner's continuing care for the serious medical condition. (4) In the practitioner's professional opinion and review of past treatments, the practitioner determines the patient is likely to receive therapeutic or palliative benefit from the use of medical cannabis. (5) The practitioner has determined that the patient has no past or current medical condition(s) or medication use that would constitute a contraindication for the use of cannabis. (6) The practitioner has determined that the patient is experiencing serious pathophysiological discomfort, disability, or dysfunction that may be attributable to a serious medical condition and may possibly benefit from cannabis treatment when current medical research exhibits a moderate or higher probability of efficacy; and (7) The practitioner has educated the patient about cannabis and its safe use.” — §16A-4-3(a), code.wvlegislature.gov/16A-4 — Accessed 2026-10-24.

Certification contents (§16A-4-3(b)): patient name/DOB/address; specific serious medical condition; statement of condition + continuing care; date of issuance; practitioner name/address/telephone/signature. “Practitioner” means a doctor of allopathic or osteopathic medicine fully licensed in WV (definition paragraph not opened verbatim [VERIFY]). The bureau's 4-hour physician training course is required by §16A-3-1(a)(6). Telemedicine: secondary sources state telehealth evaluations are allowed; no telemedicine provision was located in Chapter 16A text opened [VERIFY] — do not state as statute.

1.4.3 Patient and caregiver cards, fees

“(a) Issuance. — The bureau may issue an identification card to a patient who has a certification approved by the bureau and to a caregiver designated by the patient. An identification card issued to a patient shall authorize the patient to obtain and use medical cannabis as authorized by this act. An identification card issued to a caregiver shall authorize the caregiver to obtain medical cannabis on behalf of the patient.” … “(c) Application. — … Each application must include: (1) The name, address and date of birth of the patient. (2) The name, address and date of birth of a caregiver. (3) The certification issued by the practitioner. (4) The name, address and telephone number of the practitioner and documentation … that all of the requirements of subsection (a), section three, article four … have been met. (5) A $50 processing fee. The bureau may waive or reduce the fee if the applicant demonstrates financial hardship. … (e) Expiration. — An identification card of a patient or caregiver shall expire within one year from the date of issuance, upon the death of the patient, or as otherwise provided in this section.” — §16A-5-1, code.wvlegislature.gov/16A-5 — Accessed 2026-10-24.

OMC operational detail (OMC guidance, omc.wv.gov/media/24 — Accessed 2026-10-24): $50 for both initial and renewal; payment by ACH online or by check/money order mailed with the application number in the memo line; hardship proof by W-2s/pay stubs within 30 days or benefit statements (SNAP, Medicaid, SSI/SSDI, Section 8). OMC review “within 30 days” is stated in secondary patient guides, not in §16A-5-1 text fetched [VERIFY] against the OMC patient page. Caregiver limits (statute): “An individual may not act as a caregiver for more than five patients” (§16A-3-2(a)(4)); “A patient may designate up to two caregivers at any one time” (§16A-3-2(a)(5)). Caregiver age (21+) and residency are stated in secondary guides; not located in §16A-5-1 text fetched [VERIFY]. Minor patients: secondary guides state a parent/guardian must serve as caregiver; the minor-patient provision in Chapter 16A was not opened [VERIFY].

1.4.4 Purchase and supply limits

“(c) Limitations. — No dispensary may dispense to a patient or caregiver: (1) A quantity of medical cannabis greater than that which the patient or caregiver is permitted to possess under the certification; or (2) A form of medical cannabis prohibited by this act. (d) Supply. — When dispensing medical cannabis to a patient or caregiver, the dispensary may not dispense an amount greater than a 30-day supply until the patient has exhausted all but a seven-day supply provided pursuant to §16A-4-5 of this code.” — §16A-8-1(c)–(d), code.wvlegislature.gov/16A-8 — Accessed 2026-10-24.

Verification before dispensing (§16A-8-1(e)): dispensary must consult the bureau electronic tracking system (§16A-3-1) and the dispensary tracking system (§16A-7-1). Dispensed form must conform to any practitioner limitation (§16A-8-1(f)). Numeric 30-day amount — secondary only, CONFLICT [VERIFY]: most secondary guides state 6 ounces of flower or 60 grams of concentrate, or an equivalent combination, on a rolling 30-day basis, with 1 ounce of flower = 10 grams of processed THC, and that a practitioner may set a lower limit (sources secondary: Marijuana Herald; Compassionate Certification Centers — Accessed 2026-10-24). One secondary guide instead states “four ounces of dried usable” material (IndicaOnline — Accessed 2026-10-24). Neither figure is in the statute text opened, and the conflict is not resolved here. Confirm against OMC/Complia allotment guidance before republication.

1.4.5 Out-of-state patients / reciprocity

“The bureau may enter into reciprocity agreements with any states that have comparable requirements for the use and lawful purchase of medical cannabis in a manner consistent with the provisions of this article to allow terminally ill cancer patients to purchase medical cannabis in another state.” — §16A-3-5, code.wvlegislature.gov/16A-3 — Accessed 2026-10-24.

That is the only reciprocity provision in current Article 3 text fetched. It is discretionary (“may”), limited on its face to terminally ill cancer patients, and framed as purchase in another state, not purchase in WV by out-of-state patients. No list of executed reciprocity agreements, and no general out-of-state-patient purchase right, was located [UNAVAILABLE]. Secondary claims that a WV card “can be used in Pennsylvania and Washington D.C.” (clinic marketing) are not adopted — PA/DC reciprocity, if any, would be governed by those jurisdictions' law and by an executed agreement, neither verified here. An older bill (SB 752) proposed broader reciprocity; that is bill text, not law. Residency for WV cards: Patient residency is statutory via the §16A-2-1(a)(22)(C) definition (‘Is a resident of this state’); §16A-5-1(c) as fetched lists name/address/DOB/certification/fee but does not use the word ‘residency’ in the excerpt. OMC’s Tennessee Wine non-enforcement statement must be re-pinned to that definition, and its current operational status remains [VERIFY] against OMC guidance [VERIFY].

1.4.6 Confidentiality

§16A-3-4(a): patient/caregiver information, certifications, card information, State Police information and condition information are confidential and not subject to public disclosure under Chapter 29B. §16A-3-4(b): permit applications, practitioner names/business addresses/credentials, and disciplinary actions are public. Source: code.wvlegislature.gov/16A-3 — Accessed 2026-10-24.

1.5 Hemp

1.5.1 THC limits and how THC is measured — current WV text

“(g) ‘Hemp’ or ‘industrial hemp’ means all parts and varieties of the plant Cannabis sativa L. and any part of the plant, including the seeds of the plant and all derivatives, extracts, cannabinoids, isomers, acids, salts, and salts of isomers, whether growing or not with no greater than 0.3% tetrahydrocannabinol, or the THC concentration for hemp defined in 7 U.S.C. § 5940, whichever is greater; … (j) ‘Marijuana’ means all plant material from the genus cannabis containing more than one percent tetrahydrocannabinol or seeds of the genus capable of germination” — §19-12E-3(g),(j), code.wvlegislature.gov/19-12E-3 — Accessed 2026-10-24.

Three tensions, not resolved by guessing: (1) §19-12E-3(g) says 0.3% THC “or the THC concentration … in 7 U.S.C. §5940, whichever is greater” — §5940 is the expired 2014 Farm Bill pilot provision; the operative federal definition is now 7 U.S.C. §1639o. Whether “whichever is greater” has any present effect is [VERIFY]. (2) §19-12E-3(j) defines “marijuana” for Article 12E as >1% THC, while Chapter 60A and §19-12E-12 use 0.3%. Material between 0.3% and 1% sits in an ambiguous zone — do not advise on that band without a legal reading [VERIFY]. (3) Measurement: §19-12E-12(c)(7)(A) measures delta-9 as “a total concentration of not more than 0.3 percent on a dry weight basis”. Current WV text does not state a total-THC formula (Δ9 + THCA×0.877) and does not name THCA as a separately counted cannabinoid. That is the pre-federal-change position; see 1.5.7. Also: §19-12E-3(a) “‘Cannabidiol’ or ‘CBD’ means the compound by the same name derived from the hemp variety of the cannabis sativa L. plant”; §19-12E-3(h) “‘Hemp products’ means all products derived from, or made by, processing hemp plants or plant parts, that are prepared in a form available for commercial sale.”

Industrial hemp basics (for Part 7 cross-reference): §19-12E-2 purpose — “The Legislature finds that the development and use of industrial hemp can serve to improve the state's economy and agricultural vitality and that the production of industrial hemp can be regulated so as not to interfere with the strict regulation of controlled substances in this state.” §19-12E-4(a): “Industrial hemp is considered an agricultural crop in this state if grown for the purposes authorized by the provisions of this article. Upon meeting the requirements of §19-12E-5 of this code, an individual in this state may plant, grow, harvest, possess, process, sell, or buy industrial hemp.” §19-12E-4(b): “A person shall not cultivate, handle, or process industrial hemp in this state unless the person holds an industrial hemp license issued by this state.” §19-12E-9 defence: “(a) It is a complete defense to a prosecution for the possession or cultivation of marijuana pursuant to the provisions of §60A-4-401 et seq. of this code that defendant was growing industrial hemp pursuant to the provisions of this article. (b) This section is not a defense to a charge of criminal sale or distribution of marijuana as defined in §60A-1-101 et seq. of this code which does not meet the definition of industrial hemp.” Sources: code.wvlegislature.gov/19-12E, 19-12E-4, 19-12E-9 — Accessed 2026-10-24. §19-12E-5 licensing: application must include name/address and legal description + GPS coordinates of the land area; first-time applicants (and others as required) submit to state/national criminal-history checks via State Police/FBI; applicant pays actual costs; licence valid until 31 December of the year of application, with renewal-pending continuation if the completed renewal was submitted by the department deadline. §19-12E-5 also provides the commissioner may choose to recognise USDA-issued grower licences (PDF text, §19-12E-5(f) as paginated) — interaction with the state plan in §19-12E-10 is a Run 2 / Part 7 question [VERIFY].

1.5.2 Hemp-derived cannabinoid products: delta-8, delta-9, THCA, others

“‘Hemp-derived cannabinoid’ means a naturally occurring non-synthetic substance as follows: (A) Delta-9 tetrahydrocannabinol with a total concentration of not more than 0.3 percent on a dry weight basis; or (B) Delta-8 tetrahydrocannabinol; (C) Delta-10 tetrahydrocannabinol; (D) Hexahydrocannabinol (HHC-); (E) Tetrahydrocannabiphorol (THCp); and (F) Tetrahydrocannabivarin (THCv).” — §19-12E-12(c)(7), code.wvlegislature.gov/19-12E-12 (PDF) — Accessed 2026-10-24.
“the purpose of the act is to allow limited, regulated access to select plant-based derivatives which are naturally occurring and as authorized by the provisions of this article for adults 21 years of age and older: Provided, That the provisions of this section shall not apply to naturally occurring select plant-based derivative products not containing tetrahydrocannabinol content.” — §19-12E-12(b), same source.

Reconciliation with SB 546: SB 546 made all delta-THCs Schedule I under the lawful-channel exception by law, and expressly preserved products lawfully made/distributed/possessed under the Industrial Hemp Development Act. §19-12E-12 is that lawful channel: delta-8, delta-10, HHC, THCP and THCV are listed as regulated hemp-derived cannabinoids, but only when (i) naturally occurring and non-synthetic, (ii) manufactured/processed/distributed/sold under a §19-12E-12 permit, (iii) with department-approved label and final-product approval, and (iv) sold to adults 21+. Synthetic / converted cannabinoids are outside the definition (“naturally occurring non-synthetic”) — §19-12E-12(c)(8) defines “non-naturally occurring derivative” to include a product found on department laboratory testing to violate the article/rules or applicable federal regulations. Whether a given converted delta-8 product counts as “synthetic” vs “naturally occurring” is a fact-and-testing question the statute leaves to the department [VERIFY] against WVDA product approvals/testing guidance. THCA: THCA is not named in §19-12E-12(c)(7). No WV statute or WVDA guidance naming THCA, THCB, or intoxicating THCA flower was located [UNAVAILABLE]. Secondary summaries assert intoxicating THCA products are “treated as illegal to sell” — that assertion is not adopted as statute; the defensible statement is narrower: THCA is not a listed hemp-derived cannabinoid, and any product whose delta-9 total concentration exceeds 0.3% dry-weight is outside §19-12E-12(c)(7)(A). How WVDA treats THCA-rich flower under the current delta-9 test, and after the federal total-THC change, is a Part 7/11 follow-up. CBD / non-THC products: expressly carved out of §19-12E-12 by its proviso. Separately, Article 12E PDF text states in substance a person need not obtain a licence to possess/handle/transport/sell hemp products or extracts including hemp-derived cannabinoids including CBD; hemp-derived cannabinoids including CBD are not controlled substances or adulterants; ingestible hemp-derived cannabinoid products are to be considered foods, not controlled substances or adulterated products — PDF pagination is garbled in places; check against clean §19-12E-5/6 text before republication [VERIFY].

1.5.3 Minimum age

21 years, for hemp-derived cannabinoid products. §19-12E-12(b) (adults 21+); §19-12E-12(g)–(h) require a neutral age-screening mechanism on websites and an approved age-verification mechanism for non-in-person sales; §19-12E-12(q)–(r) criminalise sale to a person under 21 (felony, fine ≤$5,000 and/or 1–5 years) and possession by a person under 21 (first offence misdemeanour ≤$1,000 and/or ≤1 year; second/subsequent felony ≤$5,000 and 1–3 years). An 18-year-old enforcement purchaser acting at law-enforcement direction is excepted (§19-12E-12(t)). Source: §19-12E-12 PDF — Accessed 2026-10-24. No minimum age for non-THC CBD products is stated in §19-12E-12 (because of the non-THC proviso) [VERIFY] against WVDA retail guidance.

1.5.4 Product registration / approval and labelling

“(d)(1) The commissioner may issue manufacturer, processor, distributor, and retailer permits. Any person manufacturing, processing, distributing, offering for sale, or selling any hemp-derived cannabinoid products in this state shall have a permit issued by the commissioner and be otherwise authorized to do business in this State.” … “(d)(5) The commissioner shall keep a list of all persons and entities that have been issued permits pursuant to this section. Such list shall be public information and shall be published initially on or before June 30, 2024, by the commissioner on its website from time to time so as to reflect a current listing.” … “(d)(7) The commissioner shall keep a list of all hemp-derived cannabinoid products that have been approved for sale or distribution in this state. Such list shall be public information and shall be published initially on or before June 30, 2024, by the commissioner on its website from time to time so as to reflect a current listing.” … “(f)(2)(C) Hemp-derived cannabinoid products may not be sold to the final consumer in this State without an approved label.” — §19-12E-12(d),(f), same source.

Labelling content the commissioner must develop (§19-12E-12(e)(6)) includes: “KEEP OUT OF REACH OF CHILDREN. CONSULT YOUR PHYSICIAN BEFORE USE IF YOU ARE PREGNANT OR TAKING ANY MEDICATION” and “USE OF THIS PRODUCT MAY IMPACT DRUG TESTING RESULTS”. Plus (e)(7)–(9): display/staging guidelines to increase safety of underage patrons; guidelines to restrict advertising/marketing of unapproved or unlawful products; prohibitions on child-targeted packaging and shapes/forms. The WVDA Hemp/Kratom guide summarises the retailer position as: listed products (delta-10, delta-9 <0.3% total THC, delta-8, THCV, HHC, THCP, all kratom) are subject to the additional 11% tax, and retailers must follow Tax Division registration / good-standing-letter instructions. Source: WVDA Hemp-Kratom Guide (PDF) — Accessed 2026-10-24. Note the guide's “Delta-9-Tetrahydrocannabivarin (Delta-10-THCV)” line appears to conflate THCV nomenclature — do not copy it verbatim as a product list. Penalties: unapproved-product sale §19-12E-12(o) — first offence misdemeanour $1,000–$5,000 and/or ≤1 year; second/subsequent felony ≤$5,000 and/or 1–5 years. Unpermitted sale §19-12E-12(m) — first offence misdemeanour ≤$1,000 and/or ≤1 year; second/subsequent felony ≤$5,000 and/or 1–5 years. Contaminated-product offences §19-12E-12(n),(p) — felonies up to $10,000 / 2–10 years (knowing sale of product contaminated with a toxic/illegal substance) and $10,000–$25,000 / 1–5 years (knowing distribution/sale of a contaminated product). Contraband/seizure §19-12E-12(l)(1) — any hemp-derived product found in violation is declared contraband, property interest vested in the State, subject to seizure, forfeiture and destruction. Product-registration mechanics (fees, forms, renewal) were not located in §19-12E-12 text [VERIFY] — Part 7. WVDA guide and application forms are the Part 5/7 source to open next. Note: §19-12E-12 permit fees are rule-set (“fees as commensurate with need”, §19-12E-12(e)(4)) — specific dollar fees not located in the section text.

1.5.5 Hemp product tax

See Part 2.3: 11%, quarterly, in addition to other applicable taxes.

1.5.6 THC drinks

No WV statute, rule or WVDA guidance specific to THC-infused beverages was located [UNAVAILABLE]. The defensible position from current law is only by application: a beverage containing a listed hemp-derived cannabinoid (delta-8, delta-9 ≤0.3% dry-weight, delta-10, HHC, THCP, THCV) would be a hemp-derived cannabinoid product subject to §19-12E-12 permitting, approved labelling, 21+ sale, and the 11% tax; a beverage exceeding the delta-9 limit or containing a non-listed intoxicating cannabinoid would be outside §19-12E-12(c)(7) and, outside the medical programme, within Chapter 60A scheduling. No per-container milligram cap, no beverage-specific serving limit, and no beverage ban or beverage licence type was found in WV law in this run. Do not import another state's drink rules. After 11 December 2026, the federal 0.4 mg total-THC per-container cap (baseline) would, on its face, capture most intoxicating hemp drinks — but whether and how WV enforces that cap against §19-12E-12 permittees is [UNAVAILABLE] — see 1.5.7.

1.5.7 Enforcement and WV's response to the new federal hemp definition

Enforcement (current law): §19-12E-12(j): retail-sales provisions enforced by the Commissioner of Agriculture “with the assistance of” the Alcohol Beverage Control Administration Commissioner; the commissioners (plus Tax Commissioner) were to agree memoranda on enforcement cooperation, training, monthly conferral, and a joint report to the Joint Committee on Government and Finance on or before 1 January 2025. Whether that report was filed was not located [VERIFY]. Criminal enforcement by any certified law-enforcement officer; ABC enforcement agents may enforce administrative retailer provisions (§19-12E-12(l)(2)). Administrative sanctions (§19-12E-12(s)): permit revocation/suspension, fines ≤$1,000 per violation, non-issuance — extendable to alter egos/agents. State hemp plan: USDA approved WV's industrial hemp plan in 2020. WVDA announcement: “producers will continue to be regulated at the state level in lieu of federal oversight. States had to submit and gain approval for a plan before October 31, 2020… After October of this year, West Virginian's approved plan will go into effect.” Source: WVDA, USDA approves WV hemp plan (2020) — Accessed 2026-10-24. WVDA later delayed implementation of the approved plan to 30 September 2021 while operating under the 2014 Farm Bill for the 2021 season (WVDA, via mirror: WVDA mirror — Accessed 2026-10-24). Current plan status on the USDA list (updated 17 Feb 2026 in other states' research) was not re-checked for WV in this run [VERIFY] — Part 7: check USDA AMS hemp-plan list. Response to PL 119-37 §781 / PL 119-103 §2019: NONE FOUND [UNAVAILABLE]. No WVDA statement, OMC statement, Tax Division notice, Attorney General opinion, or 2026 bill amending §19-12E-3 or §19-12E-12 to adopt a total-THC definition or a 0.4 mg/container cap was located in searches of wvlegislature.gov, agriculture.wv.gov and omc.wv.gov. Consequences to state plainly: as of Accessed 2026-10-24, WV law still defines hemp-derived delta-9 by 0.3% dry-weight, not by total THC, and lists delta-8/delta-10/HHC/THCP/THCV without a per-container cap — in tension with the federal baseline after 11 December 2026. The federal change does not, by itself, amend §19-12E-12. Whether WVDA treats a product lawful under §19-12E-12 but unlawful under the new federal definition as contraband under §19-12E-12(l) (which incorporates violation of “applicable federal regulations” in the “non-naturally occurring derivative” definition) is a legal question [VERIFY] — seek WVDA guidance / AG opinion. USDA DEA-lab enforcement delay to 31 Dec 2026 (baseline) interacts with WV's state-plan testing, but no WVDA testing-rule change for that delay was located [UNAVAILABLE].

1.6 Packaging, labelling, advertising, delivery, zoning, buffers

Medical cannabis

  • Packaging/labelling — §16A-8-1(g)–(h): safety insert must cover lawful administration methods in individual doses; potential dangers; recognising problematic usage and obtaining services/treatment; preventing/deterring misuse by minors or others; other bureau information. Medicine must be dispensed “in a sealed, properly labeled, and child-resistant package” with receipt information; packaging date; use-by date if applicable; the verbatim warning: “This product is for medicinal use only. Women should not consume during pregnancy or while breastfeeding except on the advice of the practitioner who issued the certification and, in the case of breastfeeding, the infant's pediatrician. This product might impair the ability to drive or operate heavy machinery. Keep out of reach of children.” plus amount of individual doses, species and percentage of THC and CBD; warning to keep in the original container; warning that unauthorised use is unlawful and subject to criminal penalties; other bureau information. Source: code.wvlegislature.gov/16A-8 — Accessed 2026-10-24.
  • Product identification — §16A-3-2(a)(8): “Products packaged by a grower/processor or sold by a dispensary shall only be identified by the name of the grower/processor, the name of the dispensary, the form and species of medical cannabis, the percentage of tetrahydrocannabinol and cannabinol contained in the product.” Note the statute says “cannabinol” (CBN) where modern labels state CBD — a drafting lag; §16A-8-1(h)(5) says THC and CBD. Both quoted as written.
  • Advertising — §16A-3-1(a)(14): the bureau shall “Restrict the advertising and marketing of medical cannabis, which shall be consistent with the Federal rules and regulations governing prescription drug advertising and marketing.” Practitioner advertising as a certifying practitioner is restricted in current §16A-4-2(a)(3) (now confirmed live in current Code) [VERIFY] current practitioner-advertising rule. OMC marketing-approval policy (advance OMC review) is described in secondary compliance summaries but was not opened from omc.wv.gov [VERIFY].
  • Delivery — to patients: NOT authorised in the text opened. §16A-7-1(b)(4) and §16A-7-3 concern delivery/transportation among grower/processors, laboratories and dispensaries (shipping containers, secured carriers, numbered trailer seals, driver licences/registrations, GPS, recordkeeping). No §16A-8 provision authorises dispensary-to-patient home delivery, and §16A-8-2(a)(1) requires dispensing “in an indoor, enclosed, secure facility”. Patient delivery must therefore be treated as not provided for in current law [VERIFY] against 64 CSR — do not state that delivery is available.
  • Zoning/buffers — medical: §16A-15-7: “(1) A grower/processor shall meet the same municipal zoning and land use requirements as other manufacturing, processing and production facilities that are located in the same zoning district. (2) A dispensary shall meet the same municipal zoning and land use requirements as other commercial facilities that are located in the same zoning district. (3) A municipality may enact an ordinance prohibiting or limiting the number and type of medical cannabis organizations permitted to operate in the municipality, including the time, place, and manner of operation.” Plus state buffer: dispensary ≥1,000 feet from a public/private/parochial school or daycare property line (§16A-8-2(a)(3)), waivable by the bureau on clear-and-convincing evidence of need for patient access (§16A-8-2(b)). Municipal restrictive ordinances must be promptly notified to the bureau (§16A-15-8(a)); county prohibitions notified under §16A-15-8(b). Local rules in detail are Part 3. Source: code.wvlegislature.gov/16A-15 — Accessed 2026-10-24.

Hemp-derived cannabinoid products

Labelling: department-approved label required; no sale to the final consumer without an approved label (§19-12E-12(f)(2)(C)); warning statements in 1.5.4; child-targeted packaging/shapes/forms prohibited by rule (§19-12E-12(e)(9)). Advertising: commissioner to develop guidelines/standards to restrict advertising/marketing of unapproved or unlawful products (§19-12E-12(e)(8)); display/staging guidelines for underage-patron safety (§19-12E-12(e)(7)). Delivery/remote sale: out-of-state remote distributors/retailers shipping into WV are subject to WV permitting, labelling, business-registration (§11-12-1 et seq.) and taxation (§19-12E-12(b)); non-in-person sales require an approved age-verification mechanism (§19-12E-12(h)); websites require a neutral age-screen (§19-12E-12(g)). No hemp-product zoning buffer is stated in §19-12E-12 [VERIFY] against local ordinances in Part 3.

1.7 Seed-to-sale tracking system

“A medical cannabis organization must implement an electronic inventory tracking system which shall be directly accessible to the bureau through its electronic database that electronically tracks all medical cannabis on a daily basis. The system shall include tracking of all of the following: (1) For a grower or processor, a seed-to-sale tracking system that tracks the medical cannabis from seed to plant until the medical cannabis is sold to a dispensary. (2) For a dispensary, medical cannabis from purchase from the grower/processor to sale to a patient or caregiver and that includes information that verifies the validity of an identification card presented by the patient or caregiver. (3) For a medical cannabis organization, a daily log of each day's beginning inventory, acquisitions, amounts purchased and sold, disbursements, disposals and ending inventory. The tracking system shall include prices paid and amounts collected from patients and caregivers. (4) For a medical cannabis organization, a system for recall of defective medical cannabis. (5) For a medical cannabis organization, a system to track the plant waste resulting from the growth of medical cannabis or other disposal, including the name and address of any disposal service.” — §16A-7-1(a), code.wvlegislature.gov/16A-7 — Accessed 2026-10-24.

Additional tracking (§16A-7-1(b)): security/surveillance; recordkeeping/retention; acquisition/possession/growing/processing; delivery/transportation (amounts and method); dispensing (amounts, pricing, amounts collected). Tracking information is confidential under Chapter 29B, with law-enforcement access on bureau conditions/procedures (§16A-7-1(c)). Quarterly reporting to the bureau and public posting (§16A-7-1(d)): amounts sold by growers/processors, prices, amounts purchased by each dispensary, costs, and total amount/dollar value sold by each dispensary per 3-month period — whether OMC currently posts these reports was not verified [VERIFY] — Part 5/6. Vendor: “West Virginia's permit holders must utilize the Metrc track-and-trace system to track all medical cannabis and medical cannabis products being grown, processed, transported, tested, and sold in the state.” Source (Metrc partner page, vendor source, updated 24 July 2026): metrc.com/partner/west-virginia — Accessed 2026-10-24. The statute itself does not name Metrc; do not cite Metrc as a statutory requirement. OMC's patient portal is Complia/MyComplia (renewal submissions “through Complia”, OMC advisory 24 Mar 2025).

1.8 Employment and drug-testing protections for patients

“(a) Licensure. — None of the following shall be subject to arrest, prosecution or penalty in any manner, or denied any right or privilege, including civil penalty or disciplinary action by a state licensing board or commission, solely for lawful use of medical cannabis or manufacture or sale or dispensing of medical cannabis, or for any other action taken in accordance with this act: (1) A patient. (2) A caregiver. (3) A practitioner. (4) A medical cannabis organization. … (b) Employment. — (1) No employer may discharge, threaten, refuse to hire or otherwise discriminate or retaliate against an employee regarding an employee's compensation, terms, conditions, location or privileges solely on the basis of such employee's status as an individual who is certified to use medical cannabis. (2) Nothing in this act shall require an employer to make any accommodation of the use of medical cannabis on the property or premises of any place of employment. This act shall in no way limit an employer's ability to discipline an employee for being under the influence of medical cannabis in the workplace or for working while under the influence of medical cannabis when the employee's conduct falls below the standard of care normally accepted for that position. (3) Nothing in this act shall require an employer to commit any act that would put the employer or any person acting on its behalf in violation of federal law.” — §16A-15-4(a)–(b), code.wvlegislature.gov/16A-15 — Accessed 2026-10-24.

What is NOT protected (on the text opened): no provision states that a positive drug test alone, without status discrimination or on-duty influence/impairment, is protected or prohibited as a basis for action. Drug-testing rules for patients as employees were not located in Chapter 16A [UNAVAILABLE] — check WV's drug-testing statute and 64 CSR in Run 2; do not infer a test protection from §16A-15-4(b)(1). No accommodation duty (expressly disclaimed in (b)(2)). No protection where compliance would violate federal law (b)(3) — which matters for federally regulated / safety-sensitive employers and, before Schedule III, for any employer asserting a federal conflict. The Schedule III baseline narrows but does not erase that carve-out; its post-April 2026 scope for WV employers is [VERIFY] — needs employment-law reading, not assumed. Insurers/health plans are not required to cover medical cannabis (§16A-15-3, same source). Plus §16A-5-10(3)–(4): employer may bar a patient from life-threatening or public-health/safety-risk duties while under the influence without that bar counting as an adverse employment decision.

The 2026 regular session convened 14 January and adjourned 14 March 2026 per secondary session summaries; bills with no passage by adjournment are described as “did not pass in 2026”, not as permanently dead (they may be reintroduced in 2027).
