Part 2 — Taxes

2.1 Medical cannabis privilege (gross-receipts) tax — who pays, rate, mechanics

Who pays: dispensaries only. Growers and processors do not pay this tax on sales to dispensaries (pre-SB 1037 position shifted the burden to dispensaries — secondary summary, [VERIFY] enrolled SB 1037).

“(a) Tax imposed. — For the privilege of engaging or continuing within this state in the business of a dispensary of medical cannabis, as defined in §16A-2-1 of this code, there is hereby levied upon and collected from every person exercising the privilege a privilege tax. (b) Rate and measure of tax. — The rate of tax imposed by this section shall be 10 percent of the gross receipts the dispensary receives or accrues during the reporting period, depending upon its method of accounting for federal income tax purposes, from the sale of medical cannabis to a patient or to a caregiver. The tax imposed by this section shall not be added by the dispensary as a separate charge or line item on any sales slip, invoice, receipt, other statement, or memorandum of the price paid by a patient, or caregiver.” — §16A-9-1(a)–(b), code.wvlegislature.gov/16A-9-1 — Accessed 2026-10-24.
“(d) Payment of tax and reports. — Every person subject to the tax imposed by this section shall make quarterly payments … The tax shall be due and payable on the 20th day of January, April, July, and October for the preceding calendar quarter. When the payment of tax is due, the person shall file a tax return in a form prescribed by the Tax Commissioner. … (e) Electronic filing and payment. — The taxes imposed by this section shall be paid to the Tax Commissioner by electronic funds transfer, unless electronic payment is prohibited by state or federal law. Tax returns … shall be filed electronically with the Tax Commissioner.” — §16A-9-1(d)–(e), same source.
“(g) Deposits of proceeds. — All money received from the tax imposed under this section, including any interest and additions to tax paid under §11-10-1 et seq., less the amount of any refunds, shall be deposited into the Medical Cannabis Program Fund.” — §16A-9-1(g), same source.

“Gross receipts” — no deductions. §16A-9-1(c)(1): “‘Gross receipts’ means and includes the gross receipts, however denominated, derived from the sale, distribution, or transfer of medical cannabis, without any deduction on account of the cost of property sold; the cost of materials used to grow, process, or sell the medical cannabis; labor costs, taxes, royalties paid in cash or in kind, or otherwise; interest or discount paid; or any other expense, however denominated.” Cessation: if a dispensary does not renew, gives up or loses its permit, or otherwise ceases business, all tax/penalties/interest become due immediately, with a final return within 30 days; the unpaid amount is a lien on the dispensary's and its owners' property (§16A-9-1(f)). Administration: Tax Crimes and Penalties Act (§11-9-1 et seq.) and Tax Procedure and Administration Act (§11-10-1 et seq.) apply as if set out in Article 9 (§§16A-9-3, 9-4). Tax Commissioner may promulgate procedural/interpretive/legislative (including emergency) rules (§16A-9-1(j)). Information-sharing agreements among Tax Commissioner, bureau and Secretary (now Department of Health) for good-standing checks are authorised (§16A-9-1(i)) — note the statute still says “Secretary of Health and Human Resources” in (i)(2); read as Department of Health after reorganisation [VERIFY] conforming amendment.

2.2 Sales-tax treatment of medical cannabis; local taxes

“Sales of medical cannabis shall not be subject to the taxes imposed by §11-15-1 et seq. and §11-15A-1 et seq. of this code if gross receipts from the sale thereof are included in the measure of tax under this section and the tax has been paid as provided in this section. Additionally, sales of medical cannabis shall not be subject to a special district excise tax imposed by a county or municipality pursuant to this code, or to a county or municipal sales tax.” — §16A-9-1(h), same source.

Plain English: medical cannabis sales that have borne the 10% privilege tax are exempt from the 6% state Consumers Sales and Service Tax (§11-15), the Use Tax (§11-15A), any special-district excise tax, and any county/municipal sales tax. There is therefore no additional local cannabis tax on medical sales in current law. The general state sales-tax rate (6%, §11-15-3; only qualifying (pension-relief) municipalities or non-B&O municipalities may impose a municipal sales tax of up to 1% under §8-13C, for a 6%/7% combined rate in adopting municipalities only) is stated only to show what medical sales are exempt from — secondary rate summaries [VERIFY] rate sections against code.wvlegislature.gov/11-15-3. Ancillary dispensary sales (e.g. devices/instruments authorised under §16A-8-2(a)(4)) are not “sales of medical cannabis” on the face of §16A-9-1(h); their sales-tax treatment was not located [VERIFY] with Tax Division guidance.

2.3 Hemp-derived product tax — rate, effective date, distribution

“For the privilege of engaging or continuing within this state in the business of the retail sale of hemp-derived cannabinoid products, as defined in this section, there is hereby levied upon and collected from every person exercising the privilege a privilege tax equal to 11 percent of the retail sales price on each retail sale of hemp-derived cannabinoids. Such tax is imposed in addition to all other applicable taxes.” — §19-12E-12(i), code.wvlegislature.gov/19-12E-12 (PDF) — Accessed 2026-10-24.

Mechanics (same source): not to be added as a separate charge/line item on any sales slip/invoice/receipt; due quarterly on 20 January, April, July and October for the preceding quarter, on a Tax Commissioner-prescribed return, paid by electronic funds transfer, returns filed electronically. On cessation (non-renewal, relinquishment, suspension/revocation, ceasing sales), final return/payment within 90 days (contrast 30 days for medical dispensaries); unpaid tax is a lien. Effective date: the 11% tax is in SB 220 (2023). The WVDA Hemp/Kratom guide states the 11% tax applies to the listed products “Under WV SB220”. The exact operative effective date of the tax (passage vs 1 January 2024 permit/registration start, when the WVDA guide's 2024 registration form applies) was not stated in §19-12E-12(i) text fetched [VERIFY] — check enrolled SB 220 effective-date clause and Tax Division hemp-tax guidance; do not assume 1 January 2024 is the tax effective date. Distribution — two-step, as written (note awkward numbering in the PDF): (1) §19-12E-12(i)(5): all money from the tax (plus interest/additions, less refunds, less the Tax Commissioner fee retained under §11-10-27) “shall be deposited into the Agricultural Fees Fund created by §19-1-4c of this code.” (2) §19-12E-12(i)(8)(A): funds from the tax, less the §11-10-27 fee, deposited in the Agricultural Fees Fund “shall be divided and deposited as follows: (i) Sixty-five percent shall remain in the Agriculture Fees Fund for the use of the commissioner for administering and enforcing the provisions of this article; (ii) Five percent shall be transferred to the Fight Substance Abuse Fund created by §60A-9-8 of this code; and (iii) Thirty percent shall be deposited in the Alcohol Beverage Control Enforcement Fund established by the provisions of §60-7-13 of this code.” “In addition to all other applicable taxes” means the 11% does not displace sales/use tax: §19-12E-12(i)(3) expressly provides that WV use tax “shall be collected from sellers and marketplace facilitators … and referrers” making/furthering retail sales of hemp-derived cannabinoid products into WV, subject to §11-15A-1 et seq. (including §§11-15A-6a/6b), with nexus attaching “with relation to any such activity” (i.e. without the usual §11-15A-6b(e) thresholds). Whether the 6% state (+ up to 1% municipal) sales tax is in practice collected in addition to the 11% on in-state retail sales, and how the “not a separate line item” rule interacts with sales-tax itemisation, is a Tax Division guidance question [VERIFY] — do not state a combined 17%/18% rate as settled law. What is NOT taxed at 11%: by §19-12E-12(b)'s proviso, naturally occurring select plant-based derivative products “not containing tetrahydrocannabinol content” are outside §19-12E-12 — so non-THC CBD products are not within the 11% tax on the face of the section [VERIFY] against Tax Division product list.

2.4 Medical Cannabis Program Fund — source, distribution, balances

“(a) Fund established. — The Medical Cannabis Program Fund is established as a special fund in the State Treasury. Money in the fund is appropriated as set forth in subsection (c) of this section. Any amount unspent at the end of a fiscal year shall be appropriated to the bureau for its operations. (b) Source of funds. — Fees and taxes payable under this act shall be deposited into the fund. The money deposited into the fund may only be used for the purposes set forth in this section. Any interest accrued shall be deposited into the fund. (c) Use of proceeds. — Money in the fund is allocated in accordance with the following percentages: (1) Fifty-five percent of the revenue in the fund shall be allocated to the bureau. (2) The remaining forty-five percent of the revenue in the fund shall be allocated as follows: (A) Fifty percent shall be allocated to the Fight Substance Abuse Fund created by section eight, article nine, chapter sixty-a of the code. (B) Forty percent shall be allocated to the Division of Justice and Community Services, for grants to local law enforcement agencies for training, drug diversion, and other programs focused on crime and addiction, pursuant to and in accordance with the provisions of article nine-a, chapter fifteen of this code. (C) Ten percent shall be allocated to the fund created in section four, article twenty-nine, chapter thirty, to be used for law enforcement professional training and professional development programs.” — §16A-9-2, code.wvlegislature.gov/16A-9 (PDF) — Accessed 2026-10-24.

Worked percentages (arithmetic from the statute, not a separate source): Bureau 55%; Fight Substance Abuse Fund 50% × 45% = 22.5% of total; Division of Justice and Community Services 40% × 45% = 18%; Law-enforcement professional training (Ch. 30, Art. 29, §4) 10% × 45% = 4.5%. Balances / revenue figures — NO official figure retrieved [UNAVAILABLE]. The secondary CannBus illustration (“If distributed in October 2025, about $19 million would go to the Bureau for Public Health, $8 million to the Fight Substance Abuse Fund, $6 million to the Division of Justice and Community Services and $1.5 million to law enforcement training”, on cumulative collections “$34 million by October 2025 and about $38 million by April 2026, since the first dispensary opened in November 2021 (taxes, license fees and interest combined)”, with money “Held by the State Treasurer, with none spent as of October 2025”) is secondary/modelled, not an official Treasurer, Auditor or OMC figure and is not adopted as a fund balance: cannbus.org — Accessed 2026-10-24. Note also that the secondary $19m/$8m/$6m/$1.5m split sums to $34.5m, not $34m, and the “none spent” claim sits uneasily with §16A-9-2(a) (unspent year-end amounts appropriated to the bureau for operations) — both reasons to require an official source. Follow-up for Run 2 / Part 6: WV State Treasurer / State Auditor fund reports, OMC annual reports, and Tax Division medical-cannabis tax collection reports. The hemp-tax Agricultural Fees Fund balance is likewise [UNAVAILABLE]. Permit-fee revenue (§16A-6-6) is also deposited “into the fund” (§16A-6-6(4)) — so the Fund receives both the 10% tax and all organisation/permit fees, plus interest.

2.5 IRS §280E and West Virginia decoupling — the most consequential tax gap in this report

Federal (baseline, not re-researched): §280E disallows deductions for a business trafficking in Schedule I/II substances. For state-licensed medical activity moved to Schedule III effective 28 April 2026, §280E is generally lifted; adult-use remains fully subject to §280E. WV has no adult-use programme, so the live question is solely whether WV state taxable income follows the federal Schedule III relief. West Virginia position found: (1) No decoupling statute was located. No provision in Chapter 16A, and no §11-21 / §11-24 provision opened in this run, creates a WV addition or subtraction for cannabis business expenses, or expressly decouples from §280E in either direction. A secondary tax guide states: “West Virginia conforms to IRC §280E for state income tax purposes; cannabis businesses cannot deduct ordinary business expenses on their state returns” because WV personal/corporate income taxes start with federal taxable income and “[t]here is no state carve-out”. Source (secondary, 1 July 2025): Cannabis CPA tax guide — Accessed 2026-10-24. That guide predates Schedule III, so its conclusion describes the Schedule I/II era and must not be quoted as the post-April 2026 position without updating. (2) WV is a fixed-date conformity state. Secondary conformity analysis lists WV among fixed-date states, and a Deloitte summary of HB 2025 (signed 24 Feb 2025) states the 2025 conformity law “generally adopts all amendments made to federal law after December 31, 2023, but prior to January 1, 2025, for West Virginia corporation net income and personal income tax purposes” and “no amendment to the laws of the United States made on or after January 1, 2025, shall be given any effect”. Sources (secondary): Andre Tax Co.; Deloitte — Accessed 2026-10-24. If that fixed-date structure still governed in 2026, an April 2026 federal Schedule III rule would NOT automatically change WV taxable income, because the current conformity windows end 1 January 2026, before the April 2026 federal change. (3) Current Code text shows both §11-21-9(a) and §11-24-3(a) amended in 2026, with conformity windows ending 1 January 2026. The carrying 2026 conformity bill number is [VERIFY]. Tax Division guidance on how 280E relief interacts with WV’s starting-point computation is also [VERIFY]. Bottom line, flagged as analysis [VERIFY] before tax advice: federally, a WV state-licensed medical grower, processor or dispensary should generally be outside §280E for its Schedule III medical activity from 28 April 2026 (subject to the federal rule's own scope, DEA registration, and business-specific facts — none re-researched here). For WV state income tax, do not assume the same relief applies automatically: WV has no decoupling provision in either direction, and its fixed-date conformity may require a 2026 (or 2027) conformity update to import the federal change. Until the Tax Division or a 2026/2027 conformity act confirms otherwise, the prudent statement is: WV state §280E treatment after April 2026 is UNRESOLVED [VERIFY] — Tax Division guidance / conformity act. This is the single most important tax follow-up for WV operators, and it must not be collapsed into “WV decouples” or “WV conforms” without that confirmation. Hemp businesses and §280E: hemp (as defined in §19-12E-3) is not a controlled substance under the hemp definition, so §280E does not apply to lawful hemp activity on the same basis; hemp-derived cannabinoid permittees' state income-tax treatment follows ordinary conformity, subject to the same fixed-date caveat for any federal hemp-definition change. No WV hemp-specific income-tax provision was located [VERIFY].

2.6 Local taxes

  • Medical cannabis: none permitted on sales that have borne the 10% privilege tax — §16A-9-1(h) bars special-district excise tax and county/municipal sales tax on those sales (see 2.2). No county or municipal medical-cannabis gross-receipts tax was located in Chapter 16A [VERIFY] — Part 3 local-fee check: local licence/permit fees are a different question from local taxes.
  • Hemp-derived cannabinoid products: the 11% privilege tax is “in addition to all other applicable taxes” (§19-12E-12(i)), and use-tax collection from remote sellers is expressly provided (§19-12E-12(i)(3)). Municipal sales/use tax (up to 1%) may therefore apply to hemp-derived retail sales in adopting municipalities, in addition to the 6% state sales/use tax and the 11% privilege tax — but the combined application was not confirmed in Tax Division guidance [VERIFY] — see 2.3.
  • No county-level cannabis tax (as distinct from municipal sales tax) was located for either medical or hemp products [VERIFY] in Part 3.

Part 3 — Local Rules

3.1 What authority do counties vs. municipalities have? Which level does what?

General land-use authority — both levels, within their own jurisdiction:

“The governing body of a municipality or a county may regulate land use within its jurisdiction by: (1) Adopting a comprehensive plan; (2) Working with the planning commission and the public to develop a zoning ordinance; and (3) Enacting a zoning ordinance.” — W. Va. Code §8A-7-1(a), code.wvlegislature.gov/8A-7-1 — Accessed 2026-10-24. “A zoning ordinance may cover a county's entire jurisdiction or parts of its jurisdiction.” — §8A-7-1(b). “A zoning ordinance shall cover a municipality's entire jurisdiction.” — §8A-7-1(c). “A municipality may adopt, by reference, the zoning ordinance of the county in which it is located.” — §8A-7-1(d).
LevelWhat it can doWhat it cannot do / limit
County commission — by resident votePass an ordinance prohibiting a medical cannabis organization in that county. See §16A-7-6.It does not issue the state permit. Before a state permit may issue, the Bureau must obtain a written statement from the county commission that the county has not voted under §16A-7-6 to disapprove a medical cannabis organization in the county — §16A-6-13(b)(2).
County board of healthMust give written approval for the county where the permit will be located/operated, before a permit may issue — §16A-6-13(b)(1). This is a county-level gate, distinct from the county commission vote.The board-of-health approval is not itself a county “opt-out” vote.
Municipality — by ordinanceEnact an ordinance “prohibiting or limiting the number and type of medical cannabis organizations permitted to operate in the municipality, including the time, place, and manner of operation” — §16A-15-7(3). Apply its ordinary zoning to a grower/processor as manufacturing/processing/production, and to a dispensary as commercial — §16A-15-7(1)–(2).It cannot create a state permit, waive the state 1,000-foot school/daycare rule in §16A-8-2 (only the Bureau may amend/waive that prohibition, on clear and convincing evidence of need for adequate patient access), and it cannot authorise a use Chapter 16A forbids.
Municipality / county — notice dutyA municipality that enacts a restrictive ordinance under §16A-15-7 “shall promptly notify the bureau” — §16A-15-8(a). A county commission “shall notify the bureau if a county votes to prohibit allowance of a medical cannabis organization pursuant to section six, article seven” — §16A-15-8(b).The statute creates the notice duty; it does not in the text opened create a published, consolidated OMC opt-out register. See 3.3.
“(1) A grower/processor shall meet the same municipal zoning and land use requirements as other manufacturing, processing and production facilities that are located in the same zoning district.” “(2) A dispensary shall meet the same municipal zoning and land use requirements as other commercial facilities that are located in the same zoning district.” “(3) A municipality may enact an ordinance prohibiting or limiting the number and type of medical cannabis organizations permitted to operate in the municipality, including the time, place, and manner of operation.” — W. Va. Code §16A-15-7, code.wvlegislature.gov/16A-15 — Accessed 2026-10-24. Repeated in rule form at 64 CSR 109, §23 (older numbering) / §22 in the Justia republication: Justia, 64 CSR 109 §22 — Accessed 2026-10-24. [VERIFY] current CSR section number, because OMC 2025 rule revisions renumbered sections; substantive “same treatment” text is identical in both versions found.

State siting rule that local zoning does not displace — dispensary buffer:

“A dispensary may not be located within one thousand feet of the property line of a public, private or parochial school or a daycare center.” — §16A-8-2(a)(3). “A dispensary may only dispense medical cannabis in an indoor, enclosed, secure facility located within this state, as determined by the bureau.” — §16A-8-2(a)(1). “A dispensary may not operate on the same site as a facility used for growing and processing medical cannabis.” — §16A-8-2(a)(2). Bureau waiver: “The bureau may amend a prohibition under subsection (a)(3) of this section if it is shown by clear and convincing evidence that the amendment is necessary to provide adequate access to patients. An amendment may include additional security, physical plant of a facility or other conditions necessary to protect children.” — §16A-8-2(b). Source: code.wvlegislature.gov/16A-8 — Accessed 2026-10-24.

Secondary guidance (HartmanCosco, former Bureau for Public Health counsel, 7 Aug 2020) describes measurement front-door to school/daycare property line along a pedestrian-legal street/sidewalk path, horizontal measurement only. That measurement detail was not in the §16A-8-2 text opened and is therefore [VERIFY] against current 64 CSR 112: HartmanCosco — Accessed 2026-10-24. Dispensary-location factors the Bureau must consider — state “regional” test, not a local cap: “The bureau shall consider the following when issuing a dispensary permit: (1) Geographic location; (2) Regional population; (3) The number of patients suffering from serious medical conditions; (4) The types of serious medical conditions; (5) Access to public transportation; (6) Approval by local health departments; (7) Whether the county has disallowed the location of a grower, processor, or dispensary; and (8) Any other factor the bureau deems relevant.” — §16A-6-3(d), code.wvlegislature.gov/16A-6 — Accessed 2026-10-24. “When the boundaries under subsection (d), section three of this article are established, the bureau shall publish notice of the determination in the State Register. The bureau may adjust the boundaries as necessary every two years. Notice of any adjustment to the boundaries shall be published in the State Register.” — §16A-6-4, same page. No current senate-district quota, regional quota, or district map was found in the current Chapter 16A text opened. Secondary summaries claiming a senate-district distribution or dispensary figures above the statutory cap conflict with the statutory cap of 100 in §16A-6-13(3) and are treated as [VERIFY] / conflicting, not as current law. A secondary summary of SB 1037 (2019) says that bill “disbanded” defined medical-cannabis regions in favour of scoring (JD Supra — Accessed 2026-10-24). Whether OMC still publishes internal boundaries under §16A-6-4 is [UNAVAILABLE] from pages opened.

Hemp / CBD shops — different local position from medical cannabis organisations: Chapter 16A opt-out, zoning and permit rules apply to a “medical cannabis organization” — grower, processor, dispensary — not to an ordinary hemp/CBD retailer as such. For hemp products, the Industrial Hemp Act states: “a person need not obtain a license to possess, handle, transport, or sell hemp products or extracts, including those containing one or more hemp-derived cannabinoids, including CBD.” — §19-12E-5(g)(1), as reproduced at code.wvlegislature.gov/19-12E-5 and 19-12E PDF — Accessed 2026-10-24. That means, on the text opened: a hemp/CBD shop is subject to general local authority only — general zoning under Chapter 8A, general municipal business licensing and B&O tax under Chapter 8 (see 3.4), building/fire/health codes — not to a Chapter 16A county opt-out vote or a municipal §16A-15-7 medical-cannabis ordinance. Caveat: a secondary 2026 hemp-beverage survey claims WV now has retailer/manufacturer permits, age-21 and an 11% hemp excise, enforced with ABCA (CSG South — Accessed 2026-10-24). That claim conflicts with the §19-12E-5(g)(1) “need not obtain a license” text opened and was not confirmed from a WV statute or WVDA rule in this pass. Treat any post-2024 hemp-derived-cannabinoid retail permit, age or tax rule as [VERIFY] for Part 1 hemp; do not import it into Part 3 as settled local law. Note: §19-12E-12 does require permits for hemp-derived cannabinoid products (see 1.5.4) — the tension between §19-12E-5(g)(1) and §19-12E-12(d)(1) is itself a follow-up; the Part 3 statement above is the general hemp/CBD-shop position on the §19-12E-5 text, not a §19-12E-12 permittee position. Local smoke-shop / tobacco-store zoning is a separate use classification in at least Morgantown (see 3.5) and is not a medical-cannabis rule.

3.2 Local opt-out rules, bans, moratoriums and caps — mechanism and dates

County mechanism — vote of residents, indefinite until changed by a subsequent vote: “A county may pass an ordinance by vote of the residents of the county to prohibit the operation or location of a medical cannabis organization within that particular county. A prohibition under this section shall remain in effect unless and until changed by a subsequent vote.” — §16A-7-6, code.wvlegislature.gov/16A-7 — Accessed 2026-10-24. No sunset, no referendum-percentage, and no county-by-county list is stated in §16A-7-6 itself. How a particular county put the question (commission ordinance vs ballot) is a county-record question, not stated in state text. Municipal mechanism — ordinance, no resident vote required by §16A-15-7: §16A-15-7(3) authorises a municipality by ordinance to prohibit or limit number, type, time, place and manner. No state-imposed municipal cap number, moratorium length, or referendum requirement appears in §16A-15-7 or §16A-15-8 as opened. No statewide local cap in Chapter 16A: the only numeric caps in the text opened are the state caps in §16A-6-13 (10 growers, 10 processors, 100 dispensaries, 10 dispensaries per person, 1 grower and 1 processor per person). A municipality may impose a lower local number by ordinance under §16A-15-7(3), but no municipality-specific number was confirmed from an official city code in this pass except as noted in 3.5. Any claim of a named town's numeric cap found only in a secondary source is [VERIFY].

Local possession-penalty ordinances — distinct from business opt-outs, and the only confirmed, dated local cannabis ordinances found for the nine jurisdictions:

  • Morgantown, Monongalia County — possession <15 grams reduced to a $15 fine, no jail. Secondary reporting, dated 2020: unanimous Morgantown City Council vote; ACLU-WV editorial summarising the ordinance as lowering misdemeanour possession of less than 15 grams in city limits to a $15 fine and no jail time, versus state penalties of 90 days–6 months and/or up to $1,000: Daily Athenaeum / ACLU-WV editorial — Accessed 2026-10-24. Underlying Morgantown ordinance text/number was not opened — [VERIFY] against City of Morgantown code before citing a section number. State prosecution by WVU Police, county sheriff or State Police would not be barred by a municipal penalty change, per contemporaneous council discussion in the same source set.
  • Charleston, Kanawha County — citizens-initiated depenalisation, Bill No. 8039 (2024). NORML summary, 20 Aug 2024: Charleston City Council amended and approved a citizens-initiated measure under Charleston City Charter §89 (petition, 4,000+ signatures); as amended, Bill No. 8039 “removes fines and the threat of jail time for first-time low-level (possession of 15 grams or less) marijuana offenders”; prior-conviction fines $250–$500; eligible convictions dismissed after payment/terms, others expungeable within six months: NORML, 20 Aug 2024 — Accessed 2026-10-24. This is a secondary source; Charleston ordinance text itself was not opened — [VERIFY] against City of Charleston code. A later, separate Charleston petition item numbered Bill No. 8043 / “Sensible Marijuana Ordinance” appears in 2025 secondary coverage; whether 8043 is a successor, amendment or separate petition to 8039 is [UNAVAILABLE] in this pass and the two numbers must not be conflated.
  • Fairmont, Marion County — petition campaign reported, not a confirmed adopted ordinance. A 2022 secondary item reports West Virginia Can't Wait working on municipal ballot campaigns in Charleston and Fairmont for “no fines, no time and no court costs” for possession in Fairmont city limits: MJInvest, 2022 — Accessed 2026-10-24. No adopted Fairmont possession ordinance was confirmed — [UNAVAILABLE] / [VERIFY].

No adopted county business ban, municipal business ban, moratorium or municipal numeric cap with a date was confirmed from an official county/city source in this pass for any of the nine jurisdictions. That is a finding of non-retrieval, not proof that none exists.

3.3 Official list of jurisdictions that opted out or restricted — NOT retrieved

There is no official OMC opt-out/restriction list that could be retrieved and verified in this pass. Exact URLs to check (the places such a list would have to appear if published): OMC Industry page omc.wv.gov/industry — Accessed 2026-10-24; OMC Industry Applications page omc.wv.gov/industry/application — Accessed 2026-10-24; OMC Acts/Rules page omc.wv.gov/rules and omc.wv.gov/actsrules — Accessed 2026-10-24; OMC dispensary list PDF OMC facilities list (PDF) — Accessed 2026-10-24 (it lists locations that are permitted, it is not an opt-out list); OMC dashboard / stat sheet linked from the Industry page — Accessed 2026-10-24 via the Industry page text; the dashboard itself was not opened as a separate opt-out source in this pass. Why this matters: §16A-15-8 requires municipalities and county commissions to notify the Bureau, but the text opened does not require the Bureau to publish the notifications as a list, and no such list was found on omc.wv.gov in this pass. The correct follow-up is a direct OMC records request / dashboard check, not a secondary “opt-out map”. Stale secondary claim to discard: a low-quality secondary page (“State Regs Today,” undated, pre-programme in its own text) claims Berkeley County and Martinsburg opted out and Jefferson County / Charles Town were opting out. That page itself says the state programme had “not yet implemented” at the time of writing, and it is directly contradicted by present-day facts: Martinsburg (Berkeley County) and Charles Town (Jefferson County) both have operating dispensaries in the OMC facilities list and in operator listings (see 3.5–3.6). Do not carry the Berkeley/Martinsburg/Jefferson opt-out claim into Run 1 as current. If those jurisdictions ever adopted a §16A-7-6 / §16A-15-7 ordinance, it is either repealed, superseded, or was never effective — [VERIFY] against Berkeley County Commission minutes and Martinsburg / Charles Town council minutes if a historical note is needed. Positive opt-in evidence (secondary, pre-programme, for context only): Putnam County voters approved four ballot measures allowing medical-marijuana facilities (reported >60% approval, May primary, pre-2021): Green Health Docs — Accessed 2026-10-24. Putnam is not one of the nine required jurisdictions and the ballot-measure text was not opened — [VERIFY] if used.

3.4 Local licence / permit fees

No cannabis-specific local licence fee was found in Chapter 16A or in an official city code opened in this pass. What exists is the general municipal licence framework, which applies to any state-licensed activity done inside a municipality:

“Whenever anything, for which a state license is required, is to be done within the corporate limits of any municipality, the governing body shall have plenary power and authority, unless prohibited by general law, to require a municipal license and for the use of the municipality to impose a reasonable tax which may not exceed the amount of the state license tax. Upon proper application for a municipal license and payment of the prescribed reasonable tax by any person who has a valid and subsisting state license, the municipal license shall be issued.” — W. Va. Code §8-13-4(a), code.wvlegislature.gov/8-13-4 — Accessed 2026-10-24.
“Except where a business license tax or fee has been established by the West Virginia Code, the governing body of a municipality may, in lieu of the provisions of subsection (a), enact an ordinance creating an annual general municipal business license for anything which requires a state license that is done within the corporate limits of a municipality, the tax for which may not exceed 20 dollars.” — §8-13-4(b), same source.

Practical reading, stated carefully: a municipality may charge up to the state licence amount under (a), or may instead adopt the flat general licence capped at $20 under (b). Which route a given city chose for dispensaries is a city-ordinance question — [UNAVAILABLE] city-by-city in this pass. Non-official figures — do not cite as official: a secondary business-licence guide gives “typical” annual municipal fees of ~$20 Charleston, ~$20 Huntington, ~$20 Morgantown, ~$15 Wheeling, and a $30 State Business Registration Certificate: Commenda (secondary) — Accessed 2026-10-24. Those are secondary, approximate, and unverified against city fee schedules; recorded only so the dossier does not mistake them for official fees. The $30 state certificate figure is likewise secondary in this pass. Local tax note (for Part 2 cross-reference, not a fee): municipalities also levy Business & Occupation (B&O) tax on gross receipts under Chapter 8, Article 13, at city-specific rates by business class. No dispensary-specific B&O rate or exemption was found in the official text opened — [VERIFY] city by city. The HartmanCosco summary notes generally that municipalities may require a municipal licence or assess a B&O privilege tax: HartmanCosco, 2020 — Accessed 2026-10-24 (secondary, 2020). State fees are not local fees and are in Part 4: grower/processor $5,000 application + $50,000 permit + $5,000 renewal; dispensary $2,500 application + $10,000 per location + $2,500 renewal; $250 amendment — §16A-6-6.

3.5 Zones where businesses are permitted + local buffer rules differing from the state — jurisdiction by jurisdiction

State baseline for every jurisdiction: grower/processor = same zoning as other manufacturing/processing/production in that district; dispensary = same zoning as other commercial in that district (§16A-15-7, 64 CSR 109 §23/§22); dispensary ≥1,000 feet from a public/private/parochial school or daycare property line (§16A-8-2(a)(3)), Bureau-waivable only on clear and convincing evidence of patient-access need (§16A-8-2(b)); dispensary indoor/enclosed/secure, not on the same site as grow/process (§16A-8-2(a)(1)–(2)). No additional state buffer (e.g. no church, park, playground, or dispensary-to-dispensary distance) appears in §16A-8-2 as opened. Any local buffer beyond 1,000 feet school/daycare must come from a local ordinance — none was confirmed for the nine jurisdictions except Morgantown parking (below).

The fairmont.org “Cannabis Retailer / Lower-potency Hemp Edible Retailer in I-1” text is not attributed to Fairmont, WV (see scope warning).Fairmont, WV districts, buffer, cap — [UNAVAILABLE].
9. Jefferson County (incl. Charles Town, Ranson, Harpers Ferry, Shepherdstown)County ordinance scope (official): Jefferson County Zoning and Land Development Ordinance, adopted 7 July 1988, as amended, amendments adopted by the County Commission 22 Jan 2026: “These regulations shall apply to all properties within Jefferson County, West Virginia; but shall not include the incorporated areas.” Source: Jefferson County ordinance (PDF) and Jefferson County document — Accessed 2026-10-24. No medical-cannabis use, district or buffer was found in the Jefferson County ordinance excerpts searched. Charles Town does have an operating dispensary: Country Grown — Charles Town, 672 E Washington St, Charles Town, WV 25414 (operator site, Accessed 2026-10-24), disproving any current Charles Town ban. Incorporated towns (Charles Town, Ranson, etc.) zone themselves; unincorporated Jefferson County is zoned by the county ordinance above.Jefferson County unincorporated medical-cannabis district treatment, Charles Town / Ranson district treatment, any local buffer/cap — [UNAVAILABLE].

Other local-code examples found (not in the nine, useful as a template check, not to be generalised): Moundsville, WV (§1355, Historic and Neighborhood Commercial, “Medical Cannabis Dispensary” listed, passed 5-5-20; 7-5-23): Moundsville code — Accessed 2026-10-24. Montgomery, WV zoning ordinance defines “Medical Cannabis Organization” as “a dispensary, grower, or processor” per Chapter 16A: Montgomery ordinance (PDF) — Accessed 2026-10-24. These confirm the pattern — towns add the Chapter 16A use to existing commercial/industrial districts — but they are not evidence for the nine jurisdictions.

3.6 Business concentration — ONLY as official sources say

No ranking, clustering estimate or “top city” claim is made here, per instructions. What official sources actually say: (1) State caps and location factors, not quotas: §16A-6-13 caps (10/10/100) and §16A-6-3(d) factors (geography, regional population, patient numbers/types, public-transport access, local health-department approval, county disallowance). That is the only official “allocation” rule in the current text opened. (2) Boundaries: §16A-6-4 requires Bureau boundary determinations to be published in the State Register and allows adjustment every two years — but no boundary notice or map was retrieved. [UNAVAILABLE] (3) Where dispensaries actually are (OMC facilities list, official but undated in fetch): the OMC PDF lists dispensaries statewide by town/county, including, for the nine jurisdictions: Dunbar/South Charleston (Kanawha); Huntington (Cabell); Morgantown (Monongalia); Parkersburg (Wood); Martinsburg/Inwood (Berkeley); Fairmont (Marion); plus Charles Town (Jefferson) via operator listing. Wheeling and Beckley locations appear in operator listings, not in the OMC PDF excerpt retrieved. No count by city/county is stated here because the OMC PDF excerpt was truncated and a hand-count from a truncated list would be an estimate. (4) Conflicting secondary counts — do not pick one as official: May 2025 secondary summary: 65 active dispensaries, 9 processors, 9 growers; Oct 31, 2025 secondary summary citing OMC: 61+ dispensaries operating, 9 processors, 8 growers; Sep 2025 secondary: 74 dispensary permits issued, 64 operating. These conflict on both date and definition (issued vs. operating vs. active) and none was verified against a dated OMC dashboard export in this pass. Official OMC count as of Oct 2026 = [UNAVAILABLE] in this pass; follow-up is the OMC dashboard/stat sheet linked from omc.wv.gov/industry. (5) Secondary “cluster” claim to exclude: a secondary market report's claim of “8 dispensaries in the Morgantown area alone” is a secondary calculation, not an official concentration finding, and is not adopted. Conclusion for 3.6: on official sources opened, the only supportable concentration statement is qualitative: dispensaries are statewide and present in all nine required jurisdictions (by OMC list and/or operator listing), under a statewide 100-permit cap with Bureau location factors — not under a published regional quota. Anything more precise needs the OMC dashboard or the State Register boundary notices.

Part 4 — Licence Types

Official info pages (all Accessed 2026-10-24): OMC Industry omc.wv.gov/industry; OMC Industry Applications omc.wv.gov/industry/application; OMC Acts/Rules omc.wv.gov/rules and omc.wv.gov/actsrules; OMC News — “Medical Cannabis Laboratory Permit Applications Open,” 13 Mar 2024: omc.wv.gov/news; Chapter 16A, Article 6: code.wvlegislature.gov/16A-6; §16A-6-6 fees: code.wvlegislature.gov/16A-6-6; §16A-6-13 limitations: code.wvlegislature.gov/16A-6-13; §16A-7-4 laboratory: code.wvlegislature.gov/16A-7-4; CSR: 64 CSR 109 (general), 64 CSR 110 (grower/processor), 64 CSR 111 (laboratories), 64 CSR 112 (dispensaries), 64 CSR 113 (safe harbour). Rule authorisation for the 2024–2025 revisions is in §64-5-1(g)–(k) (2025). 64 CSR 111 filing/effective date 8 May 2025, sunset 1 Aug 2030, per Cornell LII republication: Cornell LII, 64 CSR 111 — Accessed 2026-10-24.

4.0 The permit system in one paragraph

“The following entities shall be authorized to receive a permit to operate as a medical cannabis organization to grow, process or dispense medical cannabis: (1) Growers. (2) Processors. (3) Dispensaries.” — §16A-6-1, code.wvlegislature.gov/16A-6 — Accessed 2026-10-24.

There is no separate wholesale, distribution, transport, delivery, or retail (non-dispensary) permit class in Chapter 16A as opened. Laboratories are not listed in §16A-6-1; they are a Bureau-approved / Office of Laboratory Services-certified testing function under §16A-7-4, governed by 64 CSR 111. Clinical registrants (Article 14) are not a fourth retail class; they are entities holding all three §16A-6 permits plus an academic-clinical-research-centre contract (see 4.5). Common rules for all §16A-6 permittees: permit is a revocable privilege, nontransferable: “A permit issued under this chapter shall be nontransferable.” — §16A-6-3(b); “The issuance or renewal of a permit shall be a revocable privilege.” — §16A-6-3(c). Validity 1 year: “A permit issued by the bureau shall be valid for one year from the date of issuance.” — §16A-6-9. Renewal window 4–6 months before expiry for both grower/processor and dispensary — §16A-6-6(1)(D), (2)(D). OMC Technical Advisory #25 (effective 24 Mar 2025) gives staggered expiry dates: growers expire 30 September each year, processors 12 November each year, dispensaries 28 January each year, renewals via Complia (MyComplia), failure to file = expiry and permanent cessation: OMC Technical Advisory #25010001 (PDF) — Accessed 2026-10-24. Permit must specify name/address, permitted activities, and land/buildings/facilities/location — §16A-6-7. Relocation / add-delete activities: Bureau may approve relocation within WV or addition/deletion of activities/facilities — §16A-6-8; amendment fee $250 — §16A-6-6(3). 64 CSR 109 §6.3 (older numbering) also lists $250 for change of ownership, change of location, or alteration of facility — [VERIFY] current CSR numbering. Grant test: effective diversion control, compliance with state law, ready/willing/able, ability to obtain land/buildings/equipment expeditiously, public interest, good moral character + financial fitness, security/tracking/recordkeeping/surveillance systems (motion-activated video surveillance may be required; recordings retained ≤180 days unless investigative/litigation need) — §16A-6-3(a)(1)–(8). Application contents: principals/operators/financial backers/employees verification, responsibilities, releases, fingerprint criminal-history checks via WV State Police → FBI, other-jurisdiction licence/discipline history, business-activity description, good-moral-character / land / security / compliance statements, names/residential addresses/titles of financial backers and principals, 10-year management/ownership affidavit, criminal/permit history — §16A-6-2(a). Felony drug bar (with dispensary waiver): financial backers, principals and employees may not hold/volunteer/be affiliated with a medical cannabis organisation if convicted of a felony related to sale/possession of illegal drugs/narcotics/controlled substances or conspiracy — §16A-6-12(a); a dispensary-affiliation waiver is in Bureau discretion under §16A-6-12(b), but “under no circumstances may a person prohibited under subsection (a) serve as a principal, financial backer, or manager who oversees conduct of the dispensary” (2021 bill text for the waiver proviso — [VERIFY] current §16A-6-12(b) wording, as the §16A-6 page fetch ended at §16A-6-13). Suspension/revocation: failure to maintain effective diversion control; violation of the Act or Bureau rule; intentional/knowing/reckless/negligent failure to comply with WV medical-cannabis law — §16A-6-11. Renewal denial: Bureau must renew unless applicant is unlikely to maintain diversion control or unlikely to comply with state law; denial/nonrenewal must specify in detail how requirements were not met; applicant may within 30 days submit additional material and/or demand a hearing — §16A-6-10. Supply chain closed loop: “A dispensary may only obtain medical cannabis from a grower or processor holding a valid permit under this act.” / “A grower or processor may only provide medical cannabis to a dispensary holding a valid permit under this act.” — §16A-6-13(a)(7)–(8). Seed-to-sale tracking: §16A-7-1 requires an electronic inventory tracking system directly accessible to the Bureau, daily, seed-to-sale for growers/processors and purchase-to-sale for dispensaries, with daily beginning/ending inventory, acquisitions, purchases/sales, disbursements, disposals, prices, recall system and waste tracking. System vendor name is not stated in the statute — vendor is Metrc per Part 1.7, not confirmed from an official page in the Parts 3–4 pass.

All §16A-6-6 fees: “All fees shall be paid by certified check or money order.” — §16A-6-6(1)(E), (2)(E). Fees “shall be deposited into the fund” (Medical Cannabis Program Fund) — §16A-6-6(4). Locations per permit: “each grower may have up to two locations per permit” — §16A-6-13(a)(1) proviso. No equivalent multi-location proviso for processors or dispensaries is stated in §16A-6-13(a)(2)–(3); a dispensary permit fee is per location, and a single person may hold up to 10 dispensary permits, which is how multi-site dispensary operators are structured. Whether a single processor permit covers multiple processing sites under 64 CSR 110 is [VERIFY].

4.2 Grower — detail

Allows cultivation, harvest, storage and sale to permitted processors/dispensaries only; must use an independent Bureau-approved laboratory, tested at harvest and at final processing (§16A-7-4). Must grow/store/harvest/process only in an indoor, enclosed, secure facility in WV with electronic locking, electronic surveillance and other Bureau-required features (§16A-7-2(b)). Seed: out-of-state seed only to initially grow; thereafter seed/plant material from another in-state grower/processor (§16A-7-2(a)). No outdoor cultivation is authorised by §16A-7-2(b) as opened. Cap / allocation: ≤10 statewide; ≤1 per person; up to 2 locations per permit (§16A-6-13(a)(1), (5)). No regional quota in current text; Bureau location factors in §16A-6-3(d) apply to dispensaries expressly, and §16A-6-3(a) public-interest / readiness test applies to all. Fees: $5,000 application (nonrefundable) + $50,000 permit (1 year, refundable if not granted) + $5,000 renewal (all locations, refundable if renewal not granted) + $250 amendment — §16A-6-6(1), (3). Capital (rule, not statute): 64 CSR 109 §26 (older numbering, Fox Rothschild republication of the rule): “An applicant for grower/processor permit shall provide an affidavit that the applicant has at least $2 million in capital, $500,000.00 of which must be on deposit with one or more financial institutions.” Affidavit in Bureau-prescribed form; signed release allowing Bureau to contact each listed financial institution to verify. Source: Fox Rothschild republication, 64 CSR 109 — Accessed 2026-10-24. This is a rule republication, not the SOS official CSR text; [VERIFY] current 64 CSR 109 §26 numbering/amounts against apps.sos.wv.gov before citing in the final dossier. No separate surety bond amount for growers was found in §16A-6-6 or in the §26 excerpt — no bond is stated; do not invent one (see 4.7 on insurance). Status: closed 18 Feb 2020, 3:00 p.m. EST — OMC Industry page. No upcoming grower window was announced on OMC pages opened. A new window would require OMC notice; none was found — [UNAVAILABLE], not “never”. Original awards (secondary, for context): growers announced 2 Oct 2020; processors 13 Nov 2020; dispensaries 29 Jan 2021 — OMC News headlines: omc.wv.gov/news — Accessed 2026-10-24. Headline dates are official; awardee lists themselves were not opened in this pass.

4.3 Processor — detail

Same fees, cap structure and closure as grower, except: ≤10 processors, ≤1 per person, no “up to two locations” proviso in §16A-6-13(a)(2). Processor activities are governed by 64 CSR 110 (growers/processors) and the permitted-forms rule at 64 CSR 110 §10: pill; oil; topical (gel, cream, ointment); form medically appropriate for vaporisation/nebulisation; liquid; dermal patch; dry leaf/plant form — Cornell LII, 64 CSR 110 §10 — Accessed 2026-10-24. A grower/processor may not manufacture/produce/assemble any medical-cannabis product, instrument or device without prior Bureau written approval — 64 CSR 110 §10.2. Capital affidavit is joint “grower/processor” in 64 CSR 109 §26.1: $2 million capital, $500,000 on deposit — same [VERIFY] caveat as 4.2. Vertical integration: expressly allowed — “A person may hold a grower permit, a processor permit, and a dispensary permit, or any combination thereof, concurrently.” — §16A-6-13(a)(9). There is no separate “vertically integrated” permit class or fee; vertical integration is achieved by holding the three permits concurrently, subject to the per-person caps (1 grower, 1 processor, up to 10 dispensaries). A secondary summary attributes vertical integration to SB 1037 (2019); the current §16A-6-13(a)(9) text is the authority, regardless of which amendment added it.

4.4 Dispensary — detail

Allows dispensing only to a patient/caregiver presenting a valid ID card; receipt with dispensary name/address/ID number, patient/caregiver name/address, date, practitioner form limitation, form/quantity (§16A-8-1(a)); receipt information filed with the Bureau via the electronic tracking system before dispensing (§16A-8-1(b)); no quantity greater than the certification allows and no prohibited form (§16A-8-1(c)); no more than a 30-day supply until all but a 7-day supply is exhausted (§16A-8-1(d)); verification via Bureau electronic database + dispensary tracking system before dispensing (§16A-8-1(e)); conformity to practitioner form limitation (§16A-8-1(f)); Bureau-approved safety insert (§16A-8-1(g)); sealed, labelled, child-resistant package with packaging date, use-by date, verbatim pregnancy/breastfeeding + driving/machinery + keep-out-of-reach-of-children warning, dose count + species + THC/CBD percentages, keep-in-original-container warning, unauthorised-use-is-unlawful warning (§16A-8-1(h)). Site rules: indoor/enclosed/secure in WV; not same site as grow/process; ≥1,000 ft from school/daycare property line; may sell, under Bureau conditions/limitations, medical devices/instruments needed to administer medical cannabis (§16A-8-2). Bureau may amend the 1,000-foot prohibition on clear and convincing evidence of patient-access need, with added security/physical-plant/child-protection conditions (§16A-8-2(b)). Fees: $2,500 application (nonrefundable) + $10,000 per location (1 year, refundable if not granted) + $2,500 renewal (all locations, refundable if renewal not granted) + $250 amendment — §16A-6-6(2)–(3). Capital: 64 CSR 109 §26.2 (older numbering): “An applicant for a dispensary permit shall provide an affidavit that the applicant has at least $150,000 on deposit with one or more financial institutions.” Same [VERIFY] current-numbering caveat as 4.2. Note the asymmetry: grower/processor affidavit is $2M total capital / $500k on deposit; dispensary affidavit is $150k on deposit only (no total-capital figure in the excerpt). Status: closed 18 Feb 2020, 3:00 p.m. EST — OMC Industry page. No upcoming dispensary window announced on OMC pages opened.

4.5 Laboratory, clinical registrant, and other types

Laboratory — correction of a common secondary error: current statute as opened:

“(a) A grower and processor shall contract with an independent laboratory to test the medical cannabis produced by the grower or processor. The bureau shall approve the laboratory and require that the laboratory report testing results in a manner as the bureau shall determine, including requiring a test at harvest and a test at final processing. The possession by a laboratory of medical cannabis shall be a lawful use.” “(b) All medical cannabis produced pursuant to this chapter shall be subject to testing as directed by the bureau.” “(c) The bureau shall ensure that there is sufficient testing capacity to meet patient demand.” “(d) All laboratories providing testing pursuant to this section shall be certified to do so by the Office of Laboratory Services.” — §16A-7-4, code.wvlegislature.gov/16A-7-4 — Accessed 2026-10-24.

There is no two-laboratory cap in §16A-7-4(d) as opened. The two-lab cap was proposed, not enacted in the text opened: SB 674 (2022, introduced 18 Feb 2022, referred to Health and Human Resources) would have added “no more than two laboratories”; HB 4627 (2022) passed the House (engrossed) with a two-lab cap terminating 1 Jan 2025, then died in the Senate (LegiScan status “Engrossed — Dead,” 12 Mar 2022 communicated to Senate). Sources: SB 674 introduced text; LegiScan HB 4627 — Accessed 2026-10-24. A secondary page claiming “following 2022 legislation, the total number of testing laboratory permits is limited to two” and “both laboratory permits are active” as of late 2025 is therefore not supported by the current §16A-7-4 text opened and must be flagged [VERIFY] — it may reflect an OMC administrative practice or a misreading of the dead 2022 bills, not a statutory cap. What is official for labs: OMC continues to accept laboratory applications (Industry page; News 13 Mar 2024 “Medical Cannabis Laboratory Permit Applications Open”; Industry Applications page: “Click here for the Medical Cannabis Laboratory Application… for all new lab applications, applicants need to click on the register tab”). Lab fees, bond, and any lab-specific capital amount are [UNAVAILABLE] in §16A-6-6 and in the 64 CSR 111 excerpts opened (testing at harvest/process lot, pesticides, solvents, water activity/moisture, THC/CBD, microbiological contaminants — 64 CSR 111 §10, via Justia, 64 CSR 111 §10 — Accessed 2026-10-24). Lab director / accreditation / COA requirements in secondary summaries are [VERIFY] against current 64 CSR 111.

Clinical registrant (Article 14):

“‘Clinical registrant’ means an entity that: (A) Holds a permit as a grower, processor and a dispensary; and (B) Has a contractual relationship with an academic clinical research center under which the academic clinical research center or its affiliate provides advice to the entity, regarding, among other areas, patient health and safety, medical applications and dispensing and management of controlled substances.” — §16A-14-1(2). “‘Academic clinical research center’ means an accredited medical school within this state that operates or partners with an acute care hospital licensed within this state.” — §16A-14-1(1). “Notwithstanding the limitations in section thirteen, article six of this chapter, the bureau may register up to four clinical registrants, and subject to the following: (1) A clinical registrant must pay the fees and meet all other requirements under this act for obtaining a permit as a grower, processor and a dispensary. (2) The clinical registrant must comply with all other requirements of this act regarding growing, processing and dispensing medical cannabis.” — §16A-14-2. Source: via code.wvlegislature.gov/16A — Accessed 2026-10-24. No operating clinical registrant was confirmed in this pass — [UNAVAILABLE]. Article 13 (health-care organisation / accredited college, university or medical-school research) is a research-study framework, not a retail permit class; its operating status is likewise [UNAVAILABLE] in this pass.

Employee registration (not a business licence, but a permit-adjacent fee) — NOTE: §16A-6-14 does not exist in current Code (Article 6 ends at §16A-6-13); the figures below are dead-bill text only and must not be presented as current law; any employee-registry rule is [VERIFY] against 64 CSR 109: 2021 bill text for §16A-6-14 states: any person employed by a medical cannabis organisation in growing/processing/dispensing must be 18+ and registered with the Bureau; the Bureau shall establish an employee registry and “may charge a registration fee not to exceed $25 for each registrant.” Source: SB 231 introduced text, SB 231 introduced text (PDF) — Accessed 2026-10-24. §16A-6-14 does not exist in current Code (Article 6 ends at §16A-6-13); the $25 / 18+ figures are therefore [VERIFY] against 64 CSR 109 (no current §16A-6-14 exists) before final use. Do not cite them as directly opened official text.

4.6 Social equity and local-ownership provisions

  • Social equity: none found in the statute or rules opened. Chapter 16A Articles 6, 7 and 15 as opened contain no equity set-aside, priority scoring, fee waiver/reduction, or ownership quota for communities disproportionately affected by cannabis prohibition, veterans, minorities, or small businesses. Secondary sources agree in the main: CannBus Q3 2026 summary (“West Virginia does not offer a state social equity program… no state-level licensing priorities, set-asides, or fee waivers/reductions”) and Drug Policy Watch 2026 brief (“does not include a robust social-equity licensing program”) — both secondary, Accessed 2026-10-24. One low-quality secondary page claims the opposite (diversity priorities, reduced fees) with no citation; that claim is discarded as unsupported. The only hardship relief found is for patients (card-fee waiver, Part 1), not licensees. Full CSR scoring-rubric check = [VERIFY]: §16A-6-3(e) requires “fair and objective evaluation… score each applicant numerically according to standards set forth in this chapter,” but the scoring rubric itself is in OMC application materials / 64 CSR 109, not in the statute excerpts opened, so a diversity point in scoring cannot be ruled in or out from the statute alone.
  • Local ownership / residency: formerly imposed, now NOT applied (official OMC notice). OMC Industry page states verbatim in substance: “Due to a recent supreme court decision (TENNESSEE WINE AND SPIRITS RETAILERS ASSN. v. RUSSELL F. THOMAS…), the residency requirement referred to by West Virginia Code §16A-6-3 Grant of Permit will not be applied to applicants seeking a West Virginia permit to operate as a medical cannabis organization.” Source: OMC Industry page — Accessed 2026-10-24. The 2019 SB 1037 residency rule (applicant must be a WV resident; business must be majority-owned by a WV resident) is therefore historical, not current practice, per OMC. Secondary summaries that still list “majority WV ownership required” as current are outdated and must not be carried forward. Whether §16A-6-3 still contains residency text on the books (as opposed to OMC non-enforcement) is [VERIFY] — the §16A-6 page opened in this pass (§§16A-6-1–6-13) does not show a residency clause in §16A-6-3(a), which is consistent with its later removal, but the full current §16A-6-3 was not separately re-opened.
  • No local-ownership preference (e.g. county-resident preference, local hiring quota) was found in §16A-6 as opened.

4.8 Adult-use licence types — PROPOSED ONLY, none enacted

There is no adult-use licence type in West Virginia law. The following are proposals only and must not be listed as available licences:

ProposalWhat it would do (proposal text)Status as found
HB 4371 (2026 Regular Session, Del. Hansen) — “Relating to legalizing cannabis production, sales and adult consumption,” new Article 16A-17 (§§16A-17-1–17-9)Legalise possession of ≤1 ounce by adults; authorise production, sales, transfer and transport only upon passing county referendums; county commissions to authorise a county referendum; Bureau for Public Health + localities to permit/licence production and sales facilities; localities to regulate manufacturing/sales locations; special excise tax + new fund; county local sales tax; employment, vehicle-operation, underage-use and private-property laws preserved; Medical Cannabis Act not alteredPending — House Judiciary Committee, last action 15 Jan 2026 (introduced 15 Jan 2026; referred to Judiciary then Finance). Source: WV Legislature bill-subject search, wvlegislature.gov subject search — Accessed 2026-10-24; bill text: HB 4371 introduced text — Accessed 2026-10-24. Not law. No adult-use licence, fee, cap or tax in HB 4371 is operative. Note the county-referendum structure: even if enacted, adult-use production/sales would be county-by-county, which would create a second, different local opt-in system from the medical §16A-7-6 opt-out.
HJR 37 (2026 Regular Session, Dels. Hornbuckle, Lewis, Garcia, Hansen) — constitutional amendment, new Art. IV §13Allow adult (21+) possession ≤2 ounces or 4 plants; Legislature may regulate manufacture/sale; prior possession ≤2 oz convictions subject to dismissal/expungement by petition to circuit court of conviction; designated “Amendment 1 / Cannabis Legalization Amendment” for the 2026 general election if referred by two-thirds of each houseIntroduced 5 Feb 2026; referred to House Judiciary then Rules; LegiScan status “Introduced — Dead” (secondary tracker). Source: LegiScan HJR 37 — Accessed 2026-10-24. Final wvlegislature.gov status = [VERIFY] before final dossier use; it did not reach voters as a 2026 amendment on the evidence found. A similarly numbered Senate resolution (SJR 5, 2026, and SJR 3, 2025) with identical 2 oz / 4 plant text was also found in search results — do not conflate SJR 3 / SJR 5 / HJR 37; each needs its own wvlegislature.gov status check.
HB 2145 (2023, cited in search results)Introduced text creates Article 5B-11, including §5B-11-15 (cannabis-establishment background checks) and §5B-11-16 (medical cannabis provisions unaffected), and “no medical cannabis provisions are affected”Status not verified in this pass — do not cite as pending or dead without a wvlegislature.gov check. Included only to flag that it appeared in the search set and must not be assumed to be an adult-use licensing bill.
HB 5260 (2026, edibles)Would allow medical edibles (per secondary 2026 briefs)Not an adult-use licensing bill; not a licence type. Secondary briefs say it passed the House 4 Mar 2026 and stalled/died in Senate Health and Human Resources at session end — [VERIFY] on wvlegislature.gov. Mentioned only to prevent confusion with HB 4371.

No other adult-use licence type (cultivator, manufacturer, retailer, microbusiness, delivery, lounge) exists in Chapter 16A as opened. Any adult-use “licence” mentioned in a secondary WV guide without a bill number is [UNAVAILABLE] / not law.

Part 5 — Licence Data Sources

Scope note: this part does not list every licence holder, as instructed. Parent agency note, for cross-checking with Parts 0–4: the OMC homepage now describes OMC as “a division under the Department of Health” — omc.wv.gov — Accessed 2026-10-24. The 2025 Biennial Report title pages still name the West Virginia Department of Health, Bureau for Public Health, Office of Medical Cannabis — OMC Biennial Report 2025 — Accessed 2026-10-24. Older DHHR-era URLs therefore redirect/duplicate current Department of Health material. Do not treat “DHHR” and “Department of Health” as two different current regulators without verification in Parts 9/11.

5.1 OMC — Biennial Report (the single most important licence-data source)

  • URL: https://omc.wv.gov/media/95/download?inline — Accessed 2026-10-24. Title/file: “West Virginia Office of Medical Cannabis — Biennial Report 2025.” The fetch returns a media/95/download?inline link; no original file name is shown in the fetch — do not invent one. Cite it by title + URL. Format: PDF, text-extractable, 1,156 lines in the fetch. No CSV, XLSX, or API version found. Legal basis stated in report: filed in compliance with W. Va. Code §16A-10-5, biennial. Coverage period: industry sales, harvest, price, patient, caregiver, physician, inspection and enforcement data from 1 November 2023 to 31 October 2025. Update frequency as stated: every two years (biennial). No interim dashboard was found to update Tables 13–15 between reports — [VERIFY] whether OMC publishes interim permit lists elsewhere.
  • Systems named in report: Metrc — seed-to-sale system “to ensure permittee accountability”; NCS Analytics — data-analytics platform that “collects and monitors Metrc data to generate reports related to medical cannabis sales, medical cannabis plant and product information, and provides alerts and reports to OMC regarding anomalous behavior”; MyComplia — houses “patient, caregiver, physician, and permittee information… patient demographics, total number of applications and their status”; Complia — renewal submission system for permittees, named in OMC Technical Advisory #25010001 — omc.wv.gov/media/67 — Accessed 2026-10-24. None of Metrc, NCS Analytics, MyComplia or Complia is a public download or public search page. They are regulator/industry systems. Do not list them as public licence sources.
  • Tables 13–15: “The Medical Cannabis Act allows for up to 10 grower permits, 10 processor permits, and 100 dispensary permits. The current operational growers, processors, and dispensaries that OMC regulates are listed in Tables 13, 14, and 15 below.” Fields in Tables 13–15 (as extracted): licence number, business name, trade name, city, county, date operational. No street address, phone, website, or permit-expiry date is shown in the extracted tables. Some dispensary rows in the extraction have no operational date shown — whether the date is blank in the PDF or lost in extraction is [VERIFY] against the PDF original.
  • Growers — Table 13, rows counted from extracted text: 9 — G020003 Columbia Care WV LLC — Falling Waters, Berkeley — 8 July 2021; G200004 Mountaineer Holding LLC / Trulieve — Lesage, Cabell — 29 June 2021; G190001 Harvest Care Medical, LLC / Country Grown Cannabis — Bridgeport, Harrison — 11 March 2022; G490002 Buckhannon Grow LLC — Buckhannon, Upshur — 11 March 2022; G440005 Tariff Labs, LLC — Millwood, Jackson — 2 Feb 2022; G410009 Holistic WV Farms I LLC / Holistic Industries — Beaver, Raleigh — 4 Aug 2022; G410007 Verano WV LLC — Beaver, Raleigh — 22 March 2022; G290008 Mountaineer Integrated Care Inc — Fort Ashby, Mineral — 2 March 2024; G490006 Armory Pharmaceutical, Inc — Buckhannon, Upshur — 13 July 2022. Source: OMC Biennial Report 2025 — Accessed 2026-10-24. Conflict [VERIFY]: a secondary market page summarising the same 31 Oct 2025 position reports 8 operating growers, not 9 — cannbus.org — Accessed 2026-10-24. The biennial report does not state a single grower total in the extracted prose. Use 9 rows in Table 13 as the auditable figure and flag the 8-vs-9 conflict; do not silently pick 8.
  • Processors — Table 14, rows counted from extracted text: 9 — P020004 Columbia Care WV LLC — Falling Waters, Berkeley — 17 Nov 2021; P170003 Harvest Care Medical, LLC — Bridgeport, Harrison — 15 Sept 2022; P490001 Buckhannon WV Processing LLC / Terrasana — Buckhannon, Upshur — 21 June 2023; P440005 Tariff Labs, LLC / Hillfire — Millwood, Jackson — 12 May 2022; P410007 Holistic WV Farms I LLC / Holistic Industries — Beaver, Raleigh — 5 April 2024; P410002 Verano WV LLC — Beaver, Raleigh — 4 Aug 2022; P290006 Mountaineer Integrated Care Inc — Fort Ashby, Mineral — 25 March 2024; P490008 Armory Pharmaceutical, Inc — Buckhannon, Upshur — 23 Feb 2023; P060009 Trulieve WV, Inc. / Trulieve — Lesage, Cabell — 9 June 2022. Source: OMC Biennial Report 2025 — Accessed 2026-10-24. This 9 matches the secondary summary's 9 processors — cannbus.org — but the secondary source is not a substitute for the OMC table.
  • Dispensaries — Table 15, rows counted from extracted text: 76. My count of distinct licence numbers in the extracted Table 15 is 76. Examples of the pattern (full holder-by-holder list is deliberately omitted per the task): Bear River (5 rows), Cannabist/Columbia Care (5), Curative Growth (3), Greene Street (3), Harvest Care (10), HCWV Sub (2), Greenlight/Logan (6), Mountaineer Releaf / Landing (7), New Leaf (5), Tariff Labs / Landing (2), Next Level Wellness (4), Trulieve entities (10), Verano / Zen Leaf (7), plus singletons including Kanacare, Foundry, Marachem/Barboursville, Mountaineer Integrated Care Fayetteville, WV Kindness/NewLeaf Huntington, Yuma Way/Huntington Gardens. Source: OMC Biennial Report 2025 — Accessed 2026-10-24. Conflicts — do not resolve by guessing [VERIFY]: secondary summary of the same report period: “Dispensaries (61 or more) — 61” operating — cannbus.org — Accessed 2026-10-24; same secondary page elsewhere: “Cap Up to 100 permits (secondary issued figure per Sep 2025 press, dropped)” — same URL; a separate secondary directory claims “a secondary licensed count above the statutory cap” and “64 dispensaries” in different pages — cannbus.org lsum and Sativa University — Accessed 2026-10-24 — both secondary, mutually inconsistent, and inconsistent with the 76-row count. Likely explanation, not proven: Table 15 may include permitted-but-not-yet-operational locations, locations whose operational date was lost in extraction, or duplicate trade-name rows for one licence. The report prose says Tables 13–15 list “current operational” organisations, but the extraction does not allow a clean operational-vs-permitted split. Recommended follow-up: recount Table 15 directly from the PDF original (not the text extraction) and ask OMC for the current operational count as of the Run 1 publication date. Data-quality warning in the extraction itself: one row reads “Harvest Care Medical, LLC / Figgs Enterprise, LLC — Wheeling — Kanawha” — OMC Biennial Report 2025 — Accessed 2026-10-24. Wheeling is in Ohio County, not Kanawha County. Treat that county field as a probable extraction/PDF error [VERIFY]; do not use it for any county count.
  • How to access/download: open the URL above; it downloads/displays inline as PDF. No export, CSV, or search filter exists for Tables 13–15. To reuse the data, the PDF must be transcribed manually. Limitations: biennial only; no live status (surrendered, suspended, revoked, relocated) between editions; no addresses; no lab table (see 5.3).
  • Earlier biennial report: the 2023 Biennial Report is cited by secondary sources as “2023 Biennial Report_Final_12.20.23.pdf” — FinChannel (secondary citation only) — Accessed 2026-10-24. Its direct omc.wv.gov URL was not recovered — [UNAVAILABLE] via searches run. Do not cite a guessed 2023 URL. The 2025 report itself gives one 2023 comparator: flower price $8.43/g in October 2023 — OMC Biennial Report 2025 — Accessed 2026-10-24.

5.2 OMC dispensary locator / dispensary list

  • OMC homepage (current navigation): omc.wv.gov — Accessed 2026-10-24. The homepage fetch shows cards for Adverse Event Reporting, Industry, How Do I?, Patients/Caregivers, Resources, Advisory Board. No dispensary-locator link, map, or address search is visible in the homepage text fetch.
  • OMC Events page: omc.wv.gov/events — Accessed 2026-10-24 (found via search; fetch shows only contact footer, no event list in text).
  • Secondary claim, not verified as a direct URL: “The West Virginia Office of Medical Cannabis provides a searchable, interactive map of licensed dispensaries” — Sanctuary Wellness Institute (secondary, updated 10 Sept 2026 in its own header — a date after the access date in the search index; treat its currency with caution) — Accessed 2026-10-24. A second secondary page says the same in substance: “The WV Office of Medical Cannabis maintains the official directory” — Squarespace page (secondary, non-official domain) — Accessed 2026-10-24.
  • Older OMC dispensary-list PDF found via search: URL (verbatim from search result): OMC facilities list (PDF) — Accessed 2026-10-24. Format: PDF list with dispensary name, website/phone, street address, county. Extracted examples include Cannabist–St. Albans (603 3rd Ave., St. Albans, WV 25177, Kanawha), Greenlight–Stollings, Greenlight–Summersville, NewLeaf–Triadelphia, Foundry–Weirton, Next Level–Weston, Trulieve–Weston, Zen Leaf–Westover, Country Grown–Wheeling, Zen Leaf–Wheeling, Kanacare–White Hall, Cannabist–Williamstown — same URL — Accessed 2026-10-24. Staleness [VERIFY]: the search index labels this result “Last Updated: 2854 days ago” (i.e. roughly 2018-era in the index, which cannot be correct for a post-2021 dispensary list). That index date is not credible as a document date. Do not present this PDF as current. It is evidence that OMC has published an address-level PDF list, not evidence that this copy is current. The 2025 Biennial Report Tables 13–15 supersede it for permit counts. No download button / export: it is a static PDF; there is no search, filter, or CSV export.
  • Conclusion for Part 5: there is no verified, current, interactive OMC dispensary locator URL to give in this report. The parent agent should either (a) ask OMC directly, or (b) have a browser-task agent navigate omc.wv.gov's Industry/Patients sections live and record the locator URL from the rendered site. Do not publish a locator URL guessed from the domain.

5.4 OMC enforcement / compliance actions

  • Source: 2025 Biennial Report, “Compliance & Enforcement” section only — OMC Biennial Report 2025 — Accessed 2026-10-24. Format: aggregate narrative + figures inside the PDF. No named enforcement list, no searchable database, no CSV found.
  • What it gives (counts only, full figures in Part 6.5 below): 817 total inspections (799 physical + 18 initial/operational), virtual-inspection counts by year, 1,088 advertising requests reviewed, 817 employee background checks, 110 investigations, 692 product/strain submissions, 11 Notices of Violation, 10 Administrative Orders, $3,900 fines collected — same URL — Accessed 2026-10-24.
  • What it does NOT give: names of violators, violation types by firm, penalty per firm, or dates of individual actions. Any firm-level enforcement history is [UNAVAILABLE] from public OMC sources found.
  • Related OMC compliance documents found (PDFs, via search): Compliance Tip — Medical Cannabis Sales / Dispensation to Patients and Caregivers, 18 Dec 2025 (header date in document; memorandum cited 16 Dec 2024 on card redesign) — OMC Compliance Tip (PDF) — Accessed 2026-10-24. Technical Advisory #25010001 — Permit Renewals, effective 24 March 2025 — omc.wv.gov/media/67 — Accessed 2026-10-24. This is the source for renewal windows (growers expire 30 Sept, processors 12 Nov, dispensaries 28 Jan each year) — same URL — Accessed 2026-10-24. Technical advisories listed in the biennial report (no direct URLs verified): Variance of THC Levels #23091501; Permit Renewals #25010001; Product, Strain Name, and Additive Approvals #25010002; Secure Delivery #25010003 — OMC Biennial Report 2025 — Accessed 2026-10-24.
  • OMC Acts/Rules page (context, not a data list): omc.wv.gov/actsrules and omc.wv.gov/rules — Accessed 2026-10-24. Lists Chapter 16A and rules 64-109 (General), 64-110 (Growers/Processors), 64-111 (Laboratories), 64-112 (Dispensaries), 64-113 (Safe Harbor Letter), “updated with the newly updated revisions now active in 2025” — same URLs — Accessed 2026-10-24.

5.6 WV Department of Agriculture — hemp product registration lists (the strongest hemp data source)

5.6.1 Hub page — start here

  • URL: agriculture.wv.gov — Hemp Products hub — Accessed 2026-10-24. Format: web page with linked lists/forms. No CSV/XLSX download button is shown for any list on this page in the text fetch.
  • Rules/fees stated on the page (for Part 7 cross-check): all hemp/kratom products for distribution in WV must register annually; $200 per product, or $100 per product if grown/harvested/manufactured in WV and registered with WV Grown; cap $1,000 per registrant ($500 WV Grown); white-label cap $2,000; retail/seller registration $100 per retail establishment; registrations run January–December and expire 31 December regardless of receipt date; “No hemp/kratom manufacturers or retailers… are registered until official notification has been received from the WVDA” — same URL — Accessed 2026-10-24.
  • Contact shown on page: hempproducts@wvda.us — same URL — Accessed 2026-10-24. (Official public contact; no personal phone/email reproduced.)
  • Links labelled on the hub page (text fetch, 2026-10-24): (1) 61 CSR 30 rule (via apps.sos.wv.gov); (2) SB 220; SB 985; (3) WV Hemp Products Guide (image/PDF); (4) WV Registration of Kratom Manufacturer 2025; (5) Hemp Manufacturer Registration of Hemp Products Form; (6) Hemp Retailer / Distributor / Seller Registration Form; (7) Kratom Retailer/Distributor Form; (8) Hemp Registered Product List — see 5.6.2; (9) Hemp Registered Retail Sellers List — direct URL not recovered — [VERIFY]; (10) Kratom Registered Retail Sellers List — direct URL not recovered — [VERIFY]; (11) Kratom Registered Product List — direct URL not recovered — [VERIFY]; (12) Retail Facilities Under Summary Suspension — direct URL not recovered — [VERIFY] (this is the closest thing to a hemp enforcement list found; its contents were not retrieved); (13) Hemp Product Complaint Form; (14) Additional WVDA Hemp Information; (15) Current List of Registerable (Allowable) Hemp Cannabinoids; (16) List of Contaminants for Hemp and Kratom. Source for all labels: WVDA hemp-products hub — Accessed 2026-10-24. Instruction: do not publish direct URLs for items 9–12, 15–16 from this report. They must be captured from the live hub page (browser task) or from WVDA, because the text fetch exposed labels but not their href targets for those items, and one guessed retail-list URL returned 404 in this research and is therefore deliberately not cited.

5.6.2 Hemp Registered Product List — verified, version-dated

  • URL (verbatim, opened directly): agriculture.wv.gov/hemp-registered-products — Accessed 2026-10-24. Format: paginated web table (A/B/C navigation in the fetch), not a CSV/XLSX/PDF download. No export button was visible in the text fetch. Copying/scraping the web table is the only reuse route found.
  • Version date shown on the list: “Registered Hemp Products v 07/20/2026” — same URL — Accessed 2026-10-24. (This is a July 2026 version stamp on a list accessed in October 2026; whether it is the latest version or a stale stamp is [VERIFY] — the hub page does not state an update frequency.)
  • Current version v 10/16/2026, last numbered row 2,421 — Daily Cellars / Honor Hemp Silly Goose 5mg D9 THC + 125mg CBD Beverage — Raspberry — 12oz — same URL — Accessed 2026-10-24.
  • Fields: row number, manufacturer name, full product name, brand. No registration number, THC mg beyond what is in the product name, manufacturer address, or registration-expiry date is shown in the extracted table.
  • Count caveat [VERIFY]: row 2,421 is the last numbered row in the current version (v 10/16/2026), but the table intersperses blank separator rows. The distinct-product count is therefore ≤2,421 and requires a non-blank-row count; do not publish the superseded last-row number as a product count as an exact distinct count without that recount. The list also mixes product types (CBD, delta-9 THC drinks/gummies, delta-8/THCP vapes and flower, topicals, pet chews, hemp milk, rolling papers) — product-type breakdowns are not provided by WVDA on this page.
  • Update frequency as stated: not stated on the list page or hub page — [UNAVAILABLE]. The version stamp (07/20/2026) is the only freshness signal found.
