  • USDA state/tribal plan list (opened directly): USDA AMS — State and Tribal Plan Review — Accessed 2026-10-24. WV status shown, updated 17 Feb 2026: “West Virginia | WV Approved Plan (pdf)*” where “*” = “Plan approved under the final rule which took effect March 22, 2021” — same URL — Accessed 2026-10-24.
  • What this proves: WV runs its own USDA-approved state plan; it is not a “USDA Hemp Producer License” state (contrast Hawaii, Iowa, Kansas, Mississippi, Missouri, Nebraska, New Hampshire, North Carolina, Ohio, Utah, Vermont, Wisconsin on the same page) — same URL — Accessed 2026-10-24.
  • What USDA does NOT provide for WV: no individual WV producer/licence list on this page. The “(pdf)” link is to the state plan document, not a licensee roster. The direct WV plan PDF URL/filename was not extracted in the text fetch — [VERIFY] before citing a plan-PDF link.
  • WVDA's own plan link: the industrial-hemp hub links “WVDA Approved Industrial Hemp Plan — Effective January 1, 2022” — WVDA Industrial Hemp hub — Accessed 2026-10-24. Its target PDF URL was not extracted — [VERIFY].
  • USDA NASS hemp production data (not a licence list, but the official acreage/value source — see Part 6.6): NASS Mountain State Reporter, Apr 2024 (PDF) and NASS Farm Facts, Apr 2024 (PDF) — Accessed 2026-10-24.
  • No USDA public WV producer search, CSV, or API was found — [UNAVAILABLE]. Anyone needing a WV grower roster must request it from WVDA; USDA is the wrong source for WV licensees by design.

5.8 WV Secretary of State — business entity search (not a cannabis licence source)

  • Primary search portal (verbatim from search result, with live data stamp): apps.wv.gov/SOS/BusinessEntitySearch — Accessed 2026-10-24. The search-result text for this portal states: “This service will enable you to view registration information on West Virginia corporations and other business types which file with the Secretary of State… you will receive entity names that match your search criteria along with entity type, city and status. Select ‘Details’ to view additional information including full address, registered number, entity creation date and registered agent contact information. There are no fees associated with viewing this information.” And: “Search Data Current as of Wednesday, October 21, 2026 12:00 AM” — same URL — Accessed 2026-10-24 (the 21 Oct 2026 stamp is the portal's own “data current as of” stamp as indexed; it is not a claim that this research was done on that date).
  • Help page (same portal): Business Entity Search Help — Accessed 2026-10-24. Certificates of Existence/Authorization: $10 each, credit-card payment, printable; Business & Licensing Division phone (304) 558-8000, toll-free (866) 767-8683 — same URL — Accessed 2026-10-24. (Official public contact numbers; no personal numbers reproduced.)
  • Second SOS search (secondary-reported, [VERIFY] before use): a secondary guide reports a second portal at apps.sos.wv.gov/business/corporations with Agent/Officer/Name modes, “%” wildcard, and 55-county advanced filters — Commenda (secondary) — Accessed 2026-10-24. The apps.wv.gov portal above is the one verified in the search-result text; the apps.sos.wv.gov advanced portal was not opened directly in this task.
  • SOS homepage: sos.wv.gov — Accessed 2026-10-24 (via wv.gov/agencies — Accessed 2026-10-24, which lists Secretary of State, phone 304-558-6000, website sos.wv.gov).
  • Format: interactive web search only. No bulk download, no CSV, no cannabis/hemp filter, no permit-status field. Search is by organisation name (and, on the second portal, by agent/officer). Results cap at 50+ (“Your search returned more than 50 records. Please refine your search”) — apps.wv.gov portal — Accessed 2026-10-24.
  • Fields available: business name, status, organisation type, control/registered number, city, full address (on Details), creation date, registered-agent contact — same URL — Accessed 2026-10-24.
  • Limitation — critical for Parts 4–5: SOS registration proves only that a business entity exists in WV. It does not prove an OMC permit, a WVDA hemp licence, or a hemp-product registration. Every SOS hit must be cross-checked against OMC Tables 13–15 or the WVDA hemp lists. A business that appears in SOS but not in OMC/WVDA lists is not a licensed cannabis/hemp business on that evidence alone.

5.9 Local (county/city/town) permit records

Finding: none found published. No county or city in WV was found in this research to publish a searchable cannabis/hemp permit list, CSV, or dashboard. OMC is the state permitter for medical cannabis; WVDA is the state registrar for hemp products. Local records, if they exist, would be zoning, building, or business-licence records held locally, not a cannabis-permit roster. Do not cite any local list for Charleston/Kanawha, Huntington/Cabell, Morgantown/Monongalia, Parkersburg/Wood, Wheeling/Ohio County, Martinsburg/Berkeley, Beckley/Raleigh, Fairmont/Marion, or Jefferson County from this report — all [UNAVAILABLE] as published permit datasets. Local zoning/bans are a Part 3 question and must be sourced from municipal codes, not inferred from state lists. Recommended follow-up: direct county-clerk / city-clerk requests for business-licence or zoning-permit extracts for the nine jurisdictions above; and a check of whether any publish a general business-licence search that could be filtered by the OMC/WVDA names (it will still not be a cannabis-specific source).

5.10 State Tax Department / Treasurer — revenue data sources (no dashboard found)

Finding: no WV State Tax Division cannabis sales/tax dashboard, CSV, or annual cannabis revenue table was found — [UNAVAILABLE]. The only official revenue-adjacent sources found are: OMC biennial sales chart (Metrc) — see Part 6.2 — OMC Biennial Report 2025 — Accessed 2026-10-24; WVDA hemp inspection/registration counts — see 5.6.5 — WVDA, 25 Feb 2025 — Accessed 2026-10-24; WV Treasurer designated-depositories list (PDF) — see Part 6.7 — WV Treasurer designated depositories (PDF) — Accessed 2026-10-24; secondary reporting of Treasurer statements on the ~$34 million Medical Cannabis Program Fund balance — see Part 6.4 — Marijuana Moment / Mountain State Spotlight (secondary) — Accessed 2026-10-24. Do not present a Tax Division URL as a cannabis data source in this report; none was verified. Part 2 must confirm the Tax Division's actual cannabis-tax publication (if any) separately.

Part 6 — Market Data

6.1 Headline market picture (official first)

Medical-only programme; no adult-use sales. First dispensary sales November 2021 (Trulieve, Morgantown, then Weston) — secondary trade press, dated 2021 — MJBizDaily, 2021 — Accessed 2026-10-24. The OMC biennial report's sales window starts November 2023; it does not restate the November 2021 launch date in the extracted text — OMC Biennial Report 2025 — Accessed 2026-10-24. Monthly medical sales have been roughly flat at ~$7–8.4 million per month in the official window (see 6.2). No official calendar-year total was found. Active patients ~35,400 at 31 Oct 2025, growth slowing (see 6.3). Official prices falling for flower since mid-2024 (see 6.5).

6.2 Medical sales by year — what official sources actually publish (and do not)

Official OMC position: OMC does NOT publish a calendar-year sales total in the 2025 Biennial Report. It publishes a chart (Figure 1: Total Sales Reported in Metrc) with only two prose data points. Any “annual sales” figure for WV from another source is therefore modelled, summed from a chart, or secondary — and must be labelled as such. Source: OMC Biennial Report 2025, Figure 1 — OMC Biennial Report 2025 — Accessed 2026-10-24. Window: 1 Nov 2023 – 31 Oct 2025. Data via Metrc, analysed via NCS Analytics — same URL — Accessed 2026-10-24. Only two prose sales values in the report: highest recorded sales in Metrc: $8,401,200.12, dated 1 August 2024; lowest recorded sales in Metrc: $6,954,344.81, dated 1 November 2023 — same URL — Accessed 2026-10-24. Caveat: the report stamps each value to the first of the month and does not state in prose whether each value is that month's total or a point-in-time Metrc snapshot. Secondary summaries read them as monthly totals (e.g. “August 2024: $8.4M”). That reading is plausible but not explicitly confirmed by the report prose — [VERIFY] against the Figure 1 chart/table if a monthly series is needed. Do not annualise by multiplying one month by 12 and call it an official annual total. No annual totals found: no 2021, 2022, 2023, 2024, or 2025 calendar-year sales total in the 2025 report's extracted text; no Tax Division annual cannabis-tax table found — [UNAVAILABLE] (see 5.10). Secondary monthly figures — marked secondary, do not present as official: August 2024: $8.39 million; October 2025: $8.09 million; December 2025: $7.97 million — reported via Dominion Post / MMJDaily summary — MMJDaily / Dominion Post (secondary) — Accessed 2026-10-24 (underlying source described as “The Transparency Project – the national data source used by the OMC,” which is not an OMC publication and was not verified). January/February 2026: $6.6 million and $4.07 million “as reported; the source does not say whether February is a complete month, so treat as provisional” — cannbus.org (secondary) — Accessed 2026-10-24 (provisional, do not use for a 2026 annual total). Annualised run-rate ~$97.1 million (Oct 2025 × 12) and “about $93 million for 2025 (not independently verified)” — same secondary URL — Accessed 2026-10-24. Both are calculations/estimates, not official totals. Early-market projections (historic, not actuals): 2021 sales $400,000–$500,000, rising to “as much as $6 million in 2022 and up to $40 million annually by 2025” (MJBiz Factbook projections, 2021) — MJBizDaily, 2021 — Accessed 2026-10-24. Do not use projections as sales history. Cannabist Company investor presentation (2023, secondary): WV estimated 2023 sales $60.8 million, estimated 2027 sales $100.1 million — Cannabist investor presentation (secondary, PDF) — Accessed 2026-10-24 (company estimate, not state data). Harvest volume (official, Metrc, same report): highest month: October 2024 — 25,657.85 pounds; lowest month: November 2023 — 10,137.56 pounds — OMC Biennial Report 2025 — Accessed 2026-10-24. Most harvests started: September 2025, followed by March 2025 (counts not given in prose) — same URL — Accessed 2026-10-24. What to write in the combined Run 1/Run 2 report for “sales by year”: state explicitly that no official calendar-year total was located, give the Nov 2023–Oct 2025 monthly range ($6.95M–$8.40M, with the two prose points above), and list secondary monthly/annualised figures only as secondary estimates. Inventing an annual total by summing chart pixels or secondary months would violate the sourcing rules.

6.3 Patient, caregiver, and practitioner counts (official)

All from OMC Biennial Report 2025 — OMC Biennial Report 2025 — Accessed 2026-10-24, unless noted.

  • Active patients, 31 Oct 2025: 35,394, “an estimated 19% increase since October 2023” — same URL — Accessed 2026-10-24. Internal discrepancy [VERIFY]: the monthly table in the same report lists 35,395 for Oct 2025 (see monthly series below) vs 35,394 in the Patients narrative. Difference = 1. Use 35,394 as the headline (narrative) and footnote the table value; do not silently correct either.
  • Applications received, 1 Nov 2023 – 31 Oct 2025: 26,549, broken down in the report as: 2023: 2,402; 2024: 14,732; 2025: 9,413 — same URL — Accessed 2026-10-24. (Note: 2,402 + 14,732 + 9,413 = 26,547, not 26,549 — a 2-application arithmetic discrepancy in the report as extracted [VERIFY]. The 2023 figure presumably covers only Nov–Dec 2023, but the report does not state that in the extracted text.)
  • Age breakdown (Oct 2025 active patients): 35–54 is largest at 15,887 patients (~45%), followed by 26–34 at ~17% — same URL — Accessed 2026-10-24.
  • Conditions (Oct 2025, active patients, counts sum to more than the patient total because patients may have multiple listings — do not sum to 35,394): severe chronic/intractable pain 21,910; PTSD 10,401; neuropathies 1,111; cancer 886; epilepsy 332; Crohn's disease 294; multiple sclerosis 176; damage to nervous tissue of spinal cord 81; intractable seizures 66; HIV/AIDS 61; Parkinson's disease 55; ALS 7; terminally ill 7; sickle cell anemia 4; Huntington's disease 3 — same URL — Accessed 2026-10-24.
  • Patients by county (Oct 2025, top counties): Kanawha 5,404 (~15%); Monongalia 2,410 (~7%); Cabell 2,044 (~6%); Harrison 1,990; Marion 1,692; Raleigh 1,670; Putnam 1,468; Wood 1,458; Berkeley 1,317 — same URL — Accessed 2026-10-24. (County counts are patient-residence counts, not dispensary counts — do not use them as a business-concentration measure in Part 3 without that caveat.)
  • Caregivers: 40 registered caregivers total — 18 active minor patients with 18 active caregivers, plus 22 active caregivers for adult patients — same URL — Accessed 2026-10-24.
  • Minor patients: 18 active — same URL — Accessed 2026-10-24.
  • Physicians: 123 registered physicians at 1 Nov 2025, plus ~26 telemedicine companies. Physician applications “are being accepted indefinitely and there is no limit on physician participation.” Physicians must complete a mandatory 4-hour course via TheAnswerPage and register with OMC — same URL — Accessed 2026-10-24.
  • Conflicting secondary patient figures — do not merge, list as conflicts: MPP patient-numbers table: WV 35,149, current through 3 Nov 2025, 1.99% of state population — MPP (secondary compilation) — Accessed 2026-10-24 (date 3 Nov 2025 is 3 days after OMC's 31 Oct 2025 count, yet lower by ~245 — [VERIFY] definition: active vs registered vs purchasers). 6.4 Revenue / fund balance (official + secondary — keep them separate)

Official tax/fee structure is a Part 2 question; for market context only: the biennial report and secondary summaries agree the programme is funded by a 10% tax/fees into the Medical Cannabis Program Fund, but no official fund-balance table was found in the 2025 Biennial Report's extracted text — OMC Biennial Report 2025 — Accessed 2026-10-24. Secondary cumulative revenue — marked secondary: “Since West Virginia's first medical cannabis dispensary opened in 2021, the state has collected nearly $34 million in taxes, licensing fees, and interest” and “not a single dollar has been spent” / funds “will remain unallocated until federal law changes” (Treasurer's office spokesperson Carrie Hodousek, via Mountain State Spotlight reporting) — Marijuana Moment / Mountain State Spotlight (secondary, Oct 2025-era reporting) — Accessed 2026-10-24 (underlying Treasurer statement was not retrieved as a primary document in this task). Same ~$34 million figure repeated by NORML summary of the same Spotlight reporting, with an illustrative split if distributed: ~$19M to Bureau for Public Health, ~$8M to Fight Substance Abuse Fund, ~$6M to Division of Justice and Community Services, $1.5M to law-enforcement training — NORML, 30 Oct 2025 (secondary) — Accessed 2026-10-24 (illustrative split, not a disbursement). A later secondary summary claims “over $38 million, in tax revenue and license fees, since inception” and that the House passed a bill 4 March 2026 allocating the fund (later vetoed by Governor Morrisey) and the Treasurer then released funds to Homeland Security and Health — WestVirginiaStateCannabis.org (secondary, non-official) — Accessed 2026-10-24 (conflicts with the ~$34M figure and with the “unspent” reporting — [VERIFY] against Treasurer/legislative records before any fund-balance figure is published in Part 2). WVSTEP policy paper (secondary, university-affiliated, 2026): “In total, ~$34 million has been raised but not disbursed… HB 5074, which was vetoed by the governor, allocated this funding” — WVSTEP (secondary, PDF) — Accessed 2026-10-24 (useful as a second witness to ~$34M, not a primary fund statement). Conclusion: no official, dated fund balance is established by this research. Part 2 must not publish “$34M” or “$38M” as an official balance without a Treasurer/State Auditor primary source. Enforcement aggregates from the same report (for context): 817 total inspections (799 physical + 18 initial/operational), 1,088 advertising requests reviewed, 817 employee background checks, 110 investigations, 692 product/strain submissions, 11 Notices of Violation, 10 Administrative Orders, $3,900 fines collected — OMC Biennial Report 2025 — Accessed 2026-10-24. Background-check approval (96% approved + 2% denied = 98%) leaves 2% unexplained in the report — [VERIFY].

6.6 Hemp product market figures (official first, secondary marked)

Official — USDA NASS (federal, state-level)

  • 2023 (latest NASS WV hemp release found): “Industrial hemp in the open: Planted area for West Virginia in 2023 for all utilizations totaled 24 acres, down 11 acres from 35 acres in 2022. Area harvested for all purposes in the open in 2023 totaled 23 acres, down 7 acres from 30 acres in 2022. Industrial floral hemp in the open: Median yield was 80 pounds per acre in 2023 and the median price was $75.00. The value of utilized floral hemp production grown in the open in 2023 totaled $139,000, down from $440,000 in 2022. Hemp under protection: In 2023 hemp growers used 11,465 square feet under protection for production, up from 5,967 square feet in 2022. Other data were withheld to avoid disclosing data for individual operations.” — NASS Mountain State Reporter, April 2024 (PDF) — Accessed 2026-10-24 (news-release index confirms “04/18/24: 2023 West Virginia Hemp Production” — NASS WV news releases — Accessed 2026-10-24).
  • Farm Facts table (same NASS programme, April 2024): WV floral hemp: planted 35 acres (2022) / 24 acres (2023); harvested 29 acres (2022) / 23 acres (2023) in the table vs 30 acres (2022) in the Reporter text above — 1-acre discrepancy for 2022 harvested area [VERIFY]; yield 220 lb/acre (2022) / 130 lb/acre (2023) in the table's units vs “median yield 80 lb/acre” in the Reporter text — different yield concepts (mean vs median) — do not merge; total production 6,000 lb (2022) / 3,000 lb (2023); value $440,000 (2022) / $139,000 (2023) — NASS Farm Facts, Apr 2024 (PDF) — Accessed 2026-10-24.
  • 2021–2022 (Farm Facts, April 2023): WV planted 65 acres (2021) / 35 acres (2022); harvested 43 / 29 acres; production 18,000 / 6,000 lb; value $2,040,000 (2021) / $440,000 (2022) — NASS Farm Facts, Apr 2023 (PDF) — Accessed 2026-10-24. Note the 2021 value ($2.04M) is an order of magnitude above 2022–2023; it is NASS-published, but the collapse 2021→2022 should be flagged as a market break, not smoothed.
  • No NASS WV hemp release for the 2024 crop was located — [UNAVAILABLE]. National 2024 hemp value ($445M, +40%) is reported secondarily — Cannabis Risk Manager (secondary) — Accessed 2026-10-24 — but no WV 2024 acreage/value split was found; do not allocate a WV share of the national total.
  • Suppression caveat: NASS withholds WV hemp data “to avoid disclosing data for individual operations” where grower counts are small — NASS Mountain State Reporter, Apr 2024 — Accessed 2026-10-24. This explains why grain/fibre/seed splits for WV are (D) withheld in the Farm Facts tables.

Official — WVDA (registration/enforcement counts, not sales value)

  • 1,063 registered hemp retailers; 1,707 hemp products registered (Feb 2025) — WVDA, 25 Feb 2025 — Accessed 2026-10-24 (see 5.6.5).
  • 2,421 last row number on the 16 October 2026 registered-products web list — WVDA Registered Products list — Accessed 2026-10-24 (see 5.6.2 count caveat).
  • No WVDA hemp sales value, retail-sales total, or excise-revenue total was found — [UNAVAILABLE]. WVDA publishes registrations and inspections, not market value. Any WV hemp “market size” in dollars is secondary/estimated — do not publish one as official.
  • Historical WVDA grower/acreage (official press release): 24 growers (2017) → 305 growers (2020); 2,531 acres planned (2020) — WVDA press release — Accessed 2026-10-24. Secondary summaries add: 158 licences issued in 2019 with 2,531 acres registered vs 46 licences / 155 acres in 2018; 2,760 acres registered in 2020; 232 applications for 2021 — Hemp CBD Business Plans (secondary) — Accessed 2026-10-24 (the 2019 “649.5 acres grown” vs “2,531 acres registered” distinction — registered vs actually grown — is [VERIFY] and must not be conflated).

Secondary — do not use as market size

“Almost all the hemp growers are growing plants for CBD extraction” (Vote Hemp-derived summary) — Hemp CBD Business Plans (secondary, undated in excerpt) — Accessed 2026-10-24. No credible secondary WV hemp/CBD retail-sales total, price series, or store-count series was found that is dated, sourced, and WV-specific. Do not import a national CBD market figure as a WV figure.

6.7 Cannabis-friendly banks and credit unions in West Virginia

There is no official OMC, Treasurer, or Division of Financial Institutions list of “cannabis-friendly banks” for WV businesses found in this research — [UNAVAILABLE] as an official list. The institutions below are the only ones with verifiable WV-specific cannabis-banking evidence. National vendors marketing into WV are listed separately and must not be presented as WV-chartered banks.

WV-chartered / WV-based — verified

  • Element Federal Credit Union (Charleston, WV) — the central WV cannabis-banking institution. Its own site: “we're the only credit union in the state of West Virginia to provide financial services to cannabis-based businesses… Learn more about our services for growers, processors, and dispensaries… Learn about CanPay, the only debit payment app that lets you pay for your purchases at cannabis retailers” — Element FCU — Green Banking — Accessed 2026-10-24 (institution's own claim; “only” is its marketing claim, not a regulator's finding — [VERIFY] against other WV credit unions before repeating “only” as fact). State-fund holder (secondary reporting of Treasurer process): “Traditional banks, wary of federal repercussions, refused to handle the state's cannabis funds when the program launched… Element Federal Credit Union, based in Charleston, accepted the challenge and became the official holder of the state's medical cannabis revenue” and CEO Linda Bodie quoted: “It's up to the individual bank if they want to take that risk. We obviously took the risk because it's important for the state and the industry to function.” — Cannabis Risk Manager (secondary) — Accessed 2026-10-24. Legislative fix (secondary legal history): HB 2538 (2019) gave the Treasurer authority to competitively bid medical-cannabis banking services after the Treasurer's vendors refused to process OMC fees in 2018; bidders included Element FCU, MVB Financial, and others — JD Supra (secondary, 2019-era) — Accessed 2026-10-24. Treasurer designated-depositories list (official PDF) includes Element Federal Credit Union among designated state depositories — WV Treasurer designated depositories (PDF) — Accessed 2026-10-24. Parsing caveat [VERIFY]: in the extracted list, the footnote “*Medical Cannabis only” appears adjacent to “Peoples Bank,” not Element, in the text flow. Do not attribute that footnote to Element without checking the PDF layout. The list proves Element is a designated depository; it does not by itself prove a retail cannabis-banking programme — Element's own site (above) is the evidence for that.
  • Other WV banks on the Treasurer's designated-depositories list (WesBanco, United Bank, City National Bank, MVB Bank, Huntington National Bank, PNC, Truist, etc. — full list at the Treasurer PDF above — Accessed 2026-10-24) are designated state depositories for state funds generally. They are not evidence of cannabis-business banking. Do not list them as “cannabis-friendly” on the basis of that PDF alone. MVB Bank/MVB Financial appears as a 2019 bidder for OMC banking services (secondary, above) but no current MVB cannabis-banking programme page for WV was verified — [VERIFY].

National / out-of-state vendors marketing WV services — not WV banks

  • Safe Harbor Financial (address shown as Golden, CO; phone 888-873-1498 on its WV page): “We work with cannabis related businesses in the state of West Virginia including dispensaries, cultivators, manufacturers, labs, distributors, and ancillary businesses… Safe Harbor was one of the first to build a fully compliant cannabis banking program [since 2015]” — Safe Harbor Financial — West Virginia (vendor marketing) — Accessed 2026-10-24 (not a WV-chartered institution, not an official list).
  • First Federal Bank (specialty banking, “all 50 states”): “Whether you touch the plant or work as an ancillary business, we are a transparent cannabis, hemp, and CBD friendly bank… Serving Cannabis, Hemp, and CBD related businesses in all 50 states” — First Federal Bank — Specialty Banking (vendor marketing) — Accessed 2026-10-24 (no WV branch or WV-specific programme evidenced on the page excerpt).
  • Community Choice Credit Union / Salal Credit Union pages returned in search are Michigan/Washington institutions — Community Choice CU (MI) and Salal CU (WA) — Accessed 2026-10-24. Exclude them from any WV list; they appeared only because the search was not state-filtered.
  • CanPay: mentioned only as Element FCU's debit-payment app (above). No separate CanPay WV merchant list was verified — [VERIFY] before listing CanPay acceptance by store.

What a WV business should actually do (practical note, not a recommendation of a specific bank): the only institution with both (a) its own WV cannabis-banking claim and (b) secondary evidence of holding state medical-cannabis funds is Element FCU. Any other WV bank/credit union's cannabis policy must be confirmed directly with that institution; absence from this report means “not verified,” not “does not bank cannabis.”

Uncertain, conflicting or unavailable items are flagged [VERIFY] or [UNAVAILABLE]. Nothing here presents a proposed bill as law.

Federal baseline applied as fixed context (not re-researched): state-licensed medical marijuana and FDA-approved products moved to Schedule III effective 28 April 2026 (91 FR 22714); all adult-use marijuana remains Schedule I; IRS 280E is generally lifted for Schedule III medical activity, adult-use remains fully subject to 280E; hemp PL 119-37 §781 takes effect 12 November 2026 but PL 119-103 §2019 limits it until 11 December 2026, after which the full total-THC hemp definition and the 0.4 mg total THC per-container cap apply; USDA enforcement of DEA-registered lab testing for hemp is delayed to 31 December 2026; the federal medical marijuana appropriations rider runs through 11 December 2026; there is no federal cannabis banking law. No contradiction to that baseline was found in West Virginia law in this run. West Virginia’s medical programme is state-licensed, so Schedule III applies to its growers, processors and dispensaries for federal purposes; that does not create any WV adult-use right, authorise interstate shipment, or settle WV’s state 280E treatment.

Part 7 — Hemp Licences

7.1 State plan — RESOLVED (Run 1 gap G-47)

West Virginia runs its own USDA-approved state plan; it is NOT a USDA-producer-licence state. The USDA Agricultural Marketing Service state-and-tribal-plan list, updated 17 February 2026, lists “West Virginia | WV Approved Plan (pdf)*”, where * means approved under the final rule effective 22 March 2021. Source: USDA AMS, State and Tribal Plan Review — Accessed 2026-10-24. Correction in audit: the 1 January 2022 date does NOT attach to the USDA plan. In WVDA's older hemp/kratom guide it attaches to the WV Grown registration-cap rule (from 1 January 2022, a $500 cap in lieu of $1,000 for WV-grown/harvested/manufactured + WV Grown products). Do not cite 1 January 2022 as a plan-effective date. The direct plan PDF URL/filename was not extracted in this pass [VERIFY] (gap R2-7.09). USDA approved the WV plan in April 2020, confirmed by the WVDA April 2020 archive (certification of approval received; no specific April day-date stated in the archive text fetched). WVDA's own announcement confirms the plan route: WVDA, USDA approves West Virginia's industrial hemp plan — Accessed 2026-10-24.

§19-12E-5(f) contains a USDA-licence-recognition alternative. How, or whether, that alternative is used while a state plan is approved was not located [VERIFY] (gap R2-7.10). Do not treat it as a second, parallel licensing route without a WVDA source.

Contradiction C-1 — fees. The official form / 61 CSR 29 fees above ($100/parcel + $100 + $5/acre; $50 alteration) are adopted. A secondary claim of a $500 cultivation base fee + $50 renewal fee is not adopted. Likewise, secondary claims of a 10-year drug-felony bar, a September–October application window, and a “local veto” over hemp cultivation were not verified in official excerpts [VERIFY] — do not quote them as law. The WVDA hub page also carries stale “Jan 1 2021 – Dec 31 2021” duration text; do not quote that as the current term.

7.3 Processor licence

Processor licensing uses the combined cultivation/processor application above. The processor fee is $500 per processing location/facility, invoiced on approval; adding a site after the application period costs the $50 alteration fee + $500. Source: official WVDA form / 61 CSR 29 — Accessed 2026-10-24. Processor-specific facility, testing, disposal and reporting requirements beyond the form (Attachment B) were not recovered in full [VERIFY] against the full 61 CSR 29 (gap R2-7.17). A processor licence alone does not authorise hemp-derived cannabinoid manufacture or sale — that is §19-12E-12 (see §7.5).

7.4 “Handler” licence — NOT FOUND as a distinct type

No distinct “handler” licence type was located [UNAVAILABLE] / [VERIFY] (gap R2-7.06). “Handle” in §19-12E-4(b) is a verb in the cultivation licence's scope, not a separate licence class. Do not invent a handler licence. This is distinct from the §19-12E-12 manufacturer / processor / distributor / retailer permits in §7.5.

7.5 Hemp-derived cannabinoid permits (§19-12E-12)

“The commissioner may issue manufacturer, processor, distributor, and retailer permits” for hemp-derived cannabinoid products; anyone manufacturing, processing, distributing, offering for sale or selling such products in West Virginia must hold a permit and be authorised to do business in West Virginia. — §19-12E-12(d)(1), code.wvlegislature.gov/19-12E-12 (PDF) — Accessed 2026-10-24 (Run 1 verbatim, carried forward).

Distinct dollar permit fee: NOT located. §19-12E-12(e)(4) provides only that fees be “as commensurate with need” [VERIFY] — check the current WVDA manufacturer form and 61 CSR 30 §§4–5 in full (gap R2-7.01). Do not substitute kratom fees: current §19-12F-8 kratom permit fees are $1,500 application + $300 annual; the older SB 220 engrossed text (an earlier engrossed draft figure covering “kratom and hemp-derived”) is superseded and must not be quoted as current hemp fees. Source: code.wvlegislature.gov, Ch. 19 Art. 12F — Accessed 2026-10-24.

Contradiction C-2 — permit vs “no licence” tension (analysis only). §19-12E-5(g)(1) says a person “need not obtain a license to possess, handle, transport, or sell hemp products or extracts, including one or more hemp-derived cannabinoids, including CBD”, while §19-12E-12(d)(1) requires a permit for hemp-derived cannabinoid products. The plausible reading is that the specific, later regime (SB 220 (2023) §19-12E-12 + 61 CSR 30) governs intoxicating hemp-derived cannabinoid products and the general 2019 (g)(1) rule governs ordinary hemp products/extracts — but that is analysis, not a WVDA or Attorney General reconciliation. No such reconciliation was located [UNAVAILABLE] (gap R2-7.03).

7.6 Product registration — 61 CSR 30 §4

FieldRule
Who must register§4.1: all hemp/kratom products for distribution/sale in WV, including out-of-state and foreign-made products, must be registered annually.
Application contents§4.2: registrant name/address; label name/address if different; product name; origin of raw material; full label copy; fee.
Expiry / deadline§4.3 expires 31 December regardless of receipt date; §4.4 due by 1 January each year.
Fees§4.5: $200 per product; §4.5.a $100 per product if WV-grown/harvested/manufactured + WV Grown; §4.5.b renewal $200 per product; caps §4.5.c $1,000 per registrant (manufactured + sold in WV), §4.5.d $500 WV Grown, §4.5.e $2,000 white-label (WV vendor, WV-grown); §4.5.f same composition/concentration in different net quantities = one product; foreign check fee $35.
FormManufacturer registration form, headed 1 Jan – 31 Dec 2026, product types Fiber, Flower, Seed, Cosmetic, Oil, Edible, Vape, Other: agriculture.wv.gov — Manufacturer Registration Form (spelling as returned) — Accessed 2026-10-24.

§4 substance is confirmed by the WVDA hemp-products hub and the Secretary of State filing for 61 CSR 30 (DocId 58879, effective 20 April 2026); the §4 fees, including the §4.5.b renewal fee, are confirmed by the final rule text (DocId 58879).

7.7 Retailer / distributor / seller registration — 61 CSR 30 §5

FieldRule
Who must registerAll retail facilities, including online domains/websites, must register. Application: store name/address (or online indication), responsible party name/home address, Tax Division Letter of Good Standing, SSN/FEIN/ITIN.
Fee / term$100 with the application (the rule text does not say “per establishment”; that phrasing is WVDA's summary), January–December, expires 31 December. Manufacturer-only sellers are exempt from the fee but not from registration. No “nontransferable” term was located in 61 CSR 30 §5 of the final rule (DocId 58879) — do not attribute nontransferability to §5 (medical-permit nontransferability is statutory, §16A-6-3(b), a different regime). Certificate must be displayed at all times.
Distributor carve-out§5.9: a distributor that does not itself sell retail to consumers is not required to register under §5. Restaurants selling take-home products are not exempt.
FormRegistration to Distribute and Sell Hemp Products (2026 heading, azure mirror) — Accessed 2026-10-24.

Section numbering is from Secretary of State excerpts [VERIFY]. Contradiction C-6: Secretary of State excerpts for 61 CSR 30 differ between document versions on enforcement consequences (15 business days vs 14 days to register/cure; 1-year vs 5-year periods; $500 / $1,000 / $5,000 penalties and embargo/destruction language in different combinations). Report the conflict; do not merge versions (gap R2-7.05).

7.8 §19-12E-12(d)(5)/(d)(7) lists — PARTLY resolved

The lists exist as WVDA hub labels and the registered-products list is a live web table (Run 1 §5.6). 61 CSR 30 §5.10 is a revocation provision, but it also contains publication sentences for an approved-products list and a revocation/non-renewal list, both public on agriculture.wv.gov (final rule, DocId 58879; DocId 59360 is a later proposed-amendment notice, not the effective rule). Direct hrefs/file URLs for the retail-sellers list, a permit-holders list as a distinct document, the allowable-cannabinoids list and the contaminants list were still not recovered [VERIFY] (gap R2-7.02). The hub itself labels these lists (Hemp Registered Product List, Hemp Registered Retail Sellers List, Current List of Registerable (Allowable) Hemp Cannabinoids, List of Contaminents [sic], Retail Facilities Under Summary Suspension), but the text fetch did not expose their hrefs. One URL guessed in Run 1 returned a 404 and is deliberately not cited again. WVDA hemp-products hub: agriculture.wv.gov — Hemp Products — Accessed 2026-10-24 (hub opened in the audit; hub labels confirmed, direct list hrefs still [VERIFY]). Public contact, official: hempproducts@wvda.us, phone 304-558-2227 — same source.

7.9 61 CSR 30 — status: in effect from 20 April 2026

61 CSR 29 §61-29-3 was located via Justia; 61 CSR 30 §§4, 5, 11, 13 were located in part via Cornell / Secretary of State excerpts. Secretary of State document IDs found by search on 2026-10-24: 58879 (20 April 2026 filing, 61 CSR 30 text); 59360 (Legislative Rule-Making Review Committee notice; hearing/comment ended 30 July 2026, comments received: yes; summary covers permitting, registration, labelling, displays, marketing, advertising, handling, transport, inspection, testing, enforcement, embargo; fiscal note: no general-revenue impact, special revenue to WVDA); 59361 (31 July 2026 WVDA letter on enforcement coordination). Audit correction: DocId 58879 is the adoption filing for the authorised legislative rule, authorised by HB 4265, §64-9-1, passed 14 March 2026, effective 20 April 2026, terminating 1 August 2031 — 61 CSR 30 is therefore presented as in effect, not [VERIFY]. DocIds 59360 (LRMRC notice) and 59361 (31 July 2026 WVDA enforcement letter) are separate later filings and must not be merged with DocId 58879; the C-6 enforcement-version pinpoints (15 vs 14 days, etc.) therefore stay flagged, with DocId 58879 as the controlling text (Contradiction C-7 resolved for status, gap R2-7.04 narrowed). Run 1 gap G-34 is therefore further narrowed. Full 61 CSR 29 text is likewise [VERIFY] (gap R2-7.04).

7.10 THCA — no THCA-specific statute; WVDA list entry only

No West Virginia THCA-specific statute was located: a search for “THCA” across the full §19-12E-12 text returns zero matches, and §19-12E-12(c)(7) lists only delta-9 (≤0.3% dry weight), delta-8, delta-10, HHC, THCP and THCV. Separately, WVDA's registrable-cannabinoids list (an official WVDA page, not statute) lists “Delta-9-tetrahydrocannabinolic acid A (THCA-A) <0.3% Total THC” — do not conflate that list entry with a statutory rule. The statute caps delta-9 at a total concentration of not more than 0.3% on a dry-weight basis; it does not codify a Δ9 + THCA×0.877 total-THC formula in §19-12E-12 (that formula appears in WVDA testing materials and in 61 CSR 30 §2.34, not in the statute). Secondary sources conflict sharply with each other and none is adopted: one claims THCA is “effectively limited to 0.3% total THC”; another claims it is allowed if delta-9 is <0.3%; a third claims selling it is illegal but cites no WV statute and contradicts §19-12E-12(c)(7); a fourth calls it “legally uncertain” but misstates SB 546 by omitting the lawful-channel exemption. The closest secondary reading (Marijuana Herald, November 2025) matches Run 1's reading but remains secondary. Do NOT state THCA flower is “legal” or “illegal” in WV as settled. After 11 December 2026 the federal total-THC definition would on its face capture high-THCA flower, but WV enforcement of that is [UNAVAILABLE] (gap R2-7.11). Contradiction C-4 records the secondary conflict.

7.11 Hemp beverages — no statutory cap or licence; §5.1 venue registration only

No WV beverage-specific THC cap or standalone beverage licence exists in §19-12E-12 (the word “beverage” occurs there only inside “Alcohol Beverage Control Administration Commissioner”). BUT 61 CSR 30 §5.1 is beverage-specific on registration: restaurants and other venues holding a WVABCA permit to sell beverages containing hemp must register with WVDA for a hemp retailer permit, and WVDA's July 2026 comment letter states it will amend the agency-approved rule to clarify that. Tension flagged: this sits against the hub's restaurant exclusion for on-site consumption of hemp foods/drinks — reconcile the two before publishing a beverage rule as settled. A secondary regional survey (CSG South, last checked May 2026) lists WV as “Regulated” (WVDA + ABCA enforcement; 21+; 0.3%; testing/labelling/child-safe packaging + Commissioner approval; §19-12E-12 permits; 11% tax) — consistent with the general framework but not a beverage statute. Registration practice proves beverages are registered (a 5 mg D9 “Silly Goose” drink appears on the WVDA products list, Run 1 §5.6 — row number not re-verified in this audit [VERIFY]) — that is not a potency rule. After 11 December 2026 the federal 0.4 mg/container cap would on its face capture most intoxicating hemp drinks, including that 5 mg drink; WV enforcement is [UNAVAILABLE] (gap R2-7.12).

7.12 No WV response to PL 119-37 / PL 119-103 — [UNAVAILABLE]

Still none found (gap R2-7.13, carrying Run 1 G-40). Two leads that are not a response: (1) WDTV news, December 2025 (secondary), quoting Del. Kayla Young warning of danger, lost revenue and black-market risk — a legislator comment in news, not WV policy. (2) SB 808 (2026) — Pending, NOT law: introduced 6 February 2026 (Sens. Takubo, Clements, Phillips); official Bill Status: Pending, Senate Health and Human Resources, last action 02/06/26 (a Finance referral is not shown in the status table; gap R2-7.14 resolved for status). It would have created Article 19-40 “Consumable Hemp Products” (ABCA as Board; federal hemp definition 7 U.S.C. §1639o; smokable hemp products banned; synthetic/chemically-modified cannabinoids banned; minor-appealing marketing banned; tax 80% General Revenue / 20% local; first violation civil penalty ≤$5,000, knowing sale of prohibited products misdemeanour ≤$10,000; local preemption except zoning/time-place-manner; effective 1 July 2026, licensing/tax enforced 1 January 2027; ABCA fiscal note cites a proposed additional 10% tax extrapolated from current 11% collections). It must not be presented as a WV response or as current law (Contradiction C-8). The federal HEMP Act (Rep. Griffith, R-W.Va. / Veasey) is a federal proposal only (5 mg/serving, 30 mg/package defaults) — not WV law.

7.13 Hemp licence counts — post-2020 grower count still unresolved

Latest official grower count remains 305 in 2020 (WVDA press release, 1 September 2020; 24 in 2017; 2,531 = planned acres for 2020 permittees, not harvested acres). Source: WVDA, Industrial hemp application period opens — Accessed 2026-10-24. A secondary 8 August 2024 claim (3 processing + 8 cultivating/processing + 44 cultivating licences; October 2024: 1,266 retailers) conflicts with the official February 2025 figure of 1,063 retailers — do not merge (Contradiction C-10, gap R2-7.08 / Run 1 G-08 carried). The official February 2025 anchor is 1,063 registered hemp retailers / 1,707 registered hemp products: WVDA, Interagency collaboration increases hemp product safety (25 Feb 2025) — Accessed 2026-10-24. The registered-products list row count and date are [VERIFY]: the list page itself was not opened in this audit, so no last-row number or date is carried as verified, and a row count would not in any case be a distinct-product count, since same-composition products in different net quantities count as one product (gap R2-7.07, carrying Run 1 G-07).

7.14 Delta-8 / delta-10 / HHC / THCP / THCV — Run 1 reading adopted

§19-12E-12(c)(7) lists delta-9 (≤0.3% dry weight), delta-8, delta-10, HHC, THCP and THCV as regulated hemp-derived cannabinoids — naturally occurring, non-synthetic only, permit + approved label + 21+ required (Run 1, verified). Measurement tension flagged in audit: §19-12E-3(g) still reads “no greater than 0.3% THC, or the THC concentration for hemp defined in 7 U.S.C. §5940, whichever is greater” (a stale 2014 Farm Bill pilot cross-reference), while 61 CSR 30 §2.15 defines hemp as not more than 0.3% delta-9 THC on a dry-weight basis and §2.34 defines THC/Total THC as delta-9 plus 0.877 × THCA. Do not quote §19-12E-3(g) alone as the operative measurement rule. Contradiction C-3: secondary blogs claiming these are banned via SB 546 omit SB 546's lawful-channel exemption and are not adopted. Source: §19-12E-12 (PDF) — Accessed 2026-10-24.

The only market activity evidenced is a confidential multi-dispensary business package in Charleston and a single Clarksburg hemp-processing lease, both unverified as to current availability. That is a finding about the sources, not proof that no off-market transaction exists.

Part 9 — Regulators and History

9.1 Every state agency regulating cannabis / hemp

9.1.1 Office of Medical Cannabis (OMC) — the medical programme regulator

Parentage — confirmed current: OMC's own homepage states: “The West Virginia Office of Medical Cannabis (OMC) is dedicated to ensuring a safe, secure, and regulated program… As a division under the Department of Health, our mission is to protect the health and safety of West Virginians…” Source: omc.wv.gov — Accessed 2026-10-24. This is consistent with Run 1 (OMC now in the West Virginia Department of Health, Bureau for Public Health, not DHHR, after HB 2006 (2023) dissolved DHHR effective 1 January 2024). Stale-naming caution [VERIFY]: an OMC application roadmap document still carries the old letterhead “West Virginia Department of Health and Human Resources Bureau for Public Health Office of Medical Cannabis” — omc.wv.gov/media/24 — Accessed 2026-10-24. Do not cite that letterhead as current parentage; the live homepage (Department of Health) controls (Contradiction R2-C-10, see Combined Notes).

Public contact (official, repeatedly shown): West Virginia Department of Health, Bureau for Public Health, Office of Medical Cannabis, 350 Capitol Street, Room 523, Charleston, WV 25301; phone 304-356-5090; fax 304-558-0035; email medcanwv@wv.gov; website omc.wv.gov. Source: OMC roadmap document — Accessed 2026-10-24. Role (Run 1, carried forward unchanged): permitting/oversight of growers, processors, dispensaries and laboratories under Chapter 16A; patient/caregiver cards; seed-to-sale electronic tracking under §16A-7-1 (statute does not name Metrc; Metrc is the vendor, Run 1). Caps carried forward unchanged: ≤10 growers, ≤10 processors, ≤100 dispensaries; applications closed 18 February 2020 except laboratories.

9.1.2 Medical Cannabis Advisory Board (MCAB) — statutory advisory board, now verified in statute

“The Medical Cannabis Advisory Board is established within the bureau” — §16A-11-1(a), code.wvlegislature.gov/16A-11-1 — Accessed 2026-10-24.

Membership (§16A-11-1(a), same source): (1) the commissioner or designee (who chairs, §16A-11-1(c)); (2) the Superintendent of the West Virginia State Police or designee; (3) four physicians appointed by the State Medical Association — Family Practice/Neurologist/General Practitioner, Pain Management, Oncologist/Palliative Care, Psychiatrist; (4) two physicians appointed by the West Virginia Osteopathic Association; (5) one pharmacist designated by the Board of Pharmacy; (6) one pharmacologist with cannabis science experience, appointed by the Governor; (7) one horticulturalist designated by the Commissioner of Agriculture; (8) one member designated by the West Virginia Association of Alcoholism and Drug Counselors; (9) an attorney knowledgeable about medical cannabis laws; (10) one member appointed by the West Virginia Prosecuting Attorneys Institute; (11) one member appointed by the Governor who is a patient, family/household member of a patient, or patient advocate. Terms are four years or until a successor has been appointed and qualified, but no longer than six months beyond the four-year period (§16A-11-1(b)); the commissioner or a designee chairs (§16A-11-1(c)) — the State Health Officer is not named as chair in §16A-11-1, so do not substitute that title for “commissioner or designee.” The two osteopathic seats were added by SB 1037; the original SB 386 board was smaller.

First meeting (official notice): Wednesday 16 August 2017, 1:00–3:00 p.m., University of Charleston, Erma Byrd Gallery, Charleston, WV; chaired by Dr. Rahul Gupta, then Commissioner/State Health Officer, Bureau for Public Health; open to the public. Source: DHHR notice (archived) — Accessed 2026-10-24. Parts 9–10 check: no first-meeting record was located on the current OMC board page (omc.wv.gov/board); the date rests on the archived DHHR notice as cited — [VERIFY] against OMC minutes before verbatim quotation. Recent meeting (as cited in the original — NOT located on the OMC board page in the Parts 9–10 check, which shows “Upcoming Meetings: MCAB Full Board Meeting TBD,” and the direct document URL cited in the original was not re-opened in that check): agenda 26 September 2025, 12:00–1:00 p.m., Google Meet: roll call/quorum, approval of 26 June 2025 minutes, public comments, introduction of new State Health Officer Mark McDaniel, DO, FAAFP, introduction of appointee Jeremy Hustead (WV Association of Addiction and Prevention Professionals, WVAAPP), OMC updates from Interim Director Lee Orr, programme data, new product approval process, MCAB By-Laws update, old business: reciprocity, next meeting scheduled 18 December 2025. Source: OMC, MCAB agenda 26 Sept 2025 (as cited in the original; direct document not re-opened in the Parts 9–10 check) — Accessed 2026-10-24. Current full roster [VERIFY] (gap G-9.1): no complete current roster page was opened; the 2025 agenda names above are NOT verified by the Parts 9–10 check and must not be relied on until the agenda document is re-opened. Verified anchor instead (Parts 9–10 check): the 19 December 2024 MCAB agenda lists Lee Orr as Interim Director and Matthew Q. Christiansen, MD, MPH as Chair/State Health Officer, with the next regular meeting then set for 20 March 2025. Do not reconstruct a full roster from the 2017 board. Historical note (secondary, Frost Brown Todd): in December 2017 the Bureau for Public Health announced proposed rules 64 CSR 109–113 and a proposed six-region map for licence allocation, with MCAB comment ending 12 January 2018 — Frost Brown Todd — Accessed 2026-10-24. That six-region proposal is historical; Run 1 found no current regional quota in Chapter 16A text opened.

9.1.3 West Virginia Department of Agriculture (WVDA) — hemp regulator

Website: agriculture.wv.gov — Accessed 2026-10-24. Hemp products public contact (official WVDA page): hempproducts@wvda.us; phone 304-558-2227. WVDA main address on its forms: 1900 Kanawha Blvd. East, Charleston, WV 25305-0170; main phone 304-558-3550. Source: WVDA — Accessed 2026-10-24. Role (Run 1, carried forward): hemp cultivation/processing licensing under Chapter 19, Article 12E (Industrial Hemp Development Act) and hemp-derived cannabinoid manufacturer/processor/distributor/retailer permits and product registration under §19-12E-12(d)(1) — the Commissioner of Agriculture issues those permits. WVDA's Hemp and Kratom Products Guide states: “All manufacturers of hemp and kratom products, excluding hemp fiber products, available for distribution in West Virginia shall register annually with the West Virginia Department of Agriculture. This includes products manufactured in West Virginia, another state, or another country” and “the level of total THC in industrial hemp and any hemp product (Cannabis sativa L.) must be no greater than 0.3 percent”. Source: WVDA Hemp and Kratom Products Guide — Accessed 2026-10-24. Volume (official WVDA, February 2025 — carried from Run 1, unchanged): 1,063 registered hemp retailers / 1,707 registered hemp products — see §7.13.

9.1.4 West Virginia State Tax Division / State Tax Department — tax administrator

Website: tax.wv.gov — Accessed 2026-10-24. Public contact (official Tax Division pages): Tax Information and Assistance (304) 558-3333 or (800) 982-8297; email TaxHelp@WV.Gov [VERIFY] — not confirmed on tax pages opened in the Parts 9–10 check (the Taxpayer Advocate address found there is TaxpayerAdvocate@WV.Gov, a different address); address 1001 Lee Street East, Charleston, WV 25301. State agency directory lists “State Tax Department | (304) 558-8683 | https://tax.wv.gov”. Source: WV business agency directory — Accessed 2026-10-24. The two phone numbers are for different Tax Division functions; the 558-3333 / 800-982-8297 pair is the published taxpayer-assistance pair. Role (Run 1, carried forward): administers the 10% medical cannabis privilege tax (§16A-9-1(b)) and the 11% hemp-derived cannabinoid privilege tax (§19-12E-12(i)), plus business registration / letters of good standing required for WVDA hemp registration. No dedicated “cannabis tax” landing page was opened in this pass [VERIFY] — do not cite a secondary “Cannabis Dispensary Tax (CDT)” page as official. Quarterly due dates confirmed in statute: both the 10% medical privilege tax (§16A-9-1(d)) and the 11% hemp tax (§19-12E-12(i)) are due 20 January / 20 April / 20 July / 20 October, by EFT with e-filing, with a final return within 90 days of ceasing (gap G-9.6 resolved for dates). Hemp-tax distribution, §19-12E-12(i)(8)(A), after the Tax Commissioner fee: 65% Agriculture Fees Fund / 5% Fight Substance Abuse Fund (§60A-9-8) / 30% ABCA Enforcement Fund (§60-7-13) — this resolves Contradiction C-5; the “15%” line was an amendment-context excerpt and is not repeated. Medical proceeds go to the Medical Cannabis Program Fund (§16A-9-1(g)); medical cannabis is sales/use-tax exempt and no county/municipal special-district excise or sales tax applies (§16A-9-1(h)). Note §16A-9-1(i)(2) still names the “Secretary of Health and Human Resources” (stale post-reorganisation text).

9.1.5 West Virginia State Police — background checks and general enforcement

Website: wvsp.gov — Accessed 2026-10-24. Public contact (official WVSP pages): 725 Jefferson Road, South Charleston, WV 25309; telephone 304-746-2100. Source: WVSP Legal — Accessed 2026-10-24. Statutory role (official Code text): medical cannabis organisation applicants “shall submit fingerprints of principals, financial backers, operators and employees to the West Virginia State Police for the purpose of obtaining criminal history record checks and the West Virginia State Police or its authorized agent shall submit the fingerprints to the Federal Bureau of Investigation” — §16A-6-2, via code.wvlegislature.gov/16A-6 — Accessed 2026-10-24. The State Police Superintendent (or designee) also sits on MCAB (§16A-11-1(a)(2)). General Chapter 60A enforcement is a State Police / local law-enforcement function; no cannabis-specific State Police unit was verified [VERIFY].

9.1.6 Alcohol Beverage Control Administration (ABCA) — hemp retail enforcement only, NOT the hemp permit issuer

Website: abca.wv.gov — Accessed 2026-10-24. Public contact (official ABCA pages): WV Alcohol Beverage Control Administration, 900 Pennsylvania Ave., 4th Floor, Charleston, WV 25302; phone 304-356-5500 (a general ABCA number, 800-642-8208, is also listed on the Revenue agencies page); licensing email abca.licensing@wv.gov. Sources: ABCA Code & Rules and ABCA new applications — Accessed 2026-10-24. ABCA sits in the Department of Revenue, alongside the State Tax Division — WV Department of Revenue agencies — Accessed 2026-10-24. Statutory hemp role — verified, narrow: §19-12E-12(l)(2): “Any certified law-enforcement officer in this state may enforce the criminal provisions of this section, and enforcement agents of the Alcohol Beverage Control Administration Commissioner may enforce the administrative retailer provisions of this section as relating to retail sales.” The Commissioner of Agriculture must provide requisite training, §19-12E-12(l)(3) (“Commissioner” = Commissioner of Agriculture, §19-12E-12(c)(2)). Source: §19-12E-12 (PDF) — Accessed 2026-10-24. Interagency memoranda [VERIFY]: §19-12E-12(j) (joint-report duty at (j)(1)(D)) requires the Agriculture Commissioner, ABCA Commissioner and Tax Commissioner to assist each other, train/cooperate with law enforcement on a statewide plan, confer monthly, and jointly compile a report to the Joint Committee on Government and Finance on or before 1 January 2025. Whether that report was filed was not located — flag [VERIFY], do not assume it exists (gap G-9.4).

Contradiction R2-C-5 — secondary ABCA-permitting claim, NOT adopted. A 2025 secondary article asserts that as of July 2025 WV requires ABCA permits for any hemp/kratom manufacturing/distribution/sale, citing “WV Code §19-12E-12, WV SB345 text” — Cannabis Regulations AI blog — Accessed 2026-10-24. This conflicts on its face with §19-12E-12(d)(1) (Commissioner of Agriculture issues hemp-derived cannabinoid permits, Run 1). SB 345 is a 2025 bill (Sen. Woodrum, introduced 13 February 2025, to Senate Judiciary) to authorise the ABCA Commissioner to promulgate legislative rule 175 CSR 11 (retail enforcement of hemp/kratom), filed in the State Register 20 August 2024; trackers record it died sine die 12 April 2025. Year-disambiguation (Parts 9–10 check): 2024 SB 345 is a homeless-education-coordinator bill (12 January 2024) and 2026 SB 345 is a Medical Imaging and Radiation Therapy Technology Board of Examiners rule (15 January 2026) — neither is hemp/kratom; the hemp/kratom bill is 2025 SB 345 only, and its final disposition rests on secondary tracker record, no primary 2025 history page having been opened in that check. The 2025 blog claim that ABCA issues hemp/kratom permits is therefore Removed as law — §19-12E-12(d)(1) (Agriculture Commissioner) controls (gap G-9.5 resolved; 175 CSR 11 in-force status remains [VERIFY]). Note also that SB 679 (2024) is secondarily described as “Amends Cannabis Oversight Under the West Virginia Alcohol Beverage Control Administration” (passed 9 March 2024; in effect from passage — enrolled text, Parts 9–10 check; secondary tallies differ, 71–26 vs 57–39, and no tally is adopted) — Accessed 2026-10-24 — which is consistent with an ABCA enforcement role, not with ABCA replacing WVDA as permit issuer.

9.1.7 West Virginia State Treasurer — fund holder / banking

Website: wvtreasury.com — Accessed 2026-10-24. Public contact (official Treasurer documents): West Virginia State Treasurer's Office, 1900 Kanawha Blvd. E, Charleston, WV 25305; office address 1900 Kanawha Boulevard East, Room E-145, Charleston, WV 25305. Source: Treasurer agency cover letter — Accessed 2026-10-24. Treasurer in 2026: Larry Pack (R) — oath 3 January 2025; full elected term from 13 January 2025 (Parts 9–10 check; Room E-145 address confirmed on Treasury letterhead). Role: designated depository / holder of the Medical Cannabis Program Fund (§16A-9-2). No official Treasurer cannabis-fund statement or balance was retrieved in this pass — see the fund-balance conflict in §9.2.2. No personal phone/email for any individual is reproduced here, per the user's rule.

9.1.8 Division of Financial Institutions (DFI) — banking relevance only

Website / phone (state agency directory): Division of Financial Institutions, (304) 558-2294, www.dfi.wv.gov — WV business agency directory — Accessed 2026-10-24. DFI regulates state-chartered financial institutions generally. No official DFI “cannabis-friendly banks” list, cannabis banking guidance, or DFI cannabis page was found [UNAVAILABLE]. Element Federal Credit Union remains the only verified WV cannabis-banking institution (Run 1, via HB 2538 (2019) competitive-bid authority). Do not present DFI as a cannabis licensing regulator.

9.1.9 Other bodies touching the programme

  • Board of Pharmacy: designates the pharmacist member of MCAB (§16A-11-1(a)(5)). Not a cannabis permit issuer.
  • County / city governments: county resident-vote ban (§16A-7-6) and municipal ordinance power (§16A-15-7) — Run 1, carried forward. Notice duty (§16A-15-8, Parts 9–10 check): a municipality enacting a restrictive ordinance “shall promptly notify the bureau,” and a county commission shall notify the bureau of a §16A-7-6 vote. No official OMC opt-out list retrieved [UNAVAILABLE] (Run 1 G-21).

  • HB 5260 (2026, medical cannabis edibles) — HOUSE-PASSED, Senate-stalled. This is the only firmly evidenced 2026 one-chamber cannabis passage. Official Legislature history (fetched directly in this pass): House Bill 5260, 2026 Regular Session, LAST ACTION: “S To Health and Human Resources 03/05/26”; summary “Relating generally to medical cannabis”; lead sponsor Eldridge; ROLL CALL VOTES: “House - Passed House (Roll No. 303)”; code affected §16A–3–2, §16A–3–3, §16A–8–1, §60A–9–4; similar to SB 892. House passage 4 March 2026 is confirmed by the primary history (Roll No. 303; Parts 9–10 check); the 66–26 vote tally remains secondary (LegiScan / MPP: “In March 2026, the West Virginia House of Delegates passed HB 5260”) — MPP — Accessed 2026-10-24. The official history confirms House passage and Senate referral 5 March 2026; the 66–26 tally and the sine-die fate [VERIFY] against House/Senate journals. HB 5260 is NOT law.   • HB 4371 (2026, adult-use by county referendum) — introduced, NOT passed, NOT law.   • HJR 37 (2026, cannabis constitutional amendment) — Pending, NOT law, NOT on the ballot (never referred to voters). NUMBERING COLLISION — do not conflate: a different HJR 37 (2025 session) contains Article XV Initiative/Referendum/Recall language. That is not the 2026 cannabis HJR 37. Likewise, SJR 3 (2025) and SJR 5 (2026) carry identical 2 oz / 4 plant text — LegiScan, SJR 5 — Accessed 2026-10-24 — but are separate resolutions, each [VERIFY] on wvlegislature.gov. 9.2.2 HB 5074 (2026, Medical Cannabis Program Fund allocation) — VETO VERIFIED OFFICIALLY

Official Legislature history (fetched directly in this pass): House Bill 5074, 2026 Regular Session, LAST ACTION: “Vetoed”; summary “Relating to changing the allocation of proceeds in the Medical Cannabis Program Fund”; code affected §16A—9—2; House passed (Roll No. 299) 4 March 2026; Senate passed (Roll No. 556) 13 March 2026; House concurred in Senate amendment and passed bill (Roll No. 659) 14 March 2026; to Governor 25 March 2026; “Vetoed by Governor 4/1/2026”. Source: wvlegislature.gov, HB 5074 history — Accessed 2026-10-24.

Veto rationale (secondary contemporary reporting): Governor Patrick Morrisey vetoed HB 5074, which would have redirected Medical Cannabis Program Fund money to specified uses; Morrisey said the bill did not leave an ongoing portion available for general revenue, limiting future budget flexibility, and that the state “can't totally pre-commit future revenue streams like this if it's going to have reserves to invest more in roads, water, sewer, site selection, rail, and future tax cuts”. Source: WHSV / Gray, 3 Apr 2026 — Accessed 2026-10-24.

What HB 5074 would have done (secondary only, NOT enacted): $5 million for newly-created Child Protection Commissioners / court commissioners for child abuse and neglect cases; $3 million for a Child Protection Commission and $5 million to combat homelessness in one secondary summary; legislature voted to allocate majority to university research grants, Child Protection Commission, and homelessness programmes — CNHI News — Accessed 2026-10-24. Do not present any of this as enacted allocation.

Fund-balance conflict — UNRESOLVED, report both figures, adopt neither as official (Contradiction R2-C-3). Run 1 secondary: ~$34M, “none spent as of October 2025.” Post-veto secondary (Mountain State Spotlight via Marijuana Moment): State Treasurer Larry Pack (R) said he will release funds under the original mandate; 100% to offices/programmes prescribed by original law — more than half to OMC, remainder split between substance-abuse treatment grants and law-enforcement grants; amount described as “$38 million collected over four years” — Marijuana Moment — Accessed 2026-10-24. A secondary market report's October 2025 allocation arithmetic (about $19M Bureau / $8M Fight Substance Abuse / $6M Division of Justice and Community Services / $1.5M law-enforcement training) — CannBus — Accessed 2026-10-24 — is consistent with the original §16A-9-2 split (55% / 22.5% / 18% / 4.5%, Run 1) applied to a ~$34M base, but is not an official Treasurer/Auditor statement. Audit confirmation: the current Code text of §16A-9-2 still shows that split (55% to the bureau; the remaining 45% as 50% Fight Substance Abuse Fund / 40% Division of Justice and Community Services grants / 10% law-enforcement training); the HB 5074 reallocation appears only as bill text and is NOT law. Do not state that Morrisey “redirected” the funds — the veto blocked the new allocation; the Treasurer release, if verified, follows the original §16A-9-2 allocation. Treasurer/Auditor primary source still required [VERIFY] (gap G-9.7).

Permit-count conflict — RESOLVED as a dated operational figure (Parts 9–10 check): OMC Stat Sheet (data 31 August 2026, omc.wv.gov/media/205): 70 operational dispensaries, 9 growers, 9 processors (“DATA UPDATED ON THE FIRST OF EVERY MONTH” — re-pull monthly). The OMC 2025 Biennial Report Tables 13–15 figures (9 growers / 9 processors / 76 dispensary rows) are table rows only, not an operating count, and must be quoted only as such. The secondary secondary operating/issued/above-cap figures are dropped as current counts — they mix award, licensed and operational definitions and match neither OMC operational sheet (the earlier 2 September 2025 sheet read 64 dispensaries / 9 growers / 9 processors). The “100 dispensary permits issued in late January 2021” figure (MPP, secondary) is a cumulative-award figure, not an operating figure. Gap G-9.8 is closed for the current operating count; the fund balance (G-9.7) remains [VERIFY].

9.3 Ballot history

9.3.1 Statewide citizen initiative: CONFIRMED ABSENT

Comparative table: West Virginia — Initiative: None; Referendum: None; Legislative referral: Yes — Wikipedia, Initiatives and referendums in the United States — Accessed 2026-10-24 (secondary compilation; corroborate with WV Constitution Article XIV amendment procedure — Article XIV requires legislative proposal by two-thirds of each house and voter ratification, the only statewide ballot route found [VERIFY] pinpoint, gap G-9.11). Advocacy petition summary, 15 October 2026 (secondary): “Did you know that West Virginians cannot collect signatures to put proposed state laws directly on the ballot? Citizens in 24 other states have some form of initiative power. West Virginia does not. During the 2026 legislative session, lawmakers introduced proposals, including House Joint Resolution 40 (HJR 40), that would have given citizens the power to propose laws and challenge certain laws passed by the Legislature. HJR 40 never made it out of committee.” — Change.org petition — Accessed 2026-10-24. HJR 40 (2026) introduced text would add new Constitution Article XV, Initiative and Referendum, reserving initiative/referendum powers to registered voters by petition — LegiScan, HJR 40 — Accessed 2026-10-24 (secondary tracker; official wvlegislature.gov status [VERIFY] — did not advance on evidence found). Consequence: no statewide cannabis ballot measure has ever been put to WV voters by citizen initiative, because the mechanism does not exist. Statewide cannabis change can only come by Legislature-enacted statute or Legislature-referred constitutional amendment (two-thirds of each house + voter ratification). The 2026 ballot does include a Legislature-referred citizenship-voting amendment, SJR 9 (House 97–0 on 13 March, Senate 34–0 on 14 March, per Ballotpedia News) — Ballotpedia News — Accessed 2026-10-24 — included only to show the referral mechanism in action; it is not a cannabis measure. Secondary 2026 brief confirms: “Citizen ballot initiatives allowed: No. West Virginia has no statewide citizen initiative process.” — Drug Policy Watch — Accessed 2026-10-24.

9.3.2 Local ballot / ordinance history (not statewide initiative)

  • Charleston, Kanawha County — Bill No. 8039 (2024), citizens-initiated under Charleston City Charter §89 — VERIFIED in secondary summary, ordinance text [VERIFY]: petition with 4,000+ signatures; City Council amended and approved (Council had the option of voting on the measure or placing it on the November ballot); as amended, removes fines and jail threat for first-time low-level possession (≤15 g); prior-conviction fines $250–$500; eligible convictions dismissed after payment/terms, others expungeable within six months — NORML, 20 Aug 2024 — Accessed 2026-10-24. Later item Bill No. 8043 / “Sensible Marijuana Ordinance” (2025 secondary coverage) — relationship to 8039 unresolved [UNAVAILABLE]; do not conflate. Note: Charleston's route was a charter petition to Council, not a statewide initiative and not, on this evidence, a ballot vote — Council adopted it as amended rather than sending it to the November ballot.
  • Morgantown, Monongalia County — possession <15 g, $15 fine, no jail (2020) — municipal ordinance, NOT a ballot referendum: “In a unanimous vote last Tuesday, Morgantown City Council became the first municipality in West Virginia to effectively decriminalize possession… Morgantown's ordinance lowers the misdemeanor crime of possession of less than 15 grams in city limits to a $15 fine and no jail time” — The Daily Athenaeum — Accessed 2026-10-24 (secondary; ordinance text/number not opened [VERIFY]). Limitation, stated in the same coverage: the ordinance does not bind county/state enforcement — a person in Morgantown city limits could still be arrested by WVU Police, Monongalia County Sheriff, or State Police under state law — The Daily Athenaeum — Accessed 2026-10-24.
  • Putnam County — ballot measures, pre-2021 (secondary, context only): voters approved four ballot measures allowing medical-marijuana facilities (reported >60% approval, May primary, pre-2021) — Green Health Docs — Accessed 2026-10-24 (secondary; ballot-measure text not opened [VERIFY]). Putnam is not one of Run 1's nine required jurisdictions. These were facility-siting measures, not decriminalisation or adult-use measures — do not over-read them.
  • Failed municipal decriminalisation efforts (secondary): “Efforts to decriminalize marijuana through municipal ballot initiatives have repeatedly failed, including attempts in 2019, 2021 and 2022… In 2022, activists attempted to put decriminalization measures on two municipal ballots in the state, but neither effort was ultimately successful. Similar efforts were undertaken in four cities a year earlier, but those efforts also fell short. In 2019, Salem voters rejected a similar reform proposal at the ballot box.” — Marijuana Moment — Accessed 2026-10-24. City names for the 2021/2022 attempts were not given in the excerpt — flag [VERIFY] before naming cities beyond Salem (2019, rejected).
  • Fairmont petition campaign (2022, secondary): West Virginia Can't Wait working on municipal ballot campaigns in Charleston and Fairmont for “no fines, no time and no court costs” for possession in Fairmont city limits — MJ Invest — Accessed 2026-10-24. No adopted Fairmont ordinance confirmed [UNAVAILABLE]/[VERIFY]. The Outlaw Report's 2022 account confirms the mechanism: a petition to Council, and if Council does not accept it exactly as worded, it can go to voters as a ballot initiative — Outlaw Report — Accessed 2026-10-24.

9.4 Public opinion polling — thin, and NO recent WV adult-use poll with full methodology found

  • Only quantified WV poll with pollster attribution and a quoted question (2017, MEDICAL use only — do NOT present as current adult-use support): Orion Strategies poll, reported at the start of the 2017 legislative session (WV Executive summary): “Regarding the issue of the legalization of marijuana, a 61% majority favor it if it is for medicinal use and prescribed by a doctor, with 35% against.” No sample size, margin of error, field dates, or full question wording given in the excerpt — WV Executive — Accessed 2026-10-24. This figure was not re-verified in the Parts 7–8/11–12 check and is carried only as a sourced secondary figure.
  • Older medical-use poll (secondary, date [VERIFY]): Public Policy Polling survey of 1,232 West Virginia voters, conducted January 7–9 (year not given in the excerpt — do not assume): “a plurality of voters support removing criminal penalties for marijuana possession by all adults and replacing them with a civil infraction and fine, similar to a parking ticket” — The Weed Blog — Accessed 2026-10-24. Full results via MPP (mpp.org/WVpoll) were not opened in this pass [VERIFY].
  • Three-state federal-legalisation poll (secondary, pollster/date/margin NOT given — do not upgrade): 1,026 West Virginia voters surveyed; overall support for federal legalization 70%, opposition 26%; Democrat 89%, Independent 74% (Republican figure cut off in excerpt) — Herbal Suite — Accessed 2026-10-24. This is a federal-legalisation question, not a WV adult-use-market question, and its sponsor/field dates are not stated in the excerpt [VERIFY].
  • No 2024–2026 WV-specific adult-use poll with pollster/date/sample/margin was located [UNAVAILABLE]. What exists is secondary characterisation without numbers: Marijuana Moment (2026): “Despite past surveys finding majority support among voters for the policy change, marijuana legalization efforts have progressed slowly in West Virginia.” No pollster, date, sample or percentage given — Accessed 2026-10-24. Cannabis Risk Manager (2025): “Despite polling that has shown majority voter support for legalization, West Virginia has moved cautiously.” No pollster/date/sample — Cannabis Risk Manager — Accessed 2026-10-24. WV Media Lab (undated, secondary, student/university-affiliated): “Polls show increasing public support for legalization in West Virginia, particularly among younger voters. However, opposition remains strong among conservative lawmakers and older residents.” No poll cited — WV Media Lab — Accessed 2026-10-24. Do not cite as a poll.
  • Related disparity figure (NOT a support poll, secondary): ACLU via MPP: WV ranks fourth in the nation for highest racial disparity in arrests for cannabis possession, Black people 7.3 times more likely to be arrested than white people — MPP — Accessed 2026-10-24 (ACLU report date/method not given in excerpt [VERIFY] before reuse). A secondary 2022 arrest figure (approximately 1,600 marijuana arrests in WV in 2022, half the 2010 number) — WV Marijuana Card — Accessed 2026-10-24 — is likewise secondary and [VERIFY].
  • Political signals, not polls: former Governor Jim Justice said in 2021 that although not personally a fan of legalisation, he would have supported reform if the Legislature sent a bill to his desk (secondary, Marijuana Moment); former Senate Majority Leader Craig Blair (R) said in 2024 legalisation could help ease fatal fentanyl overdoses and would come “sooner than later” (secondary, same article) — Accessed 2026-10-24. Record as statements, not polling.

Part 10 — Tribal and Interstate

10.1 Tribal — ANSWER: NONE. No federally recognised tribe in WV, no enacted state-recognised list, no tribal cannabis/hemp operation

10.1.1 Federally recognised tribes: none in West Virginia

By-state compilation of the BIA list: “## West Virginia — *No federally recognized tribes*” — Wikipedia, List of federally recognized tribes by state — Accessed 2026-10-24 (secondary compilation of the BIA list; use only as a pointer, not as the BIA source itself). BIA list mechanics: BIA must publish the list annually on or before every 30 January under the Federally Recognized Indian Tribe List Act of 1994. Recent lists cited in the NARF directory: 91 FR 4102, 30 January 2026; 89 FR 99899, 11 December 2024; 89 FR 944, 8 January 2024 — NARF tribal law directories — Accessed 2026-10-24. The 30 January 2026 Federal Register volume itself was returned in search (GovInfo, FR Vol. 91 Issue 20, 30 January 2026) — GovInfo, FR 2026-01-30 — Accessed 2026-10-24 — a full-text search of that document in the audit returns zero occurrences of “West Virginia” and zero of “Pocahontas” (gap G-10.1 resolved for that check; caveat: the list does not give a state for every entity, so this proves no entity is described as West Virginia and “Pocahontas Band” does not appear). Neighbouring Virginia does have federally recognised tribes (e.g. Pamunkey, Chickahominy, Monacan, recognised by Congress in January 2018) — do not import Virginia tribes into WV.

Erroneous secondary claim — DISCARD explicitly (Contradiction R2-C-6). A low-quality site claims “West Virginia is home to two federally recognized American Indian tribes: the Cherokee Nation and the Pocahontas Band of Pocahontas Indians” (Cherokee Nation, Tahlequah, Oklahoma, population 392,131; Pocahontas Band, Dunlow, WV, 1,228) — IndianReservation.info — Accessed 2026-10-24. Cherokee Nation is headquartered in Oklahoma, not a WV tribe; “Pocahontas Band” does not appear on the BIA list. Do not carry this claim forward. It is the tribal equivalent of the Run 1 same-name traps. No tribal land / reservation in WV was found; no Indian Country jurisdiction, tribal-state compact, or tribal cannabis code for WV was found. Historical presence is not current recognition: Shawnee, Mingo, Wyandot, Lenape/Delaware, Cherokee (Overhill towns) and Haudenosaunee peoples are ancestral to the region (secondary summaries) — StudyCountry — Accessed 2026-10-24 — but none is a federally recognised WV tribe with land/operations today.

10.1.2 State recognition: proposed, NEVER enacted — no state-recognised list exists in law

SB 705 (2022, introduced text ONLY): would create new Article 5-30, “Recognized Native American Tribes of West Virginia,” listing (in the introduced text) Adena; Cayuga; Cherokee; Conoy; Delaware; Iroquois including Mingo, Oneida, Onondaga, Seneca; Ottawa; Shawnee; Sioux including Blackfoot and Lakota; Susquehannock including Honniasont; Tutelo including Monacan and Saponi; Tuscarora; Uninh; Whitetop; Wyandot; plus organisations Native American Indian Federation Incorporated, Tribe of the Whitetop Band of Native Indians Incorporated, and Appalachian American Indians of West Virginia Incorporated — SB 705 introduced text — Accessed 2026-10-24. Introduced text only. No enactment was shown. Do not present this list as the state-recognised list. Earlier introduced versions with slightly different lists: SB 377 (2014, introduced text, 14 tribes + 2 organisations) — wvlegislature.gov, SB 377 — Accessed 2026-10-24; HB 2162 (2019, introduced, LegiScan) and HB 2779 (2014, amendment text, TrackBill) with further variations (e.g. Lenape, Patawomeck, Opequon in HB 2779); HB 3161 (2023) with yet another variant (Cheraw of WV; Tribe of the Whitetop Band) — Plural Policy, HB 3161 — Accessed 2026-10-24. All are proposals, not enacted recognition. Final dossier must say: WV has no enacted state-recognition statute comparable to other states' lists on evidence found; the only lists found are introduced-bill texts. Official bill-history confirmation that SB 705 / HB 3161 did not pass [VERIFY] (gap G-10.2).

Consequence for cannabis/hemp: with no federally or state-recognised tribe with WV land/operations verified, there is no tribal cannabis programme, tribal hemp plan, tribal-state cannabis compact, tribal tax, or tribal dispensary to report for WV. Any tribal member in WV is subject to the same state medical/hemp rules as any other WV resident, and any out-of-state tribal cannabis product brought into WV is subject to the interstate rules below. If a future state-recognition bill were enacted, it would not by itself create a cannabis programme — that would require separate tribal and/or state action, which does not exist today.

10.2 Interstate — core rule, reciprocity, what can be brought in, neighbours, agreements, Schedule III

10.2.1 Core rule — carried forward from Run 1, NOT expanded

WV medical cannabis is lawful only as set forth in Chapter 16A, for WV patients/caregivers with bureau-issued cards, dispensed by WV-permitted dispensaries. WV hemp is lawful only within Chapter 19, Article 12E. Everything outside those channels is a Chapter 60A offence in WV and, federally, adult-use marijuana remains Schedule I (fixed baseline).

10.2.2 WV reciprocity — narrow, discretionary, terminally-ill cancer only
