“The bureau may enter into reciprocity agreements with any states that have comparable requirements for the use and lawful purchase of medical cannabis in a manner consistent with the provisions of this article to allow terminally ill cancer patients to purchase medical cannabis in another state.” — §16A-3-5, code.wvlegislature.gov/16A-3 — Accessed 2026-10-24.

No list of executed reciprocity agreements and no general out-of-state-patient purchase right was located [UNAVAILABLE] in Run 1. The original cites a 26 September 2025 MCAB agenda listing “Reciprocity” under Old Business (see §9.1.2) — that agenda was not located on the OMC board page in the Parts 9–10 check, so the discussion point is [VERIFY], and in any case an agenda item shows the topic was discussed, not that an agreement was executed. Do not infer an agreement from an agenda item. Secondary consensus (not official): WV does not recognise out-of-state cards, and WV cards are valid only in WV — IndicaOnline (“West Virginia doesn't recognize out-of-state cards, and there's no reciprocity with neighboring states”); LegalClarity (“A West Virginia medical cannabis card is valid only within the state”); MMJNow (lists WV among states with no reciprocity for purchase or possession, alongside VA, PA, OH) — all Accessed 2026-10-24. Treat as secondary corroboration, not as WV law. Safe-harbour letter rule — historical, current status [VERIFY]: 64 CSR 113 (Run 1, per a 2017 Frost Brown Todd summary, “safe harbour letter relating to the use of medical cannabis by the terminally ill if purchased in other states than West Virginia”) is the closest historical interstate-adjacent mechanism found. Its current text and whether any safe-harbour letters are issued today were not verified — flag [VERIFY]; do not present it as a current import right (gap G-10.3).

10.2.3 What can legally be brought into WV — short answer

  • Marijuana (including medical marijuana from another state, adult-use marijuana, and WV-patient medicine bought out of state): NO. A WV patient/caregiver card does not make out-of-state marijuana lawful in WV, and an out-of-state card does not make marijuana lawful in WV. The lawful WV supply chain is closed-loop: “A dispensary may only obtain medical cannabis from a grower or processor holding a valid permit under this act” / “A grower or processor may only provide medical cannabis to a dispensary holding a valid permit under this act,” §16A-6-13(a)(7)–(8) (Run 1). Patient delivery into WV from another state is not provided for in Chapter 16A (Run 1, [VERIFY] against 64 CSR).
  • Hemp: the only interstate category with a lawful pathway, and it is conditioned. Federally lawful hemp (under the pre-11 December 2026 definition, and after that date under the new total-THC definition + 0.4 mg/container cap per the fixed baseline) shipped in compliance with federal and origin-state law is not marijuana under federal hemp law. But WV's own §19-12E-12 permitting, approved labelling, and 11% tax apply to hemp-derived cannabinoid products sold/distributed in WV, including remote sellers shipping into WV (§19-12E-12(b): out-of-state remote distributors/retailers shipping into WV are subject to WV permitting, labelling, business-registration (§11-12-1 et seq.) and taxation; §19-12E-12(h): non-in-person sales require an approved age-verification mechanism). No WV hemp-shipment licence, manifest, or interstate hemp agreement was located [UNAVAILABLE] — do not invent one. WVDA hemp product registration lists (Run 1) are the enforcement-side record, not a shipment permit.
  • WVDA hemp defence — narrow: §19-12E-9 provides a complete defence to prosecution for possession/cultivation of marijuana under §60A-4-401 et seq. where the defendant was growing industrial hemp pursuant to Article 12E. That protects growing under Article 12E, not importing marijuana or unapproved hemp-derived products. Unapproved or out-of-limit hemp-derived products found in WV are contraband subject to seizure/forfeiture/destruction, §19-12E-12(l)(1) (Run 1).
  • Federal transport bar — stated plainly by a neighbour regulator: Maryland's official FAQ: “Cannabis remains illegal under federal law, and you may not carry or transport cannabis over state lines. It remains illegal to leave (or enter) Maryland with any cannabis products — medical or adult-use (non-medical) products. Mailing cannabis in or out of the State is illegal.” — Maryland Cannabis Administration FAQ — Accessed 2026-10-24. The same federal bar applies to every WV border.

10.2.4 Neighbouring states — what a WV resident can and cannot do there (WV law still governs on return)

Maryland — adult-use, 21+ (official FAQ + secondary limits): adult-use legal since 1 July 2023; adults 21+ with valid government ID may purchase at licensed dispensaries without a medical card; medical programme continues alongside. Official Maryland FAQ confirms: purchase age 21+, government ID required, licensed dispensaries are the only legal purchase point, public smoking prohibited, driving impaired illegal, and transport over state lines illegal (quote in §10.2.3) — Maryland Cannabis Administration FAQ — Accessed 2026-10-24. Secondary purchase/possession summary: up to 1.5 ounces of flower, 12 grams of concentrates, or edibles containing up to 750 mg total THC as the “Personal Use Amount” — The Forest Dispensary (secondary) — Accessed 2026-10-24 (verify against Maryland Cannabis Administration official limits [VERIFY], gap G-10.4). WV consequence: a WV resident 21+ may lawfully purchase and consume in Maryland under Maryland law, but may not lawfully bring it back into WV — the return leg is illegal under both federal law and WV Chapter 60A/16A. Dispensary marketing that “welcomes neighbours from West Virginia” and says “you'll need to consume the cannabis you purchase before heading home” (CULTA secondary) — CULTA — Accessed 2026-10-24 — is consistent with that rule but is marketing, not law.

Ohio — adult-use, 21+ (secondary, with a 2026 change that MUST be verified officially): adults 21+ may purchase from licensed Ohio dispensaries without Ohio residency, with government photo ID; daily dispensary sale up to 2.5 ounces plant material / products containing no more than 15,000 mg total THC (secondary, Marijuana Herald); possession outside the home up to 2.5 ounces plant material / 15 grams extract; home grow 6 plants per adult, 12 per household (secondary summaries) — Marijuana Herald and Bloom Marijuana — Accessed 2026-10-24. 2026 Ohio change, secondary only [VERIFY] against Ohio Revised Code / Division of Cannabis Control: SB 56, effective 20 March 2026 per secondary summaries (after a failed veto-referendum attempt), allegedly makes it explicitly illegal to possess cannabis in Ohio purchased from another state (e.g. Michigan), requires original unopened packaging in trunk/rear inaccessible storage for transport, caps flower at 35% THC and extracts at 70%, caps adult-use edibles at 10 mg/serving / 100–110 mg/package (secondary summaries differ on 100 versus 110 — flag the discrepancy), restricts smoking/vaping to private residential/agricultural property, and repeals the Social Equity and Jobs Program — MedicateOH; Cannabis Industry Lawyer; Brian Smith Law (citing Ohio Revised Code Chapter 3796, §§3796.221, 3796.06, 3796.99, 2925.11, 4511.19) — all Accessed 2026-10-24. Do not present SB 56 details as verified Ohio law until the Ohio Revised Code text is opened. (Contradiction R2-C-2.) WV consequence (unchanged either way): Ohio-purchased marijuana may not lawfully be brought into WV, and (on the secondary SB 56 reading) WV-purchased marijuana, if it existed, could not lawfully be possessed in Ohio either — but WV has no adult-use product to export, so the practical WV→Ohio direction is moot except for WV medical patients, whose medicine is not Ohio-licensed product. Ohio medical reciprocity: none. Ohio does not recognise out-of-state medical cards (secondary reciprocity tables, §10.2.2). A WV patient cannot use a WV card at an Ohio dispensary as a medical patient, though a WV resident 21+ can buy as an adult-use customer in Ohio under Ohio adult-use law.

Virginia — possession legal, retail NOT operating until 1 July 2027 (do not assume retail): current possession (per separate Virginia research carried forward): adults 21+ may possess up to 1 ounce of marijuana; may grow up to 4 plants per household (tagged, not visible from street, not accessible to under-21s); may share up to 1 ounce with another adult 21+ with no remuneration; may smoke privately in their homes — Loudoun County (official county summary) and MPP Virginia — Accessed 2026-10-24. Retail: no licensed adult-use retail stores operating today. Standalone retail bills vetoed 28 March 2024 (SB 448 / HB 698, Gov. Youngkin), March 2025 (HB 2485 / SB 970, Gov. Youngkin), and 19 May 2026 (SB 542, Gov. Spanberger, per secondary summaries). Compromise budget language, HB 30 (2026 Special Session I), enacted per Run 1 Virginia summary on 29 June 2026 after standalone HB 642 / SB 542 vetoed 19 May 2026, provides: retail sales begin 1 July 2027; possession limit rises from 1 ounce to 2 ounces; up to 350 retail licences (plus up to 100 microbusinesses per one secondary summary); 6% state cannabis tax rising to 8% on 1 July 2029, plus 5.3–7% sales tax and 1–3.5% local tax; licence applications open by 1 February 2027 (microbusiness licences by 1 May 2027 per one secondary summary); draft rules September 2026 — MPP; MoGreenway; New York 420 Week — all Accessed 2026-10-24. DATE DISCREPANCY [VERIFY]: Run 1 Virginia summary says HB 30 enacted 29 June 2026; an MPP Virginia summary in this pass says signed 30 June 2026; Greenway says “enacted June 29.” Flag 29 versus 30 June 2026 [VERIFY] against the Virginia budget act. The 1 July 2027 retail start date is consistent across sources (Contradiction R2-C-1). WV consequence: a WV resident 21+ may lawfully possess (in Virginia) up to 1 ounce in Virginia today, but has no lawful Virginia retail store to buy from until 1 July 2027, and may not lawfully bring Virginia-possessed marijuana back into WV. Virginia medical dispensaries serve Virginia medical patients; Virginia does not recognise out-of-state medical cards (secondary reciprocity tables). A WV card gives no Virginia purchase right.

Pennsylvania — medical only, no reciprocity (secondary — verify against PA Department of Health official pages [VERIFY]): PA medical programme (Act 16, 2016) is residents-only: PA driver's licence/state ID, PA-registered physician, PA Department of Health registration, $50 card fee (waivable for Medicaid/SNAP/CHIP/PACE/PACENET/WIC in secondary summaries), up to 90-day supply (192 units in secondary summaries), dry leaf for vaporisation only (smoking prohibited), home grow prohibited — MyMMJDoctor; Pennsylvania Marijuana Card; Sanctuary Wellness Institute — all Accessed 2026-10-24. Reciprocity: “Pennsylvania does not have reciprocity for medical marijuana cards. Out-of-state cards are not accepted, and only Pennsylvania-issued cards are accepted at licensed dispensaries.” — MyMMJDoctor — Accessed 2026-10-24. Limited exception in secondary summaries: PA Act 16 §2106 allows a parent/guardian of a minor under 18 to lawfully obtain medical marijuana from another state/territory/country for the minor — LegalClarity — Accessed 2026-10-24 (secondary; verify §2106 text [VERIFY]). That exception, if verified, is a PA-side minor exception, not a WV→PA adult purchase right, and it does not make transport into WV lawful. Adult-use is not legal in Pennsylvania on evidence found. A WV resident cannot lawfully buy adult-use marijuana in PA, and a PA patient cannot lawfully buy in WV.

Kentucky (southern neighbour, not in the task's named list): no information was gathered in this pass; do not state Kentucky's position without research. Flag as [UNAVAILABLE] if the final dossier mentions it at all (gap G-10.5).

10.2.5 Interstate laws / agreements — NONE found for WV

No WV interstate cannabis compact, reciprocity agreement, hemp shipment agreement, or border-state memorandum was located [UNAVAILABLE]. The only interstate-adjacent WV provisions found are: §16A-3-5 (discretionary reciprocity agreements for terminally-ill cancer patients, no executed list found); §16A-7-3 / §16A-7-1(b)(4) (in-state grower/processor/laboratory/dispensary transport, not interstate); §19-12E-12(b)/(h)/(i)(3) (remote sellers shipping hemp-derived products into WV subject to WV permitting/labelling/registration/tax and use-tax collection); and §19-12E-10 (USDA state-plan submission, federal, not interstate). No WV law authorises a WV dispensary to sell to a non-resident, deliver across a state line, or accept an out-of-state card. No WV law authorises a WV resident to import marijuana.

10.2.6 Federal Schedule III / interstate transport limits (fixed baseline — flag any contradiction; none found)

WV's medical programme is state-licensed, so the fixed baseline applies: state-licensed MEDICAL marijuana and FDA-approved products moved to Schedule III effective 28 April 2026 (91 FR 22714); all adult-use marijuana remains Schedule I; IRS 280E is generally lifted for Schedule III medical activity, while adult-use remains fully subject to 280E. WV state 280E treatment post-April 2026 remains UNRESOLVED [VERIFY] (Run 1 G-42 — no WV decoupling statute located; fixed-date conformity). Schedule III does NOT authorise interstate transport, does NOT create a federal distribution channel, and does NOT make WV medicine bought in Maryland/Ohio/Virginia lawful in WV. Transporting marijuana across state lines remains illegal under federal law even between two legal states (Maryland official FAQ, §10.2.3). Nothing found in this pass contradicts the fixed baseline. Hemp federal dates (fixed baseline, not re-researched): PL 119-37 §781 takes effect 12 November 2026, but PL 119-103 §2019 limits it until 11 December 2026; after 11 December 2026 the full total-THC hemp definition and 0.4 mg total THC per container cap apply; USDA enforcement of DEA-registered lab testing for hemp is delayed to 31 December 2026; the federal medical marijuana appropriations rider runs through 11 December 2026; there is no federal cannabis banking law. No WV official response to PL 119-37 / PL 119-103 was found [UNAVAILABLE] (Run 1 G-40, carried forward).

Part 11 — Operations Rules

11.1 Testing: required tests, sampling, failed tests, recalls, labs

Statutory basis — §16A-7-4, Laboratory. Source: code.wvlegislature.gov/16A-7 — Accessed 2026-10-24.

“(a) A grower and processor shall contract with an independent laboratory to test medical cannabis produced… The bureau shall approve the laboratory and require reporting as the bureau determines, including a test at harvest and a test at final processing… (b) All medical cannabis produced under this chapter is subject to testing as directed by the bureau. (c) The bureau shall ensure sufficient testing capacity to meet patient demand. (d) All laboratories providing testing under this section shall be certified by the Office of Laboratory Services.”

No numeric lab cap in §16A-7-4 as opened. This confirms Run 1.

Required tests — 64 CSR 111 §10. Source: Justia, 64 CSR 111 §10, current through Register Vol. XLII, No. 25, June 19, 2025 — Accessed 2026-10-24.

  • §10.1: Before testing at a grower/processor's request, an approved laboratory must enter into a written contract with the grower/processor; copy to the Bureau within two days following the Bureau's request.
  • §10.2: Grower/processor must submit through the electronic tracking system a request to the approved laboratory with which it has a written contract, for each test.
  • §10.3: At a minimum, tests on (10.3.1) samples from a harvest batch or harvest lot prior to being used to produce a medical cannabis product, and (10.3.2) samples from each process lot before the medical cannabis is sold or offered for sale to another medical cannabis organization.
  • §10.4: Those samples must be tested, at a minimum, for: (10.4.1) pesticides; (10.4.2) solvents; (10.4.3) water activity and moisture content; (10.4.4) THC and CBD concentration; (10.4.5) microbiological contaminants.
  • §10.5: Sampling/testing must use a statistically significant number and size of samples and methodologies acceptable to the Bureau, to ensure all harvest batches, harvest lots, and products are adequately tested for contaminants and cannabinoid profile is consistent throughout.
  • §10.6: Approved laboratory may not test samples when there is evidence of improper collection, improper preservation, apparent spoilage, excessive time lapse between collection and testing, or any other factor sufficient to render findings of questionable validity.
  • §10.7: Approved laboratory must enter into the electronic tracking system and, under 64CSR110-22 (Management and disposal of medical cannabis waste), properly dispose of all tested and untested samples and test samples.

Sampling — 64 CSR 111 §8. Source: Cornell LII, 64 CSR 111 §8 — Accessed 2026-10-24. §8.1–8.2: approved laboratory must ensure employees prepare all samples per policies/procedures with information necessary for identifying, collecting, and transporting samples without endangering integrity; policies must be appropriate to the matrix sampled and in accordance with Bureau guidance. §8.3: procedures must include: surveying storage conditions; using appropriate sampling equipment and consistent procedures; selecting/removing equal portions for each sample; random or systematic taking throughout the harvest batch or lot; obtaining a minimum number of samples based on batch/lot size; checking all parts of the batch when lots are created from it; recording observations/procedures on a Bureau-prescribed form; creating a unique sample identification number linked to the batch/lot number assigned by the grower/processor in the electronic tracking system; entering all required information into the electronic tracking system.

Stability testing and retention — 64 CSR 111 §7. Source: Cornell LII, 64 CSR 111 §7 — Accessed 2026-10-24. §7.1: grower/processor must request a sample be identified and collected by an approved laboratory from each harvest batch sufficient to perform stability testing at six-month intervals for a one-year period. §7.2: stability test must ensure product potency and purity and provide support for expiration dating. §7.3: approved laboratory must retain a sample from each harvest batch sufficient for stability testing and properly store it for one year.

Transporting samples — 64 CSR 111 §14. Source: Justia, 64 CSR 111 §14, current through June 19, 2025 — Accessed 2026-10-24. §14.1: employee of an approved laboratory, grower/processor, or third-party contractor must follow transportation requirements under 64CSR110-17 and 64CSR110-18 when transporting a sample or test sample. §14.2: transporter of process lot samples from grower/processor to approved laboratory must protect physical integrity, keep composition intact, and protect against factors interfering with validity including time, temperature, and other environmental factors.

Standards for testing — 64 CSR 111 §11 — newly opened in this pass. Source: Cornell LII, 64 CSR 111 §11 — Accessed 2026-10-24. §11.1: “An approved laboratory must follow the methodologies and parameters acceptable to the Bureau which are contained in the scope of the certificate of accreditation issued to the laboratory.” No numeric pass/fail limit is stated in §11.1 itself. Numeric limits are Bureau-determined (64 CSR 111 §12.5.2: COAs must show contaminants do not exceed levels determined by the Bureau; §11.1: methodologies/parameters acceptable to the Bureau) — the numeric Bureau limits themselves remain [VERIFY] from an official OMC source — see §12.5 below on COAs. Do not quote a ppm/microbial limit from §11.

Test results, failed tests and retests — 64 CSR 111 §12 — newly opened in this pass. Source: Cornell LII, 64 CSR 111 §12 — Accessed 2026-10-24.

  • §12.1: Only these results are in compliance: (12.1.1) tests on harvest batch/lot samples requested by a grower/processor under §10 and identified/collected by an employee of an approved laboratory; (12.1.2) tests on process lot samples requested under §10 and identified/collected by either an employee of a grower/processor or an employee of an approved laboratory.
  • §12.2: Test results for each sample must be entered into the electronic tracking system and must only be accessible to the grower/processor submitting the sample and to the Bureau.
  • §12.3 — Failed test rule, verbatim in substance: If a sample fails any test required under §10: (12.3.1) the approved laboratory that performed the initial test may re-test the sample upon a request from the grower/processor in accordance with §12.4; (12.3.2, printed as “13.3.2” in the Cornell republication — numbering typo [VERIFY]) if the sample passes the re-test, a second, confirmatory re-test must be performed using the same harvest batch, harvest lot, or process lot to confirm the passing result; (12.3.3) if the Bureau does not agree to accept the results from the approved laboratory, the sample must be disposed of by the approved laboratory under 64CSR110-22.
  • §12.4: Grower/processor must notify the Bureau and the approved laboratory through the electronic tracking system of its intent to re-test the sample or test another sample from the same harvest batch, harvest lot, or process lot that failed a test.
  • §12.5: Approved laboratory must issue a certificate of analysis, including supporting data, for each harvest batch, harvest lot, or process lot sample tested. The COA must include: §12.5.1: whether the chemical profile conforms to the chemical profile of the strain as determined by the Bureau for: THC, THCA, THCV, CBD, CBDA, CBDV, CBN, CBG, CBC, and any other cannabinoid component at 0.1%. §12.5.2: that contaminants do not exceed levels as determined by the Bureau for: (a) heavy metals — mercury, lead, cadmium, arsenic; (b) foreign material such as hair, insects, or any similar/related adulterant; (c) pesticide residue; (d) microbiological impurity including total aerobic microbial count, total yeast mold count, P. aeruginosa, Aspergillus spp., S. aureus, aflatoxin B1/B2/G1/G2, ochratoxin A, E. coli, Salmonella; (e) whether the batch/lot is within specification for the strain for odor, appearance, fineness, moisture content.
  • Important distinction: §10.4 lists the minimum tests; §12.5 lists the wider COA contents, including heavy metals and foreign material, with levels “as determined by the Bureau.” No Bureau numeric action levels were retrieved in this pass — [VERIFY] against Bureau guidance/COA policy before quoting any limit.
  • No remediation rule found in §§10–12: §12 provides retest + confirmatory retest + disposal if the Bureau does not accept results. No rule authorising reprocessing, remediation, or blending of a failed batch was located in §§10–12 [VERIFY] — a full-series search is required before stating categorically that no remediation provision exists anywhere in 64 CSR 109–112. Do not state that failed product may be remediated.

Quality assurance — 64 CSR 111 §13 — newly opened in this pass. Source: Cornell LII, 64 CSR 111 §13 — Accessed 2026-10-24. §13.1: approved laboratory must establish and implement a quality assurance program to ensure measurements are accurate, errors are controlled, and devices used for testing are routinely and properly calibrated. §13.2: program must include: organisational chart with testing responsibilities of each named employee; description of sampling procedures; chain of custody protocols; analytical procedures; data reduction and validation procedures; plan for implementing corrective action when necessary; requirement for quality assurance reports to management; description of internal and external quality control systems.

Recalls — grower/processor side. Source: SOS CSR PDF, SOS, 64 CSR filing DocId 59252 (2025) — Accessed 2026-10-24; statutory basis §16A-7-1(a)(4), code.wvlegislature.gov/16A-7 — Accessed 2026-10-24. §16A-7-1(a)(4) requires a system for recall of defective medical cannabis; §16A-7-1(b) tracking includes security/surveillance, recordkeeping/retention, acquisition/possession/growing/processing, delivery/transportation, and dispensing. 64 CSR 110 §23.3.1: if a grower/processor discovers a condition relating to medical cannabis grown/processed at its facility poses a risk to public health and safety, it must (a) immediately notify the Bureau by phone, and (b) secure, isolate, and prevent distribution of affected medical cannabis remaining in its possession; it may not dispose of affected medical cannabis prior to notifying the Bureau and coordinating disposal with the Bureau. §23.3.2: if a grower/processor fails to cooperate with the Bureau in a recall, or fails to immediately notify the Bureau of a need for a recall under §23.1, the Bureau may issue a cease and desist order under 64CSR109-20 (General penalties and sanctions) and the grower/processor may be subject to other penalties/sanctions in the Act or rule. §23.4: recall plan must include: designation of recall coordinators (responsible for, among other duties, accepting recalled medical cannabis); procedures for identifying and isolating affected medical cannabis; procedures to retrieve and dispose of affected medical cannabis; communications plan to notify those affected, including other organisations/labs in possession of recalled product and press releases/other notifications to ensure patients/caregivers are notified if affected product was dispensed; procedures for notifying the Bureau; procedures for entering recall information into the grower/processor's system. §23.7: recall record must include total amount recalled including types, forms, harvest batches, harvest lots, process lots if applicable; amount received back by date/time; total amount returned; names of recall coordinators; from whom recalled product was received; means of transport; reason for recall; number of recalled samples/test samples, types/forms/batches/lots sent to approved laboratories, names/addresses of labs, dates of testing, and results by sample/test sample; manner of disposal including name of individual overseeing disposal, name of disposal company if applicable, and method of disposal. Dispensary-specific recall rule in 64 CSR 112 was not opened in full — [VERIFY].

Lab list / lab-cap claim — resolved against a cap (Contradiction R2-C-12 updated). Current statute §16A-7-4 contains no two-lab cap (above); it instead requires the Bureau to ensure sufficient testing capacity to meet patient demand. Run 1 found the two-lab proposal only in dead 2022 bills SB 674 / HB 4627 (2022, House-engrossed, died in Senate) — not enacted law. Secondary source Flourish Software, Flourish Software, West Virginia — Accessed 2026-10-24, states: “Following 2022 legislation, the total number of testing laboratory permits in West Virginia is limited to two. As of late 2025, both laboratory permits are active. Unlike the other permit types, the OMC continues to accept laboratory applications, though the two-permit cap limits availability.” This conflicts with the current §16A-7-4 text and is not adopted. Secondary source WestVirginiaStateCannabis.org, WestVirginiaStateCannabis.org, testing lab — Accessed 2026-10-24, lists as currently licensed: “Analabs Inc. of Crab Orchard” and “The Department of Health and Human Resources Environmental and Medical Cannabis Laboratory, Elkeview” [spelling as returned]. Secondary, non-official, DHHR-era name outdated after the 2024 reorganisation — names/count [VERIFY] against OMC/64 CSR 111, not adopted as an official list. No OMC approved-laboratory list was retrieved in this pass — [UNAVAILABLE] for a verified official lab list. Do not present the two names above as the official list. Secondary claims about a laboratory director, ILAC MRA accreditation, and a WV Cannabis Laboratory Proficiency Testing Program (WestVirginiaStateCannabis.org; Food Handler's Guide, Food Handler's Guide — Accessed 2026-10-24) are consistent in part with §§11/13 (accreditation scope, QA program) but were not verified against CSR text for director/proficiency-testing specifics — [VERIFY]. OMC 2021 sampling guidance (secondary/official guidance document, HubSpot-hosted PDF, OMC sampling guidance 2021 — Accessed 2026-10-24): sample size representative, no less than 0.5% of batch weight or sufficient amount to complete all analyses; maximum batch size 10 pounds raw plant / 5 pounds processed product. Currency [VERIFY] — 2021 document; do not present as current without checking against 2025 CSR revisions.

11.2 Licence rules

Nontransferability and privilege — confirmed. Sources: code.wvlegislature.gov/16A-6-3 and code.wvlegislature.gov/16A-6 — Accessed 2026-10-24.

§16A-6-3(b): “A permit issued under this chapter shall be nontransferable.” §16A-6-3(c): “The issuance or renewal of a permit shall be a revocable privilege.” §16A-6-3(a)(7): Bureau may require motion-activated video surveillance at a dispensary, grower or processor facility and retention of recordings onsite or offsite for a period not to exceed 180 days, unless otherwise required for investigative or litigation purposes.

Reconciliation — permit nontransferable ≠ ownership cannot change. Sources: Cornell LII, 64 CSR 109 §4; Cornell LII, 64 CSR 109 §12; Cornell LII, 64 CSR 109 §2 — Accessed 2026-10-24.

  • 64 CSR 109 §4.1 application types include: (4.1.1) initial permit; (4.1.2) permit renewal; (4.1.3) application for approval of a change in ownership of a medical cannabis organization authorised by a permit; (4.1.4) application for approval of a change of location; (4.1.5) application for approval of alteration of a facility; (4.1.6) application for an additional grower location. Note: older Fox Rothschild republication lists “additional dispensary locations” instead — wording difference [VERIFY] against current 2025 CSR. Source for older version: Fox Rothschild, 64 CSR 109 (older republication) — Accessed 2026-10-24.
  • 64 CSR 109 §12.1: on an impending change in ownership from that listed in the initial or a renewal application, the organisation must submit an application for approval of a change in ownership, on a Bureau-prescribed form, together with the fee required under the fees section [fee-section numbering differs between republications — §5 vs §6 — [VERIFY] current numbering].
  • §12.2: Bureau, in its sole discretion, may permit incorporation by reference of initial/renewal application information. §12.3: application not complete until all portions completed and appropriate fee submitted; Bureau may reject an incomplete application. §12.4: for each individual part of the proposed change, include all information required under §6 (Initial permit application) for individuals in those capacities. §12.5: if Bureau determines application lacks sufficient information, it will notify in writing; organisation has 30 days from mailing date to provide additional information/documentation.
  • Definitions, 64 CSR 109 §2: §2.11 “Change in control” = acquisition by a person or group acting in concert of a controlling interest in an applicant or permittee either all at one time or over a 12-consecutive-month period. §2.12 “Change in ownership” = addition or removal of a principal, operator, or financial backer, or a change in control after the Bureau approves an initial or renewal application. §2.15 “Controlling interest” = for a publicly traded company, voting rights entitling a person to elect/appoint one or more board members, or ownership/beneficial holding of 5% or more of securities; for a privately held entity, ownership of any security (text truncated in search result — [VERIFY] full §2.15).
  • Continuing duty, §16A-6-5(a), code.wvlegislature.gov/16A-6-5 — Accessed 2026-10-24: applicant/permittee is under a continuing duty to (1) report any change in facts/circumstances reflected in the application or any newly discovered/occurring fact/circumstance required to be included, including a change in control; (2) report to law enforcement within 24 hours any loss or theft of medical cannabis; (3) submit to announced or unannounced Bureau inspections of facilities and all records. §16A-6-5(b): if Bureau is not satisfied, it notifies applicant in writing; applicant may submit additional material within 30 days of receipt.
  • Practical meaning: a permit cannot be assigned/sold as a standalone instrument. A change in who owns/controls the permittee organisation is a separate Bureau-approval application. A sale structured as a change of ownership/control therefore still requires Bureau approval; a purported bare transfer of the permit itself is ineffective under §16A-6-3(b).

Renewal — statute + OMC advisory. Sources: code.wvlegislature.gov/16A-6-6; OMC Technical Advisory #25010001, effective 24 Mar 2025 — Accessed 2026-10-24. §16A-6-6(1)(D) and (2)(D): renewal application must be filed not more than six months nor less than four months prior to expiration, for both grower/processor and dispensary. Fees: grower/processor renewal $5,000 covering all locations; dispensary renewal $2,500 covering all locations; all fees by certified check or money order; $250 fee for amending to indicate relocation within the state or addition/deletion of approved activities (§16A-6-6(3)). Staggered expiries per OMC Technical Advisory #25010001: growers 30 September, processors 12 November, dispensaries 28 January each year; renewals via Complia; failure to file eliminates OMC's ability to grant extensions and results in expiry and permanent cessation (per Run 1; advisory text to be re-opened for verbatim in final compilation — [VERIFY] verbatim wording). Older 64 CSR 109 republication §11 (Fox Rothschild, above): renewal denial grounds include falsification and unlikelihood of compliance; existing permit immediately invalid on expiration if renewal + fees not filed; organisation may not operate if permit not renewed prior to expiration, except §11.5 administrative extension if renewal application + fee submitted as required, until Bureau completes review; on denial/non-filing, grower/processor must dispose of remaining product/plant matter/seed/equipment per waste rule, dispensary must return product to grower/processor of origin. Current 2025 CSR numbering [VERIFY]. Statutory renewal denial, §16A-6-10 (per Run 1, to be re-opened for verbatim): Bureau must renew unless applicant unlikely to maintain diversion control or unlikely to comply with state law; denial/nonrenewal must specify in detail how requirements were not met; applicant may within 30 days submit additional material and/or demand a hearing — [VERIFY] verbatim.

Relocation / alteration / additional locations. Sources: §16A-6-8 (per Run 1); 64 CSR 109 §4.1.4–4.1.6 (above); §16A-6-6(3) $250 amendment fee (above) — Accessed 2026-10-24. Bureau may approve relocation within WV or addition/deletion of activities/facilities; CSR route is a change-of-location / alteration / additional-grower-location application. Full §16A-6-8 text [VERIFY] in final compilation.

Suspension / revocation. Sources: §16A-6-11 (per Run 1); 64CSR109-20 referenced in 64 CSR 110 §23.3.2 (above) — Accessed 2026-10-24. §16A-6-11 grounds (per Run 1): failure to maintain effective diversion control; violation of the Act or Bureau rule; intentional/knowing/reckless/negligent failure to comply with WV medical-cannabis law. Full verbatim [VERIFY]. CSR general penalties/sanctions: 64CSR109-20.

Surrender / closure. Source: older Fox Rothschild republication of 64 CSR 109 §17 (closure), Fox Rothschild, 64 CSR 109 — Accessed 2026-10-24 — current section number [VERIFY], as Cornell LII now uses §17 for insurance (below), indicating renumbering. Organisation may not remove/destroy seeds, immature plants, plants, medical cannabis, products, other plant matter, equipment, or medical devices/instruments until the Bureau has approved its closure plan; it must comply with waste disposal under 64-109-22 (older numbering; current 64CSR110-22). If Bureau approves closure plan, organisation shall surrender its permit to the Bureau on or before the closure date in the plan. Closure plan must state how it intends to dispose of seeds/plants/products/plant matter projected to still be in the facility at projected closure, and how it intends to dispose of equipment/devices/instruments. Bureau may enter site/facility and inspect vehicles after receiving closure plan to determine whether to approve it.

Ownership caps. Source: §16A-6-13 (per Run 1, confirmed in searches) — Accessed 2026-10-24. ≤10 dispensary permits, 1 grower permit, 1 processor permit per person; vertical integration expressly permitted (§16A-6-13(a)(9)); concurrent holding permitted. No additional CSR ownership cap was located.

Insurance / bonding / capital. Source: Cornell LII, 64 CSR 109 §17 — Accessed 2026-10-24; older Fox Rothschild republication §26 (above) — Accessed 2026-10-24. 64 CSR 109 §17 (current Cornell numbering): §17.1 — organisation must obtain and maintain an appropriate amount of insurance coverage insuring the site, facility, and equipment used in operation; adequate comprehensive liability insurance covering activities authorised by the permit must begin on the date the initial permit is issued and continue as long as operating under the permit. §17.2 — must obtain and maintain workers' compensation insurance for employees at the time the organisation is determined operational by the Bureau. No dollar minimum is stated in §17 as opened. It is an “appropriate/adequate amount” standard, not a numeric minimum. Section number (17 vs 18 in older republication) differs — [VERIFY] current numbering. Capital affidavits, older §26: §26.1 — grower/processor applicant affidavit: at least $2 million in capital, $500,000 on deposit with one or more financial institutions. §26.2 — dispensary applicant affidavit: at least $150,000 on deposit. §26.3 — affidavit in Bureau-prescribed form. §26.4 — signed release allowing Bureau to contact each listed financial institution to verify. Current 2025 CSR numbering/amounts still [VERIFY] against apps.sos.wv.gov. Surety bond: no bond amount found in §16A-6-6 or §26 excerpts in Run 1, and none found in Part 11 searches. Do not state a bond amount; treat as [UNAVAILABLE] if no bond exists elsewhere in 64 CSR 109–112.

11.3 Cultivation

Indoor only — statute. Source: code.wvlegislature.gov/16A-7, §16A-7-2(b) — Accessed 2026-10-24.

“A grower or processor may only grow, store, harvest or process medical cannabis in an indoor, enclosed, secure facility which: (1) Includes electronic locking systems, electronic surveillance and other features required by the bureau; and (2) Is located within this state.”

No outdoor cultivation is authorised. Greenhouse is not named as a separate category in the statute — whether a greenhouse counts as “indoor, enclosed, secure” is [VERIFY] against 64 CSR 110 facility rules; do not assume.

Growing/processing requirements — 64 CSR 110 §9. Source: Cornell LII, 64 CSR 110 §9 — Accessed 2026-10-24. §9.1: grower/processor must use only pesticides, fungicides, or herbicides approved by USDA for use on medical cannabis plants and listed in Appendix A (Acceptable pesticide active ingredients for use); Bureau will periodically publish a notice in the State Register updating the list. §9.2: must use Appendix A products in a manner approved by USDA on the basis of federal law/regulations. §9.3: must maintain a log of all actions taken to detect pests/pathogens and measures taken for control. §9.4: must (9.4.1) use appropriate nutrient practices; (9.4.2) use fertilizer/hydroponic solution of a type, formulation, and rate to support healthy growth; (9.4.3) maintain records of type/amounts of fertilizer and growth additives used. §9.5: must perform visual inspections of growing plants and harvested material to ensure no visible mold, mildew, pests, rot, or grey/black plant material greater than an acceptable level as determined by the Bureau. §9.6: may not add additional active ingredients/materials altering color, appearance, smell, taste, effect, or weight unless prior written Bureau approval obtained; excipients must be pharmaceutical grade unless otherwise approved. §9.7: must have a separate and secure area for temporary storage of medical cannabis awaiting disposal. §9.8 onwards: text truncated in search result; full §9.8+ [VERIFY] by opening the Cornell page. Note on §9.1 wording: USDA does not register pesticides for cannabis federally in the usual sense; the rule's “approved by USDA” wording is reported as written, and the federal-approval tension is flagged [VERIFY] rather than rewritten.

Plant counts / canopy: none located. No plant-count limit, canopy limit, or square-footage limit was found in §16A-7-2, 64 CSR 110 §9, or searches. Do not invent a plant count. The controls located are facility-based (indoor/enclosed/secure; statutory, §16A-7-2(b)), permit caps (10 growers), and tracking (seed-to-sale, daily inventory), not a per-plant or per-canopy cap on the evidence retrieved. Check 64 CSR 110 §§8–9 in full and OMC grower guidance before stating “no limit” as a settled rule vs “none located.”

Sanitation / water (facility). Source: SOS CSR PDF, SOS DocId 59252, §15.2–15.6 — Accessed 2026-10-24. Employees must conform to sanitary practices including adequate personal hygiene, proper clothing including gloves, thorough hand-washing before starting work and when soiled/contaminated; grower/processor must provide adequate/convenient hand-washing facilities with running water at a temperature suitable for sanitizing hands, located in processing areas and where good sanitary practices require; effective nontoxic sanitizing cleansers and sanitary towel service/suitable drying devices; adequate, readily accessible lavatories maintained sanitary and in good repair; facility must be provided with a water supply sufficient for operations, derived from a public water system or a nonpublic system capable of providing a safe, potable, adequate supply. Must comply with all other applicable state/local building code requirements (§15.6, truncated).

11.4 Security: cameras, alarms, storage, transport

Statute. Sources: code.wvlegislature.gov/16A-7, §§16A-7-2(b), 16A-7-3; code.wvlegislature.gov/16A-6-3, §16A-6-3(a)(7) — Accessed 2026-10-24. §16A-7-2(b): indoor, enclosed, secure facility with electronic locking systems, electronic surveillance and other Bureau-required features, in-state. §16A-6-3(a)(7): Bureau may require motion-activated video surveillance and retention ≤180 days unless investigative/litigation need. §16A-7-3 (Storage and transportation): Bureau shall develop rules for storage/transportation among grower/processors, testing laboratories and dispensaries ensuring adequate security against in-transit losses; tracking system shall include all transportation/storage; rules shall provide for (1) shipping containers/packaging; (2) manner trucks/vans/trailers/carriers secured; (3) security systems including a numbered seal on the trailer; (4) obtaining copies of drivers' licences/registrations and other security/tracking information; (5) use of GPS systems; (6) number of drivers/other security required against storage/in-transit losses; (7) recordkeeping for delivery/receipt; (8) requirements to use Bureau electronic tracking system; (9) transporting to a grower/processor, approved laboratory or dispensary.

Dispensary security — 64 CSR 112 §11 — newly opened in full in this pass. Source: Cornell LII, 64 CSR 112 §11 — Accessed 2026-10-24.

  • §11.1: dispensary must have security and surveillance systems, utilising commercial-grade equipment, to prevent unauthorised entry, and to prevent and detect an adverse loss, including: §11.1.1: professionally monitored security alarm system including: coverage of all facility entrances/exits; rooms with exterior windows, exterior walls, roof hatches, or skylights; storage rooms, including those that contain medical cannabis, and safes; and perimeter; silent duress alarm (designated code signalling forced turn-off); audible panic alarm (manual activation for life-threatening/emergency requiring law enforcement); silent holdup alarm (manual activation for robbery in progress); device capable of sending a prerecorded voice message requesting dispatch over telephone/radio/other system to law enforcement/public safety/emergency agency; failure notification system providing audible/text/visual notification and alerting a designated security person by telephone/e-mail/text within five minutes after failure; smoke and fire alarms; auxiliary power sufficient to maintain security/surveillance for at least 48 hours following a power outage; ability to ensure all access doors are not solely controlled by an electronic access panel to prevent locks from becoming released during a power outage; motion detectors.
  • §11.1.2: professionally monitored security/surveillance system operational 24 hours/day, 7 days/week, recording all activity in images capable of clearly revealing facial detail, with fixed camera placement allowing clear image of: areas where medical cannabis is loaded/unloaded into/from transport vehicles; entrances/exits recorded from both indoor and outdoor vantage points; rooms with exterior windows, exterior walls, roof hatches/skylights, and storage rooms including those containing medical cannabis and safes; five feet from the exterior perimeter; all limited access areas; auxiliary power ≥48 hours; ability to operate under normal lighting of each area; ability to immediately produce a clear, color, still photograph in digital format.
  • §11.1.3: ability to clearly and accurately display date/time, synchronised and set correctly, not significantly obscuring the picture.
  • §11.1.4: ability to record all images in an easily accessed format for not less than 180 days, unless otherwise required for investigative/litigation purposes (see §11.2.5.b). Code/rule tension flagged in audit: §16A-6-3(a)(7) says “not to exceed” 180 days while this rule says “not less than” 180 days — operationally cite the rule; recordings kept at the facility in a locked cabinet/closet/other secure place in a limited access area/room limited to authorised individuals, or at a secure location other than the facility if approved by the Bureau.
  • §11.1.5: separate security alarm system, meeting the same requirements as the primary system, covering the limited access area/room where recordings are stored.
  • §11.2: inspection/servicing/alteration/upgrade: systems inspected and all devices tested once every year by a qualified alarm vendor and qualified surveillance vendor as approved by the Bureau; dispensary must conduct maintenance inspections once every month; retain records of inspections/servicing/alterations/upgrades at the facility for at least four years and make available to Bureau/agents within two business days following a request; if mechanical malfunction anticipated to exceed four hours, notify Bureau immediately and, with Bureau approval, provide alternative security measures that may include closure; designate an employee to continuously monitor the systems; within two business days following a request, provide screen captures of an unaltered copy of a video recording to Bureau/agents, law enforcement, or other government officials if necessary; if notified in writing of a pending criminal/administrative investigation for which a recording may contain relevant information, retain an unaltered copy for two years or until investigation/proceeding is closed or entity notifies retention is no longer necessary, whichever is longer.
  • §11.3: commercial-grade, nonresidential doors and door locks on each external door; keys/key codes remain in possession of designated authorised individuals.
  • §11.4: during all nonworking hours, all entrances/exits securely locked.
  • §11.5: electronic back-up system for all electronic records.
  • §11.6: lighting to ensure proper surveillance inside and outside the facility.
  • §11.7: access to room containing security/surveillance monitoring equipment limited to persons essential to maintaining security/surveillance operations; federal/state/local law enforcement; security/surveillance system service employees; Bureau/agents; and other persons with prior written Bureau approval. Dispensary must make available to Bureau/agents on request a current list of authorised employees/service employees/contractors with access to security/surveillance areas; keep security/surveillance rooms locked at all times and not use them for any other purpose/function.

Grower/processor security section in 64 CSR 110 and any 64 CSR 109 general security section were not opened in full in this pass — the dispensary §11 above must not be assumed to be identical for growers/processors. Cross-references to open: 64CSR110 §§17 (Transportation), 18 (Manifest); 64CSR112 §§15 (Transportation), 16 (Manifest). Plan-of-operation security policies (SOS PDF, 64 CSR 110 §4.1.2) include staff identification, monitoring of staff/visitor attendance, alarm systems, video surveillance, and monitoring/tracking inventory — source: SOS DocId 59252 — Accessed 2026-10-24.

11.5 Waste disposal

Source: SOS CSR PDF, SOS DocId 59252, 64 CSR 110 §22; 64 CSR 111 §10.7 (above); §16A-7-1(a)(5), code.wvlegislature.gov/16A-7 — Accessed 2026-10-24. Acceptable methods of rendering waste unusable and unrecognizable include thermal treatment or melting; shredding, grinding, or tearing; and incorporating the medical cannabis waste with other municipal waste. Secondary summary (High Yield Solutions, Cannabis Waste Regs, July 2023 — Accessed 2026-10-24) adds: incorporating waste with other ground material so the resulting mixture is at least 51% noncannabis waste; other methods must be approved by OMC before implementation. The 51% figure is secondary-summary only; [VERIFY] against §22 text itself. §22.4: unusable/unrecognizable waste identified in §22.2 and other solid/semi-solid waste that is not hazardous must be disposed of at a permitted municipal waste landfill or processed at a permitted resource recovery facility or incinerator. §22.5: wastewater or spent hydroponic nutrient solution from growing/harvesting/processing immature or mature plants must be managed by (22.5.1) discharge into a permitted sewage treatment system per local/federal/state requirements including the Water Pollution Control Act (W. Va. Code §22-11-1 et seq.); (22.5.2) treatment and discharge into waters of the state under an NPDES permit or water quality management permit per local/federal/state requirements including §22-11-1 et seq.; or (22.5.3) disposal in a solid waste landfill if placed in a container less than one gallon in size. §22.6: hazardous waste must be managed per federal/state hazardous waste law/rules, including RCRA §§3001–3024 (42 U.S.C. §§6921–6939g), Hazardous Waste Management Act (W. Va. Code §22-18-1 et seq.) and rules thereunder. §22.7: waste type in §22.2.2 may be composted and beneficially used at the grower/processor facility through a permit provided 33CSR3 (Yard Waste Composting Rule) requirements are satisfied and compost is beneficially used at the facility. Lab samples: 64 CSR 111 §10.7 — approved laboratory must enter into the electronic tracking system and, under 64CSR110-22, properly dispose of all tested/untested samples and test samples. Statutory waste tracking: §16A-7-1(a)(5) — system to track plant waste from growth or other disposal, including name/address of any disposal service; §16A-7-1(b) tracking includes disposal. Numbering note: Run 1 Parts 3–4 cited waste as 64-109-22 in the older republication; the SOS PDF and 64 CSR 111 §10.7 cite 64CSR110-22. This is a renumbering difference, not a substance conflict; final compilation must use current 2025 numbering and note the older citation. Not yet retrieved: full §22.1–22.3 (waste definitions, segregation, storage, recordkeeping, witnessing/verification), and any dispensary-specific waste rule in 64 CSR 112.

11.6 Environmental: water, pesticide limits, energy

Pesticides — strong. Sources: Cornell LII, 64 CSR 110 §9 (§9.1–9.2, above); Cornell LII, 64 CSR 110 §24 — Accessed 2026-10-24. 64 CSR 110 §24.1: use of a pesticide by a grower/processor in growing/processing must be in accordance with the West Virginia Pesticide Control Act (W. Va. Code §19-16A-1 et seq.) and this rule. §24.2: Bureau and WVDA will cooperate to inspect/enforce this section. §24.3 recordkeeping: record of each application must include date (with hour completed if re-entry time applies); place including specific block/section or immature/mature plants treated; size of area treated; product name of every pesticide used; EPA product registration number (unnecessary for products exempted under FIFRA §25, 7 U.S.C. §136w); total amount in pounds/ounces/gallons/liters applied; dosage/rate; if applicable, employee ID numbers of individuals involved and permit/certification numbers of individuals making/supervising application; copies of pesticide labels and Safety Data Sheets for pesticides used at the facility. §24.3.2: record must be completed within 24 hours of application completion and maintained for at least four years; further text truncated — [VERIFY] full §24. Testing for pesticides is a required lab test (64 CSR 111 §10.4.1; COA pesticide residue, §12.5.2.c), but a numeric pesticide residue limit / action level was not retrieved. Do not state a ppm limit without a Bureau/Appendix A source. Gap: Appendix A list itself and any Bureau pesticide limits.

Water. Sources: SOS PDF §15.5 (above); 64 CSR 110 §22.5 (above) — Accessed 2026-10-24. Facility water supply must be sufficient, from a public system or a nonpublic system capable of providing a safe, potable, adequate supply. Wastewater/spent hydroponic solution must follow §22.5 routes (permitted sewage system, NPDES/water-quality permit discharge, or <1-gallon container to landfill). No cannabis-specific water-use limit, irrigation limit, or water-rights rule was found. Gap.

Energy. No cannabis-specific energy rule (e.g., lighting efficiency, PPE, renewable requirement) was found in searches. Do not import another state's energy rule. Gap: check 64 CSR 110 facility sections and WV building/energy codes for any applicable general requirement, or state [UNAVAILABLE] for a cannabis-specific energy rule.

11.7 Sampling, giveaways, discounts, promotions

Source: Cornell LII, 64 CSR 112 §7 — Accessed 2026-10-24. §7.1: dispensary must dispense the form of medical cannabis per §4 of the rule. §7.2: dispensary must purchase medical cannabis products only from a grower/processor. §7.3: dispensary may sell, offer for sale, or provide at its facility, with prior written Bureau approval, instruments, devices, and services related to use of medical cannabis. §7.4: dispensary may dispense a product so long as it purchases it from a grower/processor and the grower/processor obtained Bureau approval under 64CSR110-10 (Forms). §7.5: a dispensary may not: (7.5.1) advertise medical cannabis (a) as a promotional item, (b) as part of a giveaway, (c) as part of a coupon program; (7.5.2) provide medical cannabis at no cost or free; (7.5.3) make dispensing conditional upon (a) purchase of a medical device/instrument/service at the dispensary facility, or (b) purchase at a location other than a dispensary facility; (7.5.4) offer delivery of or deliver medical cannabis to a patient or caregiver at the patient's/caregiver's home or any other location.

Interpretation: the ban is on advertising medical cannabis itself as a promotional item/giveaway/coupon, and on free medical cannabis. It does not on its face ban all price discounts on medical cannabis, nor discounts on non-cannabis items/services — but any general discount/promotion rule beyond §7.5 was not retrieved. Conflict to report, not resolve: secondary marketplace evidence (Weedmaps Martinsburg deals page, Weedmaps, Martinsburg deals — Accessed 2026-10-24, listing daily deals, BOGOs, first-time discounts, promo codes, coupons, limited-time offers, 420 deals) conflicts in appearance with §7.5.1(c) if those coupons are for medical cannabis itself. Narrowing from the check: free-certification discount codes, “first purchase 50% off” and card-renewal order discounts are not on their face offers of free cannabis, so they do not evidence a §7.5 violation; keep a flag only for any listing actually offering free cannabis — none was verified. Do not present Weedmaps listings as lawful without reconciling them against §7.5 and any OMC advertising-approval policy. OMC reviewed 1,088 advertising requests in Nov 2023–Oct 2025 (OMC Biennial Report 2025, omc.wv.gov/media/95 — Accessed 2026-10-24) — advertising approval exists in practice, but the approval rule text was not opened in this pass [VERIFY]. Sampling — two meanings: no consumer sampling (free samples to patients) is authorised in §7.5; “sampling” in the CSR sense means laboratory sampling (64 CSR 111 §8). Distinguish the two. Non-cannabis discount example (secondary, not a cannabis discount): Elevate Holistics / Greenlight Give-Back Program, Elevate Holistics — Accessed 2026-10-24, describes a discount code removing the price of a first-time medical cannabis certification (physician appointment), not a discount on medical cannabis itself. Commercial, secondary; use only to illustrate the certification vs product distinction, with that caveat.

11.8 Drug testing for permit holders and staff

Background checks — confirmed. Sources: code.wvlegislature.gov/16A-6-2, §16A-6-2(a)(4); 64 CSR 109 §8 (older republication) — Accessed 2026-10-24. Application must include criminal history record check; organisations shall submit fingerprints of principals, financial backers, operators and employees to WV State Police; State Police or authorised agent shall submit to FBI for identity verification and current record of arrests/convictions; information may be interpreted/used by the Bureau only to determine character, fitness and suitability. Exception for owners of securities in a publicly traded corporation if Bureau determines owner not substantially involved. OMC Biennial Report 2025 aggregate: 817 employee background checks (Run 1); approval 96% + denial 2% = 98%, leaving 2% unexplained [VERIFY].

Drug testing for permit-holder staff — NOT found as a CSR requirement. No 64 CSR 109/110/111/112 provision requiring pre-employment, random, or post-incident drug testing of medical cannabis organisation employees was retrieved. Secondary source StateRegsToday, StateRegsToday — Accessed 2026-10-24, claims: “all employees must pass a drug test prior to employment and may be subject to random drug tests” and “employees must be at least 21” and “must not have been convicted of any felony drug offense within the last 5 years or any misdemeanor drug offense within the last 2 years.” Secondary, low-quality, NOT adopted — it conflicts with the absence of such a rule in the CSR excerpts opened and with Run 1's felony-drug bar in §16A-6-12(a) (no 5-year/2-year lookback stated in Run 1). Treat all three claims as [VERIFY]; do not state them as law. Note: 64 CSR 110 §3.2.2 (SOS PDF) does state a grower/processor may not employ an individual under 18 at its facility — that is an age floor of 18 for grower/processor employment, not 21.

General WV employer drug-testing law (secondary summaries, for context only, not a cannabis-permit rule): WV Safer Workplace Act, Chapter 21, Article 3E (secondary summaries cite §§21-3E-7 split-sample retest at employee's expense at a different lab; §21-3E-16 workers' comp forfeiture only with advance notice; §21A-6-3 refusal/failure in safety-sensitive position as gross misconduct for unemployment). Secondary source American Screening Corp, American Screening Corp — Accessed 2026-10-24, states: §16A-15-4 protects certified patient status but does not require accommodation or allow impairment, and does not bar testing; employers may enforce safety-sensitive standards and discipline documented impairment; DOT rules (49 CFR Part 40) still apply to covered employees. Secondary summaries; open Chapter 21, Article 3E itself before quoting any section, and keep general employer testing distinct from any permit-holder testing rule (none found).

Training (related, confirmed) — 64 CSR 109 §21. Source: Justia, 64 CSR 109 §21, current through June 19, 2025 — Accessed 2026-10-24. §21.1: principals (prior to starting initial operation) and employees with direct patient/caregiver contact or who physically handle medical cannabis (within 90 days after starting work) must complete a two-hour Bureau-developed training course. §21.2: course must cover Act/rules relevant to grower/processor responsibilities; proper handling; proper recordkeeping; preventing/detecting diversion; best practice security; best practice safety including medical emergency, fire, chemical spill, threatening event (armed robbery, burglary, criminal incident). §21.3: organisation must retain training attendance records and make available to Bureau/agents on request.

11.9 Research / university licences

Article 14 — Academic Clinical Research Centers — current Code confirmed. Sources: code.wvlegislature.gov/16A-14-1; code.wvlegislature.gov/16A, §16A-14-2 — Accessed 2026-10-24. §16A-14-1: (1) “Academic clinical research center” means an accredited medical school within this state that operates or partners with an acute care hospital licensed within this state. (2) “Clinical registrant” means an entity that (A) holds a permit as a grower, processor and a dispensary, and (B) has a contractual relationship with an academic clinical research center under which the center or its affiliate provides advice regarding patient health/safety, medical applications, dispensing and management of controlled substances. §16A-14-2: “Notwithstanding the limitations in section thirteen, article six of this chapter, the bureau may register up to four clinical registrants,” subject to (1) paying fees and meeting all requirements for grower, processor and dispensary permits, and (2) complying with all other Act requirements for growing, processing and dispensing. This confirms Run 1's ≤4 figure as current Code.

Contradiction R2-C-13 — bill vs Code: 2020 bill text (Eng. CS for SB 752, SB 752 engrossed text — Accessed 2026-10-24) proposed changing “up to four” to “up to eight” clinical registrants and broadening the academic center definition to “accredited college, university, or medical school.” That is BILL TEXT, not current Code. Do not state 8 as the current cap. The bill text also proposed a research-study section §16A-14-3; current §16A-14-3 text was not opened in full — [VERIFY] current §16A-14-3.

Article 13 — Health-care medical cannabis organisations / university research — existence confirmed, operating status NOT confirmed. Sources: §16A-15-4(a)(5), (8) and SB 231/SB 752 bill texts (cross-references); §16A-13-6, §16A-13-8 via bill text — Accessed 2026-10-24. Protected persons include “A health care medical cannabis organization or an accredited college, university, or medical school participating in a research study under §16A-13-1 et seq.” and employees thereof. §16A-13-6 (via bill text): a health care medical cannabis organization that grows/processes shall maintain Bureau licensure; only make medical cannabis available to health care organizations that dispense; keep a daily log of medical cannabis intended for ultimate use by patients in a research study. §16A-13-8 (Nonentitlement): nothing in the chapter creates an entitlement/right to receive medical cannabis or participate in a research study. §16A-13 tracking includes plant waste tracking and testing by an independent laboratory including test at harvest and test at final processing, plus any other Bureau procedure. No operating Article 13 programme, no named WVU or Marshall clinical registrant, and no named WVU/Marshall research study was found. Do not name WVU or Marshall as operating clinical registrants or research centres without an OMC/university source. Gap: search OMC clinical registrant list, WVU School of Medicine / Marshall Joan C. Edwards School of Medicine research pages, and OMC news for any Article 13/14 registration. If none is found, state [UNAVAILABLE]/none confirmed, not “none exist” as a proven negative beyond the search. Practical barrier (analysis, labelled as such, not a separate rule): because a clinical registrant must hold all three §16A-6 permits and grower/processor/dispensary applications closed 18 Feb 2020 (secondary timelines only — see §9.2), a new clinical registrant cannot be created through a new permit window on current OMC practice; it would require an existing permit holder to contract with an academic center. This follows from the permit-window closure (secondary timelines, §9.2) and the three-permit requirement.

Part 12 — Consumer and Social Rules

12.1 Paraphernalia and smoke shops — CORRECTION: §60A-4-403a is repealed; operative law is Chapter 47, Article 19

New direct opening — §60A-4-403a is repealed. Source: code.wvlegislature.gov/60A-4-403a — Accessed 2026-10-24. Current WV Code page reads, in full substance: “§60A-4-403a. Prohibition of illegal drug paraphernalia businesses… [Repealed.]” Repealing act: Enr. CS for SB 269 (2024), passed 26 January 2024, in effect from passage, “to amend and reenact §47-19-3…; and to repeal §60A-4-403a… relating to excluding test strips from the definition of drug paraphernalia; and specifying that possession, sale, or purchase of drug test strips is not prohibited.” Source: SB 269 signed bill (2024) — Accessed 2026-10-24. Narrowing: the enacted exclusion is for fentanyl test strips specifically (§47-19-3(a)(4) proviso) — do not state the exclusion generically for all test strips.

Therefore the older §60A-4-403a “illegal drug paraphernalia business” offence (misdemeanor, up to $5,000 / 6 months–1 year, “drug device” list including pipes, bongs, chillums, etc., as reproduced in Justia 2022) must NOT be presented as current law. It is historical text. Any prior summary quoting it as current must be corrected to the Chapter 47, Article 19 regime below. This corrects the secondary 2022 republication, not a Run 1 baseline fact (Run 1 did not decide paraphernalia). (Contradiction R2-C-11.)

Operative paraphernalia law — Chapter 47, Article 19 — newly opened in full for §§47-19-1 to 47-19-4. Sources: code.wvlegislature.gov/47-19; §47-19-1; §47-19-2; §47-19-4 — Accessed 2026-10-24.

  • §47-19-1 (Licence required): “It shall be unlawful for any person or persons as principal, clerk, agent or servant to sell any items, effect, paraphernalia, accessory or thing which is designed or marketed for use with controlled substances, as defined in chapter sixty-a of this code, without obtaining a license therefor from the State Tax Commissioner. Such licenses shall be in addition to any or all other licenses held by applicant. The fee for such license shall be $150.”
  • §47-19-2 (Application): application to sell any such item shall be accompanied by affidavits by applicant and each and every employee authorised to sell such items that such person has never been convicted of a drug-related offense.
  • §47-19-3 (Definition): items, if marketed for use or designed for use with controlled substances, are drug paraphernalia for §47-19-1 et seq., including: kits for planting/propagating/cultivating/growing/harvesting a controlled-substance plant; kits for manufacturing/compounding/converting/producing/processing/preparing controlled substances; isomerisation devices; testing equipment for identifying/analysing strength/effectiveness/purity — Provided, That test strips are not considered drug paraphernalia; scales/balances; diluents/adulterants; separation gins/sifters for cleaning/refining marijuana; blenders/bowls/containers/spoons/mixing devices for compounding; capsules/balloons/envelopes/containers for packaging small quantities; hypodermic syringes/needles/objects for parenteral injection; paper of colorful design with names oriented for use with controlled dangerous substances and displayed (white paper or tobacco-oriented paper not necessarily designed for use with controlled substances is not covered); pipes displayed in proximity of roach clips, or literature encouraging illegal use (pipes otherwise displayed are not covered); roach clips; miniature cocaine spoons and cocaine vials; chillums or bongs.
  • §47-19-3(b) factors for whether an object is marketed/designed as paraphernalia (State Tax Commissioner or other authority should consider): proximity in time/space to a controlled substance; residue; instructions/descriptive materials; national/local advertising; manner of display for sale; whether owner/controller is a legitimate supplier of like/related items (e.g., licensed tobacco distributor/dealer); ratio of object sales to total business sales; existence/scope of legitimate uses in the community.
  • §47-19-4 (Records): every licensee must keep a record of every item/effect/paraphernalia/accessory/thing designed/marketed for use with controlled substances which is sold, open to inspection of any police officer at any time during business hours. Record shall contain name and address of purchaser, name and quantity of product, date and time of sale, and licensee/agent signature. Records retained not less than two years.

Penalties under Chapter 47, Article 19 beyond §§47-19-1 to 47-19-4 were not opened in this pass — [VERIFY] §§47-19-5 et seq. before stating a penalty for unlicensed paraphernalia sale.

Medical-cannabis device distinction — must be reconciled, not assumed. Sources: §16A-8-2(a)(4) (per Run 1); Cornell LII, 64 CSR 112 §7 (§7.3, above); 64 CSR 110 §10.2 (per Run 1) — Accessed 2026-10-24. Dispensary may sell, under Bureau conditions/limitations, medical devices/instruments needed to administer medical cannabis (§16A-8-2(a)(4)); dispensary may sell/offer/provide at its facility, with prior written Bureau approval, instruments, devices, and services related to use of medical cannabis (64 CSR 112 §7.3); grower/processor may not manufacture/produce/assemble any medical cannabis product, instrument, or device without prior written Bureau approval (64 CSR 110 §10.2). No WV source retrieved expressly states that §16A-8-2/64 CSR 112 §7.3 creates an exemption from Chapter 47, Article 19 for non-dispensary smoke shops. Do not state such an exemption without a source. The safer formulation: a device sold by a dispensary with Bureau approval for administering lawful medical cannabis is on a different footing from a general paraphernalia sale, but the Chapter 47 licence/record regime is the general smoke-shop regime on current Code. Smoke-shop zoning / statewide licence: no statewide smoke-shop licence, age, or buffer rule for hemp/CBD smoke shops beyond Chapter 47, Article 19 was found. Morgantown ordinance material (16 July 2024 agenda, Morgantown agenda 16 July 2024 — Accessed 2026-10-24) creates a separate smoke-shop/tobacco-store use classification and states “‘Drug store’ shall not include or be construed to be a medical cannabis dispensary.” Gap: check WV tobacco retailer law (Chapter 16, Article 9A) and local smoke-shop ordinances for any cannabis-specific application, or state [UNAVAILABLE].

12.2 Driving: THC limits and impairment testing

Patient-specific rule — carried forward, confirmed. Source: Justia, §16A-5-10 — Accessed 2026-10-24. (1) Patient may not operate/be in physical control of chemicals requiring a permit, high-voltage electricity/public utility, or vehicle/aircraft/train/boat/heavy machinery while under the influence with blood content of more than 3 ng active THC per mL of blood in serum. (2) Patient may not perform employment duties at heights or in confined spaces including mining while under the influence. (3)–(4) Employer may prohibit life-threatening or public-health/safety-risk tasks while under the influence; prohibition is not an adverse employment decision even if it results in financial harm. Wording note: NORML's summary (“3 ng/mL or more”) differs from the statute's “more than” 3 ng. The statute controls. Source for NORML summary: NORML, WV drugged driving — Accessed 2026-10-24 (secondary).

General DUI law — newly opened in full for §17C-5-2(a)–(j). Source: code.wvlegislature.gov/17C-5, §17C-5-2 — Accessed 2026-10-24.

  • §17C-5-2(a)(1) “Impaired state” means: (A) under influence of alcohol; (B) under influence of any controlled substance; (C) under influence of any other drug or inhalant substance; (D) combined influence of alcohol and any controlled substance/other drug; or (E) alcohol concentration ≥0.08% by weight. There is no general THC concentration tier in §17C-5-2(a) — general drug DUI is impairment-based, unlike alcohol's 0.08% / 0.15% tiers. The only THC number in WV law located is the patient-specific >3 ng/mL serum rule in §16A-5-10.
  • §17C-5-2(e) — basic impaired driving (no injury): misdemeanor, up to 6 months jail, $100–$500 fine, licence revocation 6 months or Test and Lock period per §17C-5A-3a. The (e)(2) sub-tier (“in an impaired state but has an alcohol concentration… less than 0.15%”) is an alcohol-concentration formulation, not a THC tier.
  • §17C-5-2(f) — alcohol ≥0.15%: misdemeanor, 2 days–6 months jail (≥24 hours actual confinement), $200–$1,000 fine, revocation 1 year or Test and Lock. Alcohol tier only; do not apply to THC.
  • §17C-5-2(d) — impaired driving proximately causing bodily injury (other than driver): misdemeanor, 1 day–1 year jail (≥24 hours actual confinement), $200–$1,000 fine, revocation 2 years or Test and Lock; prior conviction → revocation for life or Test and Lock.
  • §17C-5-2(c) — proximately causing serious bodily injury: felony, 2–10 years, $1,000–$3,000 fine, revocation 5 years or Test and Lock; prior conviction → life or Test and Lock.
  • §17C-5-2(b)(2) — proximately causing death (within one year): felony, 3–15 years, $1,000–$3,000 fine, revocation 10 years or Test and Lock; prior conviction → life or Test and Lock.
  • §17C-5-2(b)(1) — driving in an impaired state with deliberate disregard for safety of others (as defined in §17C-5-1) proximately causing death (within one year): felony, 5–30 years, $2,000–$10,000 fine, licence revoked for life; mandatory sentence, not eligible for suspension/home incarceration as stated. Amendments enacted during the 2026 regular session are known as “Baylea's Law.”
  • §17C-5-2(g) — habitual user of narcotic drugs or amphetamines (or derivative) driving: misdemeanor, 1 day–6 months jail (≥24 hours actual), $100–$500 fine, revocation 6 months. Note: this is a narcotic/amphetamine habitual-user offence, not a general “habitual cannabis user” offence on its face.
  • §17C-5-2(h)–(i) — knowingly permitting a vehicle to be driven by a person in an impaired state, or by a habitual narcotic/amphetamine user: misdemeanor, up to 6 months, $100–$500, revocation 6 months (or Test and Lock for (h)).

Secondary penalty summaries NOT adopted: NORML's “first offence … 6 months (90 days mandatory)” and WestVirginiaStateCannabis.org's “jail 6 months to 10 years, fines $100 to $5,000” and under-21 “2 days to 1 year, $200 to $1,000” conflate tiers and are superseded by the §17C-5-2 text above for the tiers opened. Under-21 alcohol tier §17C-5-2(j) was only partially opened (first offence $25–$100, 60-day suspension or Test and Lock, as truncated) — full (j) [VERIFY] before quoting under-21 penalties. Implied consent / blood testing — NOT settled in this pass: NORML (secondary, above) states implied consent in WV requires a person suspected of DUI alcohol to submit to breath/blood/urine, but does NOT require a person suspected of DUI marijuana/controlled substance to submit to chemical testing for drugs, and no penalties/sanctions apply for refusal for drugs. Do NOT state this as law without opening §17C-5-4 (implied consent) and §17C-5-6 (blood test administration; additional test at option of person tested). The §17C-5 page also contains an officer-training gate (officer who has not completed minimum training in recognition of impairment by controlled substances/drugs other than alcohol may not require a secondary chemical blood test for a controlled substance/drug) and §17C-5-5 preliminary breath analysis (State Police Forensic Laboratory-approved device, results used solely to guide arrest decision) — full subsection context [VERIFY]. Impairment testing methods: no WV roadside THC test, oral-fluid test, or cannabis-specific Drug Recognition Expert programme was confirmed. Blood testing is the method implied by §16A-5-10 (serum) and §17C-5-6 (blood test), but do not claim a specific roadside cannabis test exists without a State Police / §17C-5 source. Gap. Commercial drivers / DOT: DOT testing under 49 CFR Part 40 still applies to covered employees (secondary summaries, §11.8 above). No WV-specific CDL cannabis rule was opened — [VERIFY] against WV CDL law if needed, or state as federal overlay only.

12.3 Expungement and sealing

Conditional discharge — §60A-4-407 — confirmed. Source: code.wvlegislature.gov/60A-4-407 (PDF) — Accessed 2026-10-24. (a) Person who has not previously been convicted of any offence under Chapter 60A or under any US/state statute relating to narcotic drugs, marihuana, or stimulant, depressant, or hallucinogenic drugs, who pleads guilty to or is found guilty of possession under §60A-4-401(c), the court, without entering judgment of guilt and with consent of the accused, may defer proceedings and place the person on probation on terms/conditions. On violation, court may enter adjudication of guilt and proceed otherwise. On fulfillment, court shall discharge and dismiss proceedings. Discharge/dismissal is without adjudication of guilt and is not a conviction for purposes of this section or for disqualifications/disabilities imposed by law on conviction, including additional penalties for second/subsequent convictions under §60A-4-408. Effect is to restore the person in contemplation of law to the status occupied prior to arrest/trial. Person so discharged/dismissed shall not thereafter be held guilty of perjury/false swearing/false statement by reason of failure to disclose/acknowledge arrest/trial in response to any inquiry for any purpose. Only one discharge/dismissal under this section per person. (b) After not less than six months beginning immediately upon expiration of probation, the person may apply to the court for an order to expunge from all official records all recordations of arrest, trial, and conviction under this section. If the court determines after a hearing that the person during probation and prior to application has not been guilty of any serious or repeated violation of probation conditions, it shall order expungement. (c) Person whose case is disposed under this section is liable for court costs assessable against a person convicted under §60A-4-401(c); payment may be made a condition of probation. Link to Run 1: §60A-4-401(c) proviso requires first offence for <15 g marijuana to be disposed under §60A-4-407.
