Municipal analogue: South Charleston Code §511.01 (via AmLegal, South Charleston Code §511.01 — Accessed 2026-10-24) mirrors §60A-4-407 for ≤15 g first offence in the city, with 30 days probation, discharge/dismissal, and expungement application after ≥6 months — municipal example only, not statewide law. §60A-4-407a, code.wvlegislature.gov/60A-4-407A — Accessed 2026-10-24: when a person pleads/found guilty under §60A-4-401(c) or a municipal ordinance with same elements where the substance is listed in §60A-2-204 other than marijuana, or listed in §§60A-2-206, 2-208, 2-210, the court may as an additional condition for a final discharge/dismissal order under §60A-4-407 require evaluation for drug court or participation in drug treatment; if appropriate candidate, successful completion may be required. Marijuana is expressly excluded from this additional-requirement trigger.

General expungement — §61-11-25 — newly opened in full. Source: code.wvlegislature.gov/61-11-25 — Accessed 2026-10-24. (a) Any person charged with a criminal offense under WV law who has been found not guilty, or against whom charges have been dismissed, and not in exchange for a guilty plea to another offense resulting in a conviction, may file a civil petition in the circuit court in which charges were filed to expunge all records relating to arrest, charge, or other matters arising out of arrest/charge. Any person whose charges have been dismissed following full and successful completion of pretrial diversion under §61-11-22, or deferred adjudication under §61-11-22a, may file a civil petition for expungement of all charges originally brought, provided charges arose from same transaction/occurrence. Proviso: no Division of Motor Vehicles record may be expunged by virtue of an order under §17C-5-2b, nor may charges ultimately dismissed by full/successful deferred adjudication be expunged for §§61-2-28(a)/(b), 61-2-9(a), 61-2-9a, 61-2-9(b)/(c) where alleged victim is a family/household member as defined in §48-27-204; any person previously convicted of a felony may not file a petition under this section. (b) Petition filed not sooner than 60 days following order of acquittal/dismissal; court entering acquittal/dismissal shall inform person of rights to file under this section. (c)–(d) Court may set hearing, notify prosecuting attorney and arresting agency; if court finds no current charges/proceedings pending relating to matter, court may grant petition and order sealing of all records in court custody and expungement of records in custody of any other agency/official including law enforcement; every agency ordered to expunge shall certify to court within 60 days that expungement completed; all orders enforcing expungement procedure shall also be sealed. (e) Upon expungement, proceedings shall be considered never to have occurred; court/agencies shall reply to any inquiry that no record exists; person shall not have to disclose fact of record on application for employment, credit, or other application. (f) Inspection of sealed records thereafter only on motion by subject or petition by prosecuting attorney that inspection/use is necessary to investigation/prosecution of a crime, if court finds interests of justice served. (g) No filing fees or costs for filing under this section. Secondary guide (Collateral Consequences Resource Center, WV profile, CCRC, West Virginia profile — Accessed 2026-10-24): in 2024, HB 4399 amended §61-11-25(a) to make clear that when charges are dismissed under §61-11-22 or §61-11-22a, expungement is available under §61-11-25 (with generally applicable exceptions for domestic violence and DUIs, and those with a prior felony conviction). Prosecutor pretrial diversion authorised since 2001, codified at §61-11-22; judicial pretrial diversion of first-time drug possessors = §60A-4-407(a); after at least six months following probation completion, person may apply to have case expunged, §60A-4-407(b). Secondary; §61-11-26 (expungement of criminal records — convictions) was not opened in full — [VERIFY] before stating any conviction-expungement eligibility, waiting period, or offence list.

No cannabis-specific expungement statute, no automatic expungement, and no WV Second Chance Act cannabis provision was confirmed. Run 1's DC research noted Second Chance Act date conflicts, but that is DC law, NOT WV law — do not import it. HJR 37 (proposal, NOT law): 2026 constitutional amendment proposal (possession ≤2 oz / 4 plants, Legislature may regulate manufacture/sale, prior possession ≤2 oz convictions subject to dismissal/expungement by petition to circuit court of conviction) — introduced 5 Feb 2026, referred to House Judiciary then Rules, LegiScan status “Introduced — Dead” (secondary tracker, LegiScan, HJR 37 — Accessed 2026-10-24). Final wvlegislature.gov status still [VERIFY]. Must be presented ONLY as a proposal, never as an expungement right. Similarly, HB 4371 (adult-use by county referendum) and SB 100 (2026 decriminalisation ≤15 g civil offence ≤$25) are proposals, not law (Run 1). Charleston local expungement (secondary, Run 1): Charleston Bill No. 8039 (2024, citizens-initiated, NORML summary 20 Aug 2024) — as amended, removes fines/jail threat for first-time low-level (≤15 g) possession; prior-conviction fines $250–$500; eligible convictions dismissed after payment/terms, others expungeable within six months. Secondary only; Charleston ordinance text not opened; Bill No. 8043 relationship [UNAVAILABLE]. Do not present as statewide expungement law.

12.4 Consumption lounges and events — none authorised

No consumption lounge licence, permit type, or authorisation was found in Chapter 16A, 64 CSR 109–112 excerpts, or searches. Chapter 16A authorises only growers, processors, dispensaries (§16A-6-1) plus laboratories (testing) and clinical registrants (Article 14 overlay). No lounge, café, event, or on-site consumption permit class exists on the evidence retrieved. Supporting negatives (Run 1 / Part 11 searches): smoking is unlawful even for patients (§16A-3-3(b)(1)); vaporising is the lawful inhalation route, but no public-consumption permission for patients was located in §§16A-3-2/3-3, 16A-5-10 or 16A-8. Dispensary must dispense only in an indoor, enclosed, secure facility (§16A-8-2(a)(1)); dispensary may NOT offer/deliver to a home or any other location (64 CSR 112 §7.5.4) and may NOT provide medical cannabis free or as a giveaway/coupon/promotional item (64 CSR 112 §7.5.1–7.5.2) — these rules point against on-site consumption events, but they are not a lounge ban per se. No recurring WV cannabis trade show/expo with verified WV venue/date was found for 2025–2026 in Run 1 (OMC events page shows no events in text fetch; historic OMC events were patient sign-up events, not trade shows; Morgantown, PA Canna Fair 7–8 Nov 2026 is Pennsylvania, NOT Morgantown, WV — same-name trap, must be excluded). Gap: check for any local (municipal) consumption-lounge ordinance, any OMC advisory on events, and any §16A-3-3 / 64 CSR 112 on-site consumption prohibition in full text before stating “consumption lounges are prohibited” vs “no lounge licence exists and no lounge was located.” The safer formulation on current evidence is the latter, plus the smoking ban.

12.5 Visitor / out-of-state patient rules — narrow provision confirmed

Source: code.wvlegislature.gov/16A-3, §16A-3-5 — Accessed 2026-10-24. “The bureau may enter into reciprocity agreements with any states that have comparable requirements for the use and lawful purchase of medical cannabis in a manner consistent with the provisions of this article to allow terminally ill cancer patients to purchase medical cannabis in another state.” This is the ONLY reciprocity provision in current Article 3 text fetched in Run 1. It is discretionary (“may”), limited on its face to terminally ill cancer patients, and framed as purchase in another state, not purchase in WV by out-of-state patients. No list of executed reciprocity agreements and no general out-of-state-patient purchase right was located [UNAVAILABLE]. Secondary clinic-marketing claims that a WV card “can be used in Pennsylvania and Washington D.C.” are NOT adopted. An older bill (SB 752) proposed broader reciprocity (possess/transport/use/transfer without remuneration in approved states); that is bill text, not law. Practical consequence (stated carefully): a visitor who is not a WV cardholder has no WV medical purchase right on current law; a visitor who is a WV cardholder must still present a valid WV ID card and comply with §16A-8-1 dispensing limits (30-day supply, practitioner form limitation, verification via Bureau/dispensary tracking systems). A non-patient visitor's possession remains a Chapter 60A offence (Run 1). Gap: check OMC visitor/out-of-state patient page and any executed §16A-3-5 agreement list; if none, state [UNAVAILABLE].

12.6 Landlord / tenant rules — no cannabis-specific statute found

No WV landlord-tenant statute specific to medical cannabis was located. Chapter 37 (Real Property) and Chapter 55, Article 3A contain general landlord-tenant and recovery-residence rules, but no medical-cannabis tenant protection or eviction rule was retrieved. Context only, not WV cannabis law: Wheeling Housing Authority Public Housing Non-Smoking Lease Addendum, Wheeling Housing Authority, Smoke-Free Lease Addendum (activation 7/1/2017) — Accessed 2026-10-24: tenant must promptly give written statement of any incident where tobacco or marijuana smoke, or vapor from an electronic cigarette, is migrating into the unit from outside; landlord posts no-smoking signs; other tenants are third-party beneficiaries and may sue for injunction/damages but not evict; material breach of the addendum is material breach of the lease and grounds for enforcement including eviction, plus liability for smoke odor/residue repair costs. Public-housing lease addendum only, not statewide statute; do not generalise. Recovery residence rule (Chapter 37 bill text, SB 147 House Judiciary amendment (2023) — Accessed 2026-10-24): a recovery residence may immediately discharge a resident without a summary-relief petition for, among other reasons, use/possession/distribution of alcohol, any controlled substance, or prescription medication for which the resident does not have a valid prescription. Recovery-residence rule only, not general tenancy; whether a medical card counts as a “prescription” for this purpose is [VERIFY] — do not assume. General legal commentary (JustAnswer, secondary, NOT law): landlords could prohibit on-premises smoking before medical laws and still can after; fair-housing disability-accommodation arguments for smoking typically fail; many leases prohibit “illegal activity,” and because medical cannabis remains illegal federally, smoking could violate such a clause. Secondary commentary only. Gap: open WV Code Chapter 37, Article 6A (Residential Rental) and any WV Housing Development Fund / public housing rule on medical cannabis; check for any WV Attorney General or court decision on medical cannabis tenancy. If none, state: no WV cannabis-specific landlord/tenant statute located; tenancy is governed by the lease, general landlord-tenant law, and (for public/federally assisted housing) federal/housing-authority rules — with the smoking ban (§16A-3-3(b)(1)) and §16A-15-4 employment/licensure protections NOT extending to tenancy on the text opened.

12.7 Gun ownership — federal conflict + WV state law newly opened: NO medical exception

Federal baseline (commission context, NOT WV law): 18 U.S.C. §922(g)(3) prohibits firearm possession by an unlawful user of / addicted to a controlled substance. Federal-track notes (do not import as WV law unless a WV source says so): US v Hemani, No. 24-1234 (decided 18 June 2026, Gorsuch) held a status-only §922(g)(3) prosecution of a regular marijuana user for in-home possession, with no danger showing, inconsistent with the Second Amendment (as-applied, narrow); ATF interim final rule eff. 22 Jan 2026 rewrote 27 CFR 478.11 to require regular use continuing into the present; Schedule III alone does not remove medical users from §922(g)(3) text and no ATF safe harbour was located [VERIFY] in federal track. WV's medical programme is state-licensed, so Schedule III (91 FR 22714, eff. 28 Apr 2026, for state-licensed medical marijuana and FDA-approved products; adult-use remains Schedule I) affects 280E/tax treatment, not the §922(g)(3) text on its own.

WV state law — §61-7-7 — newly opened in full in this pass. Source: code.wvlegislature.gov/61-7-7 — Accessed 2026-10-24. §61-7-7(a)(3): no person shall possess a firearm who “Is an unlawful user of or habitually addicted to any controlled substance.” No medical-cannabis cardholder exception appears in §61-7-7(a) as opened. §61-7-7(a) penalty: misdemeanor, fine not less than $100 nor more than $1,000, or confined in county jail not less than 90 days nor more than one year, or both. §61-7-7(c)(4): a person may carry a concealed deadly weapon without a licence only if, among other conditions, “Not prohibited from possessing a firearm under the provisions of 18 U. S. C. §922(g) or (n).” WV concealed-carry eligibility therefore expressly incorporates the federal §922(g) bar. §61-7-7(f): a person prohibited under subsection (a) may petition the circuit court of the county of residence to regain ability to possess a firearm if the court finds by clear and convincing evidence the person is competent/capable, and the court may enter an order allowing possession “if such possession would not violate any federal law.” State reinstatement therefore cannot cure a federal §922(g)(3) bar. §61-7-7(b)(2) (felony tier, for context, not a patient rule): felony controlled substance offense involving a Schedule I controlled substance other than marijuana, a Schedule II or Schedule III substance, plus firearm possession, is a felony up to five years / $5,000. This tier expressly carves marijuana out of the Schedule I predicate — do not misread it as a patient protection. §61-7-7(g): person whose disqualifying conviction was expunged/set aside or who receives an unconditional pardon shall not be prohibited by virtue of that conviction.

WV proposals (NOT law, must be distinguished): HB 4150 (2026, introduced 14 January 2026 by Del. Horst, House Judiciary, official status Pending, last action 01/14/26; LegiScan LegiScan, HB 4150; FastDemocracy summary FastDemocracy — Accessed 2026-10-24): would amend §61-7-7 to provide that a person who is an unlawful user/habitually addicted, “with the exception of lawful medical cannabis card holders, unless the current or aftereffects of such consumption affects mental, emotional or physical processes to a degree that would result in abnormal behavior, a medical marijuana card holder or caregiver shall not be denied the right to own, purchase or possess a firearm, ammunition, or firearm accessories based solely on his or her status as a medical cannabis card holder or caregiver,” and that no state/local agency or municipal/county authority shall restrict/revoke/suspend/infringe firearm rights based solely on medical card status. INTRODUCED text only; final wvlegislature.gov status [VERIFY]; it is NOT current law. Its existence implies the exception does not currently exist — but that inference is confirmed by the current §61-7-7 text above, which contains no such exception. HB 5488 (2024, Introduced, LegiScan LegiScan, HB 5488 — Accessed 2026-10-24): would have required card application/renewal forms to include a separate-page, separately signed notice stating: (i) Gun Control Act of 1968, 18 U.S.C. §922 prohibits any person who is an unlawful user/addicted to any controlled substance from shipping/transporting/receiving/possessing a firearm or ammunition; (ii) until marijuana is legalized federally, a current marijuana user is under federal law an unlawful user; (iii) federal law does not exempt medicinal use. Proposed notice requirement, not current law; its presence in a bill does not prove current OMC forms contain that notice — [VERIFY] OMC application forms. Secondary position NOT adopted as law: WestVirginiaStateCannabis.org, WestVirginiaStateCannabis.org, gun ownership — Accessed 2026-10-24, states “No. West Virginia complies with the federal law that prohibits medical marijuana users from possessing firearms” and “There are no West Virginia laws protecting MMJ cardholders…” The conclusion (no WV protection) is now consistent with the opened §61-7-7 text, but the site's “Class 6 felony” / Schedule I-or-II framing conflates federal and state classifications and is NOT quoted as WV law. Its claims that spouses of cardholders who are not unlawful users may own firearms, and patients may obtain firearm permits after cards expire, are secondary — [VERIFY]. Safe formulation on current evidence: federally, a regular medical cannabis user faces §922(g)(3) risk, narrowed but not eliminated by Schedule III and Hemani (federal track, [VERIFY] for post-April 2026 scope); in WV state law, no patient firearm protection was located, and a 2026 bill to create one (HB 4150) is only introduced. Patients should be advised to seek legal advice — this is a risk note, not legal advice.

12.8 Pet / veterinary products — no WV-specific rule found

No WV statute, WVDA rule, or Board of Veterinary Medicine rule specific to hemp/CBD pet products, veterinary cannabis, or veterinary recommendation of cannabis was located. What was found: secondary commercial/consumer sources state hemp CBD pet products (dog treats, oils, chews) are widely available in WV under the same hemp framework as human products, and pet owners should consult a veterinarian (New Phase Blends, New Phase Blends; Weedmaps, Weedmaps — Accessed 2026-10-24). Weedmaps summarises HB 2694 (April 2019) as clarifying: hemp ≤0.3% THC is an agricultural commodity; no licence required to possess/handle/transport/sell hemp-derived cannabinoids such as CBD; CBD from hemp is not a controlled substance or adulterant; CBD products intended for consumption are to be considered foods, not controlled substances. Secondary summaries of Chapter 19, Article 12E; Code text controls. WVDA Registered Products list (Run 1 link, agriculture.wv.gov/hemp-registered-products — version, row count and date not re-verified in this audit [VERIFY]) mixes product types including pet chews, but does not break out pet products, and no pet-specific registration category was identified. Gap: count/identify pet products on the WVDA list, or state that WVDA does not publish a pet-specific breakdown. Veterinary practice law (general, not cannabis-specific): §30-10-1, code.wvlegislature.gov/30-10-1 — Accessed 2026-10-24 — unlawful to practice/offer veterinary medicine, veterinary technology or animal euthanasia without a licence/registration/certificate, or to advertise/use a title conveying veterinarian/veterinary technician status without being duly licensed/registered/certified; business entity may not render veterinary services except through a licensee/registrant/certificate holder. Practice includes diagnosing/treating/correcting/changing/relieving/preventing disease/deformity/defect/injury/condition of any animal, or prescribing/administering any drug/medicine/biologic/apparatus/application/anesthetic/therapeutic/diagnostic substance/technique, or rendering advice/recommendation on the foregoing (§30-10-3, via LawServer summary, LawServer, §30-10-1 — Accessed 2026-10-24). No provision on cannabis/CBD recommendation by veterinarians was found in §30-10-1/30-10-3 excerpts. Medical cannabis for animals: Chapter 16A authorises use only for certified human patients/caregivers; no animal-patient, veterinary certification, or pet medical cannabis provision was located. State explicitly that no WV veterinary medical cannabis programme was located, and that hemp pet products fall (if at all) under the general hemp framework in §19-12E-3/§19-12E-12, with THC-content limits applying as for human products — pet-specific application [VERIFY] against WVDA guidance.

12.9 Medical cannabis in schools

Statute — §§16A-15-5, 16A-15-6. Source: code.wvlegislature.gov/16A-15 — Accessed 2026-10-24. §16A-15-5 (Schools): “The Department of Education shall promulgate rules within six months of the effective date of this section regarding the following: (1) Possession and use of medical cannabis by a student on the grounds of a preschool, primary school and a secondary school. (2) Possession and use of medical cannabis by an employee of a preschool, primary school and a secondary school on the grounds of such school.” §16A-15-6 (Daycare centers): Bureau shall promulgate rules within six months regarding (1) possession/use by a child under care of a child-care/social service center licensed/operated by the Bureau of Family Assistance (now Department of Health / Bureau for Family Assistance — name [VERIFY] after reorganisation); (2) possession/use by an employee of such a center; (3) possession/use by employees of a youth development center or other facility housing children adjudicated delinquent.

DOE rule — 126 CSR 25A §14 — county-policy corroboration, rule text itself not yet opened directly. Sources: Americans for Safe Access, ASA, pediatric access in schools — Accessed 2026-10-24 (secondary summary); county board policy document citing 126-25A-14.13, County board policy (5il.co) — Accessed 2026-10-24; WestVirginiaMarijuanaCard blog, WV Marijuana Card blog — Accessed 2026-10-24 (secondary, last updated ~1093 days ago in search index). ASA summary: WV Department of Education Legislative Rule 126-25A-14 as required by WV Code Chapter 16A — must be administered by a designated caregiver, not a school employee, nor the minor patient themselves; cannabis may not be stored on school grounds but can be administered there; allows a county board that can reasonably demonstrate it will lose or has lost federal funding to seek a waiver from the WVBE (not a unilateral county opt-out). County policy citing 126 WV Code of State Rules 126-25A-14.13: “The use of medical cannabis on school property or at a school-related event by students is regulated by 126-25A-14.13. A parent of any student who has been issued a medical cannabis identification card by the West Virginia Department of Health [and Human Resources, Bureau for Public Health — pre-reorganisation name in the document], and who has been issued a physician certification to use medical cannabis as part of the treatment of a serious medical condition, shall notify the school principal before medical cannabis may be administered on or in school property or at a school-related event. Such notification shall be on a form that shall include the name of the student and the name and contact information for the student's caregiver. In addition… a parent shall provide: A. the student's identification card…; B. the caregiver's identification card…; and C. a certification issued by a physician who is registered… that authorizes the use of medical cannabis by the student and that meets the requirements of WV Code 16A-4-3 and 16A-4-4. The notification and documentation… shall be updated at least annually by the student's parent.” Blog corroboration (NOT law, consistent with ASA/county policy): Ohio County Board of Education briefed on a new state policy mirroring prescription-drug administration guidelines but more restrictive; student must have a prescription/certification from a qualified doctor/physician; school nurse is explicitly prohibited from administering medical marijuana; responsibility falls on the state-approved caregiver, typically parent/legal guardian, who must physically visit the school, present the prescription, dispense to the student, and leave with remaining product.

Conflict to flag, not resolve, until Rule 126 CSR 25A §14 itself is opened: DocMJ blog, DocMJ — Accessed 2026-10-24, adds: schools do not permit possession on school property, administration by a school nurse, or (in older practice) parents administering on school grounds without a policy; proposed changes would allow school RNs to assist with dosing on school property during school hours, including reviewing prescription/dose/potency and speaking to the physician — this is a PROPOSAL/discussion, not current rule, and conflicts with the ASA “caregiver only, not school employee” summary.

126 CSR 25A §14 details (Cornell republication; SOS/DOE original still not opened — re-pin before relying): §14.7 a designated caregiver may administer on school property/at school events per physician certification; §14.8 medical cannabis shall not be stored on school property and shall not be administered by school personnel unless the student is the child or in the legal custody of a school employee who is the student's designated caregiver; §14.9 no dry leaf/plant form; §14.13 a student, even if 18 or older or emancipated, may not possess or self-administer — only a designated caregiver may; §14.14 the caregiver must remove any remainder.

Gap — highest priority for Part 12.9: open WV DOE Legislative Rule 126 CSR 25A, §14 from the Secretary of State / WVDE site, and any county board waiver/opt-out list. Until then, do NOT state the school rule as settled law beyond the statute (§16A-15-5) plus the county-policy citation above; state the DOE rule summary as secondary [VERIFY] (gap G-12.9). Related school-adjacent rules (already confirmed, for cross-reference): dispensary 1,000-foot buffer from school/daycare property line (§16A-8-2(a)(3)). Minor patients: 18 active minor patients at 31 Oct 2025, 18 caregivers for minors (OMC Biennial Report 2025, Run 1). Caregiver limits: ≤5 patients per caregiver, ≤2 caregivers per patient at one time (§16A-3-2(a)(4)–(5)). Smoking ban applies to all patients including minors (§16A-3-3(b)(1)); no school-specific smoking exception was located.

Warning on 2026 bill texts seen during school searches — NOT law: introduced 2026 bills HB 5188, SB 892, HB 5260 contain texts that would add an “Edible” form (§16A-3-2(2)(H)) and restate dry-leaf restrictions. Those are introduced bill texts only. They do not change the current Run 1 baseline (dispensary edibles banned, §16A-3-3(b)(2), with patient/caregiver home incorporation excepted, §16A-3-3(c)). Do not present edible-form authorisation as current law.
